In 2017 results were not achieved. Amazon annual deforestation was estimated at 358,960 ha (GEAM, SMBYC*). These figures imply an increment of 23,893.4 tCO2eq in the gross emissions, above the assessed FREL.

By 2018 deforestation in the biome was 138,176 ha, implying a reduction of 5,000 tCO2eq compared to 2017. However, the results will be assessed against the recently national FREL (2019) and the new reference level defined by UIC, which is currently under the Technical Assessment process.

As mentioned in the FREL, the implementation of the peace agreements between the government and FARC was an important factor that could incentivize deforestation in the region. The agreement also facilitated access to State and private investment during the peace negotiations and the subsequent years. This has created several enabling conditions that made the area more accessible and less secure for communities, leading to a growing expansion of illegal and informal economies. The government has taken steps towards improving local governance, law enforcement in the region, improvement of security conditions and legalization of property rights.

(6) Consideration of information: The FREL/FRL should contain all relevant information that supplements the information contained in the Technical Assessment Report in relation to the consistency of the FREL/FRL with the GHG inventory, including any differences between the FREL/FRL and the GHG inventory. Colombia submitted in December 2014 its "Proposed Forest Reference Emission Level for deforestation" in the Colombian Amazon Biome for results-based payments for REDD under the UNFCCC. The definition of forest used in the FREL is the same as that defined by the National Forest Inventory (NFI) and includes all tree forms for monitoring purposes, such as a "Land mainly covered by trees which might contain bushes, palms, guadua, grasses and vines, where tree cover predominates with a minimum canopy density of 30%, a maximum canopy height (in situ) of 5 meters or more. The land is not used for agriculture or pasture, but may include small areas of secondary forest, palm crops and planted trees for agricultural production are excluded".

## The Technical Assessment Report (TAR) of the FREL (2015) considers that the submission and the annexes provided by Colombia, together with the clarifications given during the TA, are sufficient to provide a transparent and accurate description of FREL construction, including the data sets, approaches and methods used. Regarding the GHI-Inventories, the TAR notes that the proposed GHI-Inventories for Colombia based on the FREL exceeded the forest and grassland estimates in the published GHG inventories by a factor of five. TA found that the proposed GHI-Inventories were not consistent with the national inventory system and was not further considered because in Colombia a new and improved GHG inventory was being drafted under the first BUR and the third National Forest Inventory.

Following a stepwise approach, Colombia has made efforts to ensure consistency between GHI-Inventories and FREL.

The GHI-inventories under BUR (2018) included i) improved and updated activity data (time series of AD based on remote sensing images from Landsat and MODIS), ii) the latest National Forest Inventory (NFI) and iii) Revised Emission Factors (EF) from the preliminary results from the National Forest Inventory (which was 30% advanced).

The recently submitted National FREL (2019) included the Emission Factor (EF) improvements from BUR2 reflecting the preliminary results from the National Forest Inventory. The National FREL is currently under the technical assessment process.

### Data Source of the FRELFRLR

Please provide any additional information that supplements the information contained in the GHI-Inventories and/or the FREL, including the data source used to generate the FRELFRLR specifying whether the FREL/FRLR is based on historical data and is equal to or below the average annual historical emissions for the period 2000-2012.

The construction of the FREL for reducing emissions from deforestation (grass deforestation) in the Colombian Amazon Biome is based on the information generated by Colombia's Forest and Carbon Monitoring System (SMBCV), operated by the National Institute of Agricultural Sciences (ICAA) with the guidance of the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development (MADS).

### Activity data (AD) were generated based on the use of images from the Landsat program (USGS, 2014). Deforestation is defined as the loss of forest cover for a period of at least one year. The AD are classified in three phases of pre-processing, processing and validation of data. The methodology quantifies only those areas in which forests were lost during the period under analysis. Forests that were lost before 2000 but reforested within the analyzed period. Forest losses detected after one or several periods of time without information are not included in the calculation, in order to prevent over-estimations during periods in which areas without information increase.

FREL was defined by using the historical average of deforestation for the period 2000–2012. The reference level also incorporates the historical average of forest regrowth (forest cover gain) for the same period, as well as the CERs, as 10% over the value of annual deforestation from 2000-2012. The adjustment was justified by the results of a qualitative analysis that showed that the reference level is not consistent with Colombia's historical average and an increase of historical deforestation.

According to TAR, the methods used by Colombia are consistent with the Good Practice Guidance for Land Use, Land-Use Change and Forestry (GPG) of the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC), and the Guidelines for National Greenhouse Gas Inventories (IPCC, 2006). Paragraph 10 of the TAR points out that "The FREL is based on the mean historical deforestation rate from 2000 to 2012. The reference level includes the historical average of forest gain and grass deforestation (defined as loss of forest cover) for the period 2000–2012." It also points out that "Colombia has adjusted its proposed FREL, upward by 10 percent compared with the historical average emissions for the period 2000-2012."

### Colombia's Forest Transition and Deforestation Trends

#### Historical Context:
- **Transition from Deforestation:** Colombia transitioned from a stage of low deforestation into a period of high deforestation in the early 2000s.
- **Drivers of Deforestation:** The signing of the peace agreement with the FARC is cited as one of the main drivers, alongside the expansion of illicit crops with glyphosate.

#### Forest Cover and Deforestation Rates:
- **Forest Loss:** Colombia reported a natural forest area loss of 60,025,731 hectares in 2018.
- **Deforestation Rate Trends:**
  - The average deforestation rate from the late 1990s was at 2.4% per year.
  - The lowest value was recorded in 2013 (0.16%) and the highest in 2017 (5.8%).
- **Amazon Free FREL Period:** In the Amazon Free FREL period (2000–2012), the deforestation rate was 0.21%, while for the reference period, it increased to 0.34%.

#### Post-conflict Dynamics:
- The associated post-conflict dynamics had substantial impacts on deforestation rates reaching their peaks during the years 2017 and 2018, mainly in the Amazon region.

### LULUCF Expert Notes:

The LULUCF experts noted the impact of peace negotiations and recognized that the average annual historical emissions during the reference period would underestimate future rates of deforestation or forest degradation. They also highlighted that Colombia concluded successful negotiations with FARC in 2013–2016, which culminated in the signing of a peace agreement on August 24, 2016.

### Carbon Stocks and Emissions:
- **Carbon Stocks:** The carbon stocks in above-and below-ground biomass are 566.1 Gg CO2e (p24 TAE). The forest area is 12 million hectares.
- **Total Carbon Stock:** The total carbon stock is 39,973,700 t ha−1 (2.8 g C/m²).
- **Reference Period Emissions:** Colombia's original FREL submission to REDD+ in 2015 estimated the reference period emissions as 64.5 Mt CO2e.

### Adjustments and Calculations:
The section 2.a(ii) of the Scoring Criteria for the pilot programme for REDD+ result-based payments (RBP) states that "the average annual historical emissions do not exceed 0.1% of the carbon stock over the eligibility period in the relevant national or subnational area." Colombia's original FREL submission exceeded this threshold, and adjustments were made to ensure compliance with the criteria.

### Conclusion:
The table below provides a recalculation of the adjustment to the average annual historical emissions.

## (II) FRE/LRL in accordance with Decision 12/CIP-17: Please provide any additional information that supplements the information contained in the Technical Assessment Report (TAR) of the FRE/LRL. Include whether the FRE/LRL was constructed in accordance with the guidelines in Decision 12/CIP-17; specifically on the methodology for FRE/LRL and whether the results are considered reliable or not related to the qualified estimates of the FE/FLRL.

The construction of the FRE, for reducing emissions from deforestation (gross deforestation) in the Colombian Amazon Basin (CAB), was constructed in accordance with IPCC (2003). The TAR provided a "view" on the national and subnational scale to the development of the FRE according to the guidelines in the Annex to Decision 12/CIP-17. This approach is consistent with the recommendations for the construction of the FRE/LRL, including the methodologies and, when appropriate, new carbon pools and activities, and transition from subnational to a national FRE.

Paragraph 43 of the TAR points out: "The information used by Colombia in constructing its FRE is in all overall accordance with the guidelines for submission of information on FREs (as contained in the annex to decision 12/CIP-17). The TAR comments that on this basis, it is considered that the Colombian FRE/LRL is consistent with the requirements of the IPCC and the CDM."

### (e) FRE/LFL accuracy: reliability

Accuracy: Please provide any additional information that supplements the information contained in the Technical Assessment Report in relation to the consistency of the methodology used over the time series used for the construction of the FREL/FRL. The TAR addresses: "The documentation on methods, data and assumptions used, as well as the additional information provided by the FREL/FRL during the period covered by the FREL/FRL, was calculated. The four annexes of the modified submission provided a significant amount of additional technical material and increased transparency."

The AT noted that carbon densities that determine emission factors may have been based on different methodologies and assumptions than those used in the 2006 IPCC Guidelines. The AT also highlighted research data used for comparison. The report highlights efforts of Colombia to start the implementation of the National Forest Inventory (NFI) as a reference for the calculation of EF, reducing bias and increasing accuracy.

Currently Colombia is implementing the National Forest Inventory since 2012 and for the BUR2 used preliminary results for the calculation of EF, reducing bias and increasing accuracy. Colombia will include a thematic accuracy assessment for the NFI in the second FRE/LFL. In the second FRE/LFL, this accuracy assessment will follow the GFOI method and guidelines as well Olfsen et al. (2004).

### Significant points: Please provide any additional information that supplements the information contained in the Technical Assessment Report in relation to the inclusion of the most significant points, if applicable, justify whether applying default values for biomass and/or carbon stocks is appropriate or not, and whether there are underestimates or overestimates. In addition, provide a plan to include all significant points in future FREL/FRI submissions.

Colombia, following guidance from decisions 1/CP.16, paragraph 70 and the decisions 12/CP.17, paragraph 10; and paragraphs 35-48 of the Technical Assessment Report, has applied default values for biomass and carbon stocks according to their existing national capacities, due to lack of information available and representative of other comparable countries in the region. The application of these default values is justified by the fact that no data has not been reported due to a lack of data. The TAR sees this as an area for future improvement and recommended: "Use reference sites with better activity dates estimates, and NFI for better statistical sampling of biomass and inclusion of additional pools".

In relation with the use of IPCC default values, Colombia considers that for the Amazon natural region, the default values suggested by the IPCC are not representative. The reason is that these values are based on a large number of countries which could not be representative values of the distribution of biomass in these pools, given the level of aggregation of this information. Using these aggregated values would have an increasing effect on the reference level with higher levels of uncertainty. Colombia considers that the default values should be applied only to those countries where there is no data available and that they "should be accurate in the sense that they are neither over- nor underestimated as far as can be judged"; and (b) and provide a plan for future improvement, including the use of reference sites if practicable" [IPCC, 2006, Volume 1, Chapter 3], good practice for reporting activity data and emissions inventories).

### REDD-plus RFB Funding Proposal

#### Colombia data during the TA that an NFI planned for 2015-2017 was expected to generate data that would enable the inclusion of dead wood and soil organic carbon pools in a future FREL/FRI. The Technical Assessment Report included preliminary results of aboveground biomass estimates. Colombia clarified that, at the time of the TA, there were no plans to estimate the live core forest component of the NFI. The TA did not include any information on the inclusion of dead wood.

#### The only gas category reported in the FREL/FRI are those of CO2, as a result of changes in carbon stocks in living above- and below-ground biomass due to deforestation. The TAR raised an area for potential improvement on this topic: "Emissions from non-CO2 gases, using the methods developed for the GHG inventories to be published in association with the FREL/FRI."

#### As part of the second BUR Colombia estimated emissions of non-CO2 gases derived from fires in three land use categories: 3C1a forest lands; 3C1b Grasslands; 3C1c Grasslands. The annual area burned between 2002 and 2014 was obtained using MODIS images, which were processed by the Colombian Institute of Amazon Research (ICAM). A lognormal model, which takes into account extreme precipitation and drought phenomena (IDEAM, 2010), is used to estimate the emissions from fires. The data are provided in a report that includes information on other environmental authorities, which are consolidated annually by IDEAM.

#### As this information is coming from different entities, it was not possible to include it as part of the FREL/FRI. However, Colombia has started monitoring systems that have been determining deforested areas associated to hot spots using MODIS images. It is expected that this information will be available for the periods 2015 and 2016 and would be consistent with deforestation analysis and could be included in future reports.

#### (ix) IPCC guidance for FREL/FRI: Please indicate if whether the construction of the FREL/FRI, (data, methodologies and estimates) was guided by 2003 GPGs or 2006 GLs.

The methods used by Colombia are consistent with the Good Practice Guidance for Land Use, Land-Use Change and Foresty (LULUCF) [hereinafter referred to as the IPCC good practice guidance for LULUCF], and the 2006 IPCC Guidelines for National Greenhouse Gas Inventories (IPCC, 2006).

#### Paragraph 12 of TAR highlights: "The methods used by Colombia are consistent with the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) Good Practice Guidance for Land Use, Land-Use Change and Forestry [hereinafter referred to as the IPCC good practice guidance for LULUCF], and the 2006 IPCC Guidelines for National Greenhouse Gas Inventories (IPCC, 2006), as applied to the construction of the FREL/FRI, and as discussed further below."

#### Issues related to applying IPCC guidance: Please mention any significant issues related to the application of IPCC GLAGLAC. The TAR points out that "The methods used by Colombia are consistent with the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) Good Practice Guidance for Land Use, Land-Use Change and Forestry […] and the 2006 IPCC Guidelines for National Greenhouse Gas Inventories […] as applied to the construction of the FREL/FRI."

The TAR raises no issues related to the application of IPCC GLAGLAC. The TAR points out: "The methods used by Colombia are consistent with the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) Good Practice Guidance for Land Use, Land-Use Change and Forestry […] and the 2006 IPCC Guidelines for National Greenhouse Gas Inventories […] as applied to the construction of the FREL/FRI."

# REDD-plus RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND | PAGE 10 of 104

### Differences in the estimations between FREL and BUR2 were reported in the NIR, highlighting the following:

- **BUR2 uses less and uses more methodologies (IPCC, 2006), which includes estimates of deforestation and new land uses of areas previously deforested.** FREL does not include this approach, as the methodology for typical forested soils is not yet standardized until 2012. This improvement is in progress and is expected to include it in future reports.
- **BUR2 estimated emissions derived from deforestation starting at 1990, while FREL estimation period begins in 2000.** The time horizon determines a change in the annualization of gross emissions of the soil stock, because BUR2 includes data up until 2015 and FREL up to 2016.
- **BUR2 includes calculation of dead organic matter based on the factors T1 (IPCC 2006).** This stock is not included in the FREL since the country has not developed its own factor T1. It is expected to include this component in future reports, once Colombia is at a development stage based on the results.

### (b) Transparency of the data: Please provide any additional information that supplements the information contained in the Technical Analysis Report in relation to the transparency of the data and information provided in the technical annex of the BUR and the TATR. The TATR points out that Colombia provided relevant information regarding the implementation of actions related to REDD+, including relevant links where more detailed information could be accessed. ## REDD+ Early Movers RFP Project under the Amazon Vision Program (AVP)

Colombia and the United Kingdom signed agreements with the Governments of Norway, United Kingdom and Germany, which was structured as a Results-Based REDD+ financial cooperation program in the Colombian Amazon. This agreement is completed by the implementation of the Emission Reduction Payment Mechanism (ERPM) for the reduction of carbon dioxide to be financed by the program and the disbursement procedures for the implementation of the Program. This program is being implemented under the framework of the Amazon Vision Program http://www.amazonvision.com/mensajes-por-cuota-que-consiste-al-pago-con-resultados.

The specific agreement, signed in February 2016, established that payments will be conditional to the report, verification and approval by Colombia of the Emission Reduction Report (ERR) for the reference period from January 2013 to 2017 (section 8.11 of the agreement). Likewise, section 8.4 of this agreement establishes that the REM-VHP will be implemented in the Colombian Amazon using the National Reference Level (NRL) and Colombia's Emission Reductions from Deforestation (82,863 ha/year). For the carbon cost, the NRE uses a value of 154.3 C$/ha for the forests of the reference region, which corresponds to the average price in the market.

The agreement included the implementation of a risk mechanism; this risk mechanism considered that for each ton of rewarded emission reduction, an additional ton had to be declassified/cancelled. The government of Colombia and REDD+ Early Movers agreed on the application of this risk mechanism. Thus, for 2016, following the successful implementation of a MRV reporting to reduce the uncertainty in the emissions reductions offered by Colombia and REDM agreed that for each ton of rewarded emission reduction, 0.9 tons will be declassified/cancelled.

As established in the agreement, the Colombian government has reported emission reductions under REDD+ Early Movers from January 2013 to December 2016, achieved by Colombia's Emission Reductions from Deforestation in the Amazon biome for the periods: 2013 - 2014, 2015 and 2016.

In order to ensure transparency with the emission reductions offered and paid, the Colombian government made public all the information related to the ERR, including the methodology used for its verification/reporting and the registry of the emissions reductions between 2013-2015 and the official communication payments of the REM-VHP. These documents can be found in the following links:

* Emission reduction report 2013 - 2014: https://www.amazonvision.com/mensajes-por-cuota/contenidos/uploads/2017/09/20160916_Reporte_REM-03012015_01c81bce.pdf
* Emission reduction report 2015: https://www.amazonvision.com/mensajes-por-cuota/contenidos/uploads/2019/01/Reporte_REM-06015_09242015.pdf
* Emission reduction report 2016:

Therefore, the ERs Rewarded and the ERs for the risk mechanism are both deactivated/cancelled in REMARE. The deforestation in Colombia raised to 219,793 ha, an increase of 23% compared with the deforestation reported for the year 2016.

As referred in the RFP, the peace talks between Colombian government and the Revolutionary Armed Forces of Colombia (FARC) have been ongoing since August 2016. The agreement reached by both parties includes a provision that foresees potential negative consequences of the post-conflict period on forests, mainly due to access to areas and the lack of planning for the sustainable development of territories. In the last five years deforestation in Colombia has been concentrated in the departments of Cauca, Meta and Guaviare, involving municipalities where the armed conflict

The impact of post-conflict is also evidenced in other deforestation hotspots such as Serenita de San Lucas (Antioquia) and Bolívarcatá (Nariño). The authors argue that the conflict would increase deforestation, particularly in areas where the conflict would increase its development at expenses of the removal of vast areas covered by natural forests. Even in the most environmentally optimal scenario, the authors argue that the environmental impact of the conflict will be significant and irreversible.

The authors also argue that the economic benefits of the conflict will be short-term and limited to the extraction of natural resources. The long-term economic benefits at the end of the conflict will come from the abundant natural assets available in the region. In this context of accelerated economic growth, forests will continue to be one of the most affected resources.

## Instruments and mechanisms to increase transparency and avoid double payment:

The Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development (MADS acronym in Spanish) is the national government entity in charge to regulate the management of the environment and natural resources in the territory and also represents Colombia as a party to international environmental agreements. The MADS is in charge of the management of environmental and renewable natural resources, responsible for guiding and regulating the use of these resources through the National Environmental System (SINA), organized in accordance with Law 498/1993 and other laws.

The Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development is also responsible for directing the National Environmental System (SINA), organized in accordance with Law 498/1993, which includes the Regional Autonomous Corporations (CARs) that are in charge to ensure compliance of the duties and rights of the State and of individuals in relation to the environment and the natural resources. The CARs are also responsible for the execution of the National Environmental System (SINA), by law 498/1993 and other laws, the MADS will (...) exercise the inspection and surveillance of the Regional Autonomous Corporations.

By the Regulation 14172018 of the MADS, Colombia regulates the National Monitoring, Reporting and Verification System, specifying the instruments related to accounting of GHG emissions reductions and removals, and the National Registry of Emissions Reductions (NARE). The NARE is a registry that allows for the registration of projects or initiatives or projects, and claiming for results-based payments or similar compensations, as well as those interested in registering their projects or initiatives. The NARE also allows for the registration of projects or initiatives with result-based payments by registering in the RENARE, the emission reductions achieved on the Amazon biome for the period 2013-2016 against the sub National ForesT, assessed by the UNFCCC. According to its Resolution 1487, the

reduced emissions accredited by the MADS within the framework of national or sub-national GHG emission reduction programs will not have a complaining claim by any other party.

One of the instruments regulated by this legislation is the National Registry of GHG Emissions Reductions (RENARE), which is a technological platform (part of the MRV system) developed with the purpose of managing information of all GHG emissions reductions and credits. The information related to the formulation, implementation, monitoring, evaluation, and other activities related to initiatives aimed at reducing deforestation in the Amazon region, among other relevant activities, will be registered in RENARE.

### 1. GHG mitigation programs: NAMA, REDD+ programs, low-carbon development programs.

3. Other initiatives of GHG mitigation defined by the MADS or under the GHG mitigations mechanisms of the UNFCCC.

Currently, RENARE is in the starting phase; thus, in the meantime, Colombia implemented an Interim Registry, which was designed to order to report reduced ER as well as the payments received; both sources of information will be available while the starting phase is finalized. Following national regulations, payments received from GCF will be registered in the national registry (RENARE) for public consultation and publication. It is important to highlight that the Interim Registry is also the instrument that Colombia agreed to use with the REM to report emissions reductions and payments.

Regarding risks management, as part of the REM agreement Colombia agreed on a mechanism to address risks of loss or damage related to the implementation of the project. The risk mitigation measure will be considered deactivable for each unit of ER paid by the REM Program another verified ER unit of the same year, as an own contribution of Colombia. This arrangement is subject to verification and approval by GCF. The arrangement would be revised every year during the implementation of the project, after the verification. If Colombia implements this measure, the level of the own contributions may lead to reduce uncertainty and improve data examination. Colombia could reduce its own contributions up to 15%. According to this mechanism Colombia's own contribution will be reduced from 20% in 2014 to 8.3% in 2016.

# REDD-plus RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND | PAGE 18 OF 104

### Amazon Vision Program AVP: As an umbrella program this initiative is negotiated by the Government of Colombia aimed at reducing deforestation in the Amazon Region. The program includes four relevant projects including this REDD+ RBP project.

#### 1. GEF-Heart of the Amazon Project: funded with resources from the Global Environmental Facility and managed by the World Bank (GCF).

#### 2. Conservation and Biodiversity Conservation in the Colombian Amazon (child project): conceived as additional funding to complement activities of the Heart of the Amazon Project and tourneague specific strategies to promote rural development, territorial governance, and land use planning; the first strategy is to support local communities' tenure governance and promote sustainable land use activities (implemented by World Bank), and the second to promote rural development through a market-based peace building approach (being implemented by UNDP under the name of Amazonia Sostenible para la Paz).

#### 3. REM – Amazon Vision Program (AVP): as described before this is a REM project funded by the Governments of UK, Norway and Germany and is the only Results-Based project that is being implemented in the Amazon.

It is important to highlight that under the umbrella of the AVP, Colombia has made efforts to align other international cooperation actions with the objectives of the AVP. The Colombian Government has signed a Memorandum of Understanding (MoU) with the World Bank and UNDP, and that joint international cooperation actions contribute to the achievement of results.

#### Colombia Sustainable Fund: Funded by the International community, this mechanism was developed to support the execution of projects in Colombia that promote peace in Colombia with environmental sustainability. The Fund is managed by the International Development Bank (IDB) and started implementation in 2019 through a series of pilot projects. It is expected that projects supported under this fund will also contribute to reduce deforestation and GHG emissions in the region.

#### Herencia Colombiana (HCo): A program designed with the objective of contributing to the achievement of Colombian goals in terms of reducing deforestation, promoting sustainable land use practices, and improving rural development. The Program will operate through the design and subsequent implementation of a long-term financing model for the National System of Forests and Watersheds (SINIFOR) and the creation of a national carbon market to generate revenues from allocation of Colombia’s Carbon Tax. Under the umbrella of this initiative, Colombia proposed a project to the Green Climate Fund (GCF), which was approved in 2018. The project is called Herencia Colombiana (HCo) and it focuses upon milestone-based payments led to policy, institutional, and management reforms. During the design process, HCo is promising landscapes for intervention at the national level, including the Amazon Region with the objective of complementing efforts of the AVP and consequently align the investments that will be made with resources of the Green Climate Fund.

Colombia has created and implemented a National System of Safeguards (SNS in Spanish) that includes five essential aspects: i) understanding the context; ii) identification of risks according to national circumstances; iii) consolidation of the national legal and institutional framework; iv) implementation of tracking tools; v) engagement of the Safeguard Information System (SIS in Spanish); and vi) reporting mechanisms and continuous improvement.

# REDD-plus RBP Funding Proposal

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND | PAGE 19 OF 104

In order to address these safeguards Colombia has accomplished as follows:

### i) Understanding: From the seven Carbon Safeguards, Colombia has defined 15 elements adapted to the national context of Colombia. These are referred to as Social and Environmental Safeguards for REDD+ in Colombia. A Caruán refers to the element A1: Correspondence with forest, biodiversity and climate change international agreements signed by Colombia (letters correspond to the consecutive national assignment).

The understanding of element A1 is based on the "Integrated Strategy of Deforestation Control and Forest Management" (SICAF) approved by Law 694 of 1998, the National Action Plan for Mitigation of Deforestation (PAMD), which represents the REDD+ National Strategy. The ECDIG constitutes a bridge between green growth and climate change national policies concerning to implementation of the international agreements ratified by the country.

The ECDIG is consistent with the National Climate Change Policy (PNCC in Spanish), the National Policy for the Integral Management of Forests (PNIFF in Spanish) approved by Law 1598 of 2012, the National System of Protected Areas (SNPA in Spanish) Decree 368, the Policy of Sustainable Soil Management (PSMS in Spanish) approved by Law 741 of 2002, and the National Forest Policy (PNF in Spanish).

### ii) Legal and institutional framework: The legal basis for REDD+ is established taking into account the national regulations where the scope of application and the subjects of rights were identified. This baseline was consolidated in a matrix and a document that contains the regulatory framework for REDD+ social and environmental safeguards.

Under A1 safeguarded and the ECDIG policy framework, the relevant international agreements signed by Colombia are:

- United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC), notably the Kyoto Protocol ratified by Law 629 of 2004.
- Ramsar Convention on Wetlands of International Importance.
- Convention of Biological Diversity (CBD) approved by Law 537 of 1987 and Nagoya Protocol approved by Law 1008 of 2008.
- International Tropical Timber Agreement, approved by Law 664 of 1998.
- United Nations Convention to Combat Desertification and Drought (UNCCD), approved by Law 461 of 1993.
- Convention on International Trade in Endangered Species of Wild Fauna and Flora approved by Law 17 of 1995.
- Andean Decision 391 adopted by Law 165 of 1994.

Every four years, the Government of Colombia agrees on a National Development Plan (NDP), which provides the strategic framework for the country's development. The NDP is aligned with the national environmental goals and provides an agenda for the national environmental policies for a four-year period. The next National Development Plan (2016-2019) was approved by Law 1758 of 2014, which established the ECDIG and developed the National Safeguard System (SNS). Both instruments recognize the environmental and social safeguards for Colombia as a means to materialize the commitments made in the UNFCCC.

In order to respect these safeguarding tools: Within the SNS Colombia has implemented the Tracking Protocol for Safeguards that addresses and tracks each safeguard. This protocol includes structural, process and result indicators for the design and implementation of REDD+ actions at national, regional and local levels.

Referring to this safeguard, the Proposal proposes three tracking mechanisms that aim to incorporate commitments and monitor the progress in the achievement of these commitments: The ECDIG; the SNS; and the National Action Plan (NAP). At the national level, the tracking mechanism 1.1 – "The ECDIG incorporates international commitments and obligations acquired by Colombia related to forests, biodiversity and climate change" is used. This mechanism includes a matrix that tracks the international commitments and obligations acquired by Colombia related to forests, biodiversity, land and climate change" are verified with.

### National reports elaborated to comply with international commitments.
- **ECOSUR actions and goals in chapters 6 and 7.**
- **Online repository of deforestation data in the documentation centers of the Colombian Environmental Information System (SINAMA).**

Annual reports on the status of the National Environmental Monitoring System (SNIF) (in Spanish).

At the regional and local levels, the SNIF includes 13 "Number of actions" of ECOSUR initiatives that contribute to the implementation of the ECOSUR. This is verified with the elaboration of regional plans, such as the Operative Annual Plans (POAs in Spanish) or municipalities' projects etc.

### iv) Operationalization of SNIF
The ECOSUR includes a monitoring and evaluation component that is carried out through:

- A results framework to monitor the progress of the proposed activities included in the lines of action. This is based on the indicators defined for each line.
- Existing institutions for Forest Monitoring: Forest and Carbon Monitoring System (SMBYC in Spanish), National Forest Inventory (INIF in Spanish) and the National Forest Information System (SNIF in Spanish). These systems allow to monitor the effectiveness of the implemented actions and to inform results in reducing deforestation.

The SNIF is a system that was designed for the collection, processing, storage, analysis and dissemination of data. Its design was based on the following principles: standardization of monitoring, measurement, collection, data management and reporting processes; modularity and scalability; flexibility and adaptability; and interoperability with other systems. The SNIF ensures the quality and consistency of the reported data. In essence, the MMRV depends on the SMBYC, SINIF and POA. The SNIF is a system that allows for the generation of reports and the development of national reports (NDC: National Communications, BUR, PREL etc.).

On the other hand, during 2016 and 2016 the country designed the National Registry of Emissions of REDD+ Programs and Projects (REDD+ REGISTRO NACIONAL DE EMISIONES - RENUE in Spanish). It establishes clear accounting rules for public and private initiatives at the national and regional level.

## (i) Transparent and effective national forest governance structures, taking into account national legislation and sovereignty.
In order to address this safeguard Colombia has accomplished the following:

- Understanding: This safeguard integrates four elements (lettercode number corresponds to the Cancún safeguard label in the national REDD+ strategy document).
  - B2. Transparency and access to Information: As far as possible, it must be ensured that REDD+ information is transparent, accessible (available including open data) to every stakeholder, as well as complete, clear and easy to understand. This includes the publication of relevant documents such as national strategies, action plans, project proposals, booklets, internet, radio, television or the Citizen Grievance Mechanism (MAC in Spanish, part of the SINIF). However, specific mechanisms must be defined for each particular case according to the information needs and circumstances.
  - B3. Tracking safeguards: Institutions and actors must submit reports to stakeholders on their REDD+ initiatives, including progress towards meeting national commitments. All parties involved are committed to attending the informative sessions and reports must be public and accessible to all of them.

- B4. Recognition of forest governance structures: REDD+ actions must be developed according to the existing forest governance provided by the national (National REDD+ board), regional and local (forest boards) environmental authorities, in accordance with the National Forest Policy and the participatory decision-making process. The national regulatory framework establishes hierarchies and functions in the National Environmental System (SINAMA). These are defined at the level of the Autonomous Regions, Departments or Districts or Municipalities. At the national level there are other different coordination mechanisms that allow for the participation of all stakeholders in the participatory decision-making related to forest governance and management, including measures and actions to reduce deforestation:

1. National Environmental Council

# REDD+ National Board

2. International Commission on Climate Change and Technical Committees (Currently Considered)
3. National Forest Board
4. Climate Change Working Groups Nodes
5. Regional Forest Boards: Putumayo, Caquetá, Amazonas, Guaviare
6. Regional Forest Management Commissions
7. Territorial planning councils
8. Territorial Forest Councils of Macro-basins
9. Basin councils

Local and traditional communities are involved at the national level through the National Environmental Council, the REDD+ National Board, the Technical Committees and the Commission of the Council. At the regional/local level, local communities, indigenous peoples and other ethnic groups or civil society participants are invited to participate in the regional nodes of the regional forest boards, regional forest management commissions and territorial planning councils.

### Safeguard 8: Capacity building

The strengthening of the technical, legal, management and governance capacities of all stakeholders must be guaranteed, so that the Parties can document and make informed decisions.


## ii) Legal and institutional framework: Colombian legal framework for the elements B2 and B3 is comprised of the historical and current legislation in force. The B2 element refers to the information provided by the State to public information and other binding instruments such as: mechanisms of information request under constitutional rights, public participation in decision-making processes, etc.

In addition, the forest governance structure (B4) is legally addressed firstly by the National Environmental System (SINA) led by the MADS. The SINA is comprised of 33 regional and six urban autonomous authorities and, five scientific research centers. The B4 element refers to the information provided by the State on its forest governance strategies, which includes the forest policy, the forest resource management regime and consequently, the implementation of the National Forest Policy in accordance with the Colombian Constitution; the SINA has defined instruments for ecosystems planning and management and established mechanisms for access to spaces within national forest reserves and zoning of protected areas.

In Colombia the information on protected areas, such as management plans and geocoding/characteristics, is publicly available through the Rural Use and Natural Resources Registry (RUNAP) in Spanish under the National System of Protected Areas (SINAP), which includes public and private governance schemes for all the different types of protected areas.

Other communication instances between the government and local and ethnic communities have been established in the National Climate System (ESCLIMAN in Spanish), the cross-sectoral Commission for Control of Deforestation and the intersectoral Commission for Forest Management. The SINA has also defined instruments to strengthen capacities with the Board, the Environment and Rights board of Afro-communication and the Environment and Climate Change Board of Indigenous communities.

Finally, the Capacity Building element (B5) is aligned to the national regulation of SINAP's entities, which defines roles to strengthen capacities of communities through training and communication programs to promote greater knowledge of general forest management issues. The B5 element refers to the information provided by the State on its informed, documented and analyzed decision-making processes of the local and ethnic communities. On this regard, the ECOFONDO's strategy is focused on strengthening capacities for decision-making of local communities and institutional capacity building in response to a timely manner to difficulties associated to monitoring and enforcement of deforestation prevention.

In order to respect this safeguard, Colombia has accomplished the following:

### Comments: In order to address this safeguard, Colombia has accomplished the following:
- **Understanding:** In order to attain this safeguard in the Colombian context, the national government has defined four elements for its implementation. The first three correspond to the safeguard numbers and the last letter-number codes correspond to Cancún safeguard labels, and to the consecutive national assignment.
  - CIF: Free, Prior and Informed Consent (CPIC) in Spanish: When an intervention may impact one or more ethnic groups the national consultation mechanisms such as, free, prior and informed consent, established in the legislation of each department, must be applied. The National Indigenous Council and the indigenous communities must guarantee the rights of communities to effectively participate in decision-making processes.
  - CIF: Free, Prior and Informed Consent (CPIC) in Spanish: Under the national law, the participation of indigenous communities is guaranteed during the planning and construction processes. In addition, decision-making spaces must be created respecting the autonomy of people in the management of their territories.

#### C7: Respect for Traditional Knowledge:
Traditional knowledge systems and visions of ethnic and local communities in territories are respected, recognized, and promoted, in accordance to the provisions with international conventions. When any initiative to reduce deforestation is deployed, the cultures of inhabitants in the protected areas are not affected. The actions derived from the EICDSB must promote a different approach that respect and promote the traditional knowledge systems and visions of ethnic and local communities in territories, with the designed jointly with communities, incorporating, recognizing and respecting their knowledge, traditional practices and livelihoods.

#### C8: Benefit distribution:
The fair and equitable participation and distribution of the benefits generated by PAM to reduce deforestation among ethnic and local communities, and all those benefits derived from traditional knowledge systems and visions of ethnic and local communities in territories are guaranteed. The EICDSB must integrate knowledge and understanding of all types of benefits—whether economic, social, cultural or ecosystem—in order to ensure they are fair and equally distributed. If necessary, roles of mechanisms should be clarified.

#### C9: Territorial Rights:
The collective and individual territorial rights of ethnic and local communities are respected as well as their uses and cultural, economic and spiritual significance. The development of PAMs must recognize and respect the traditional land tenure systems and practices in territories, and the use of land and tenure in areas expected to implement REDD+ measures and actions and make decisions accordingly. During the EICDSB process, it is necessary to ensure that all parties involved are informed about how planning processes are related to land tenure and use conflicts, which affect governability of indigenous peoples, after- deforestation activities, and the implementation of PAMs. The EICDSB must also address the problems associated with land tenure; the EICDSB includes measures to promote harmonization of planning instruments (including PAMs) with existing land tenure systems and practices in territories, and avoid conflicts of land use and tenure, specifically in protected areas, deforestation hotspots and forest management units. The EICDSB also includes measures to address the problems associated with land tenure (planning processes). The EICDSB also proposes elements to improve conditions of use and management of the territory in areas where PAMs are implemented. In addition, the EICDSB promotes the strengthening of indigenous governance in collective territories and respect traditional practices, uses, and cultural, economic and spiritual significance of ethnic and local communities in territories. In the case of protected areas and national parks lands, the National Parks Unit is implementing a Special Management Regime, which was conceived as planning and management instruments for the conservation of natural resources and the implementation of Indigenous Agreements between the National Parks Unit and indigenous authorities that recognize strategic principles and guidelines of the Policy of Social Participation of indigenous Peoples of Colombia in Conservation.

#### ii) Legal and institutional framework:
The four elements of this safeguard are recognized under 26 legal instruments (including international conventions, national laws, regulations, decrees, agreements, policies, plans, and local communities regarding prior consultation, traditional knowledge and territorial rights. The main legal instruments are listed below:

1. "ILO Convention 169 and the United Nations Declaration on Indigenous Peoples," adopted by Law 21 of 1991, is the main reference of the current regulation on the protection of ethnic communities, and support of the special constitutional status that they have in Colombia. This instrument recognizes the right to self-determination; the close relationship of indigenous peoples with their lands as fundamental bases of their cultures, their integrity and their economic development; and the need for the State to respect these rights. In addition, according to Article 16 of the Inter-American Court of Human Rights, it is clear the need to consult ethnic communities for the development of REDD+ projects, to the extent that those projects directly affect the communities in forests where the projects will be developed.

2. Political Constitution of Colombia "arts. 7, 2, 63, paragraph article 330, art 93 and transitory 55." The rights to consultation and participation are recognized as a fundamental right for ethnic peoples. These rights are aimed at the generation of spaces for decision-making, where the autonomy of indigenous peoples and black communities in the management of their territories is respected. In this sense, information on REDD+ projects or initiatives and any participatory process must be based on information guaranteed through this safeguard and the four elements that compose it.

3. The United Nations Declaration on the Rights of Indigenous Peoples adopted in 2007 recognizes the spiritual relationship between indigenous peoples and their land; it also recognizes that they have rights to lands they traditionally occupied, and to own and control the resources they own. Colombia recognizes the high ethnic diversity of indigenous peoples and their cultural heritage. The Colombian Constitution recognizes the importance of knowledge, innovations and practices incorporating traditional lifestyles, which have also been fundamental for the conservation and sustainable use of natural resources.

4. The Policy for the Safeguarding of the Cultural and Intangible Heritage seeks to guarantee and promote respect for the intangible cultural heritage of indigenous peoples at both national and international levels. It recognizes that at a local level the importance of the recognition of intangible cultural heritage and international cooperation and assistance to make effective the safeguard, which has normative instruments that it developed as its Decree 2914 of 2009.

5. Law No. 783 of 2002 (planning law) recognizes that indigenous communities must participate effectively in planning processes as a mechanism for guaranteeing their rights. In addition, mechanisms are provided for abo-descendant and indigenous communities to carry out development planning in their territories. The participation of the affected communities is also required when making decisions on territorial development and land management decisions must also be incorporated into the planned instruments of the territorial entities.

6. Jurisprudence (CC - C-89102) has also indicated that citizen participation has developed as a two-way issue between the offer of opportunities and the popular receptivity of spaces, scenarios and mechanisms of participation. The legislation provides for the participation of indigenous peoples in the design and implementation phases of the project and respect for the deliberative and organizational instruments (developers of REDD+ projects must guarantee their full exercise).

7. Orders 004 and 005 of the Constitutional Court define that prior consultation must be carried out when the design and implementation of the Program for the Guarantees of the Rights of Indigenous Peoples is carried out and ethnic and cultural diversity is taken into account in the planning, design and implementation phases of the project as indicated by the Constitutional Court. In the case of the Afro-Colombian population, the preservation of cultural heritage in areas of displacement and confinement is a priority for the design and implementation of specific protection and care plans for each of the Afro-Colombian communities in a situation of displacement and confinement and the characterization plan of the collective and ancestral territories is carried out.

### and effective participation of all stakeholders is respected to ensure governance and adequate decision-making on REDD+ actions.

#### Implementation of REDD+ initiatives must ensure the effective, free, full and informed participation of stakeholders. To do so, adequate mechanisms of participation must be deployed in territories, their own existing participation structures must be strengthened, and new ones created where necessary, with and members of local communities, according to national legislation and international agreements signed by Colombia.

#### ii) Legal and institutional framework: Colombia is a democratic, participatory and pluralistic State of rule of law. Organized around the National System of Citizen Participation (SINAC), which guarantees citizen participation as a constitutional right, defines the obligations and responsibilities of public administration entities to guarantee citizen participation in decisions that may affect them by promoting specific citizen participation mechanisms.

#### Colombia has the National System of Citizen Participation, which seeks to articulate instances, deliberation spaces, financial resources, and decision-making processes at all levels of government. The National Council of Citizen Participation, jointly with the National Planning System leads the National System and articulates the national agenda for citizen participation in public policies and programs at different territorial levels. These instances include the youth council, afro-descendant peoples council and the culture council, among others.

#### In addition, SINAC has instances and mechanisms for citizen participation in environmental matters that guarantee the right of citizens to be informed, intervene in decisions that affect them and request deliberation instances to discuss issues related to their rights.

#### At the local level, the entities of the department, municipal and district territories have within their organizational structure, an area in charge of promoting citizen participandion responsible for: a) Formulating total participation policies; b) Promoting the development of mechanisms for the effective participation of citizens in the implementation of the local participation policies; and c) Ensuring proper studies and integration of the recommendations made by civil society actors.

#### Finally, the EICOGIB incorporates the plan for participation and involvement of stakeholders that recognizes all the existing platforms for participation and decision-making of the communities and their stakeholders. All actors participate in the REDD+ National Board.

#### The Regional Climate Change Nodes within the National Climate Change System (SSCLIMA in Spanish) is another national mechanism established by Colombia in 2018, its objective is to coordinate and implement climate actions with stakeholders of different regions of Colombia.

## (v) Actions are taken to protect the conservation of natural forests and biodiversity, ensuring that:

The actions referred to in paragraph 70 of this decision are not used for the conversion of natural forests, but are instead used to incentivize the protection and conservation of natural forests and their ecosystems services.

In order to address the Cancun Safeguard E, Colombia has accomplished the following:

### (i) Understanding: Colombia's interpretation of safeguard E comprises the following two elements:
- **E11. Forest and biodiversity conservation**: REDD+ initiatives contribute to the conservation of forests and biodiversity goals of Colombia. They must support the provision of ecosystem services and the access of communities to these services, as well as the implementation of forest and biodiversity strategies. FMs must consider actual and future conservation processes in the territory.
- **E12. Ecosystem services**: REDD+ initiatives must benefit directly or indirectly of the provision of ecosystem services (supply, support, regulation, and culture).

### (ii) Legal framework: Colombia recognizes the benefits of environmental services to communities provided by the conservation and management of ecosystems and their biodiversity. To guarantee this, legal and regulatory provisions are aligned with coordinated actions of the environmental authorities and the territorial entities. The National Forest Policy (PNF), which is the main instrument for reducing deforestation emissions, due to deforestation and soil degradation. The ECOBID (REDD+ Strategy) includes specific action for conservation and sustainable use of forests and biodiversity.

### Local environmental authorities are responsible instruments to promote conservation, generate local benefits and reduce risks of conversion of natural forests. At the same time, they have a fundamental instrument to promote conservation and sustainable use of biodiversity and the associated ecosystem services:

- **National Policy for Integrated Management of Biodiversity and its Ecosystem Services (PGBSE)**: 2014.
- **National Forest Policy**: 2015.
- **Forest Sector Planning, regulated by Law 2811 of 1874, Law 193 of 1963 and Decree 1718 of 1991**.
- **Marine Biodiversity Conservation Plan (PBCM)**: 2015. This plan is regulated by Regulation No. 1511 of 2017.
- **Conservation planning on private lands regulated by Decree 198 of 1999**.
- **Management plans and zoning of forest reserves, regulated by Decree 2372 of 2010**.
- **Guidelines for the implementation of the National Forest Policy (PNF)**: Law No. 655 of 1993, regulated by resolutions 1922, 1924, 1925 and 1928 of 2013.
- **Forest management plans, regulated by Decree 1640 of 1962**.
- **Protected area management plans, regulated by Decree 2372 of 2010**.
- **Integrated Management of Biodiversity and its Ecosystem Services (PGBSE in Spanish)**: National Policy for the Integral Management of Biodiversity and its Ecosystem Services.
- **Forest conservation agreements, regulated by Decree 458 of 1963 and Decree 1458 of 2012**.
- **Payment for Environmental Services, regulated by Decree 870 of 2012 and Decree 953 of 2013.**
- **Environmental and sustainable development sector, regulated by Decree 1076 of 2015.**

### Local environmental authorities guarantee the compatibility of REDD+ actions by implementing ecosystem management plans that are aligned with the National Forest Policy (PNF) and other policies aimed at achieving the objectives of conservation, rehabilitation and recovery of ecosystems and environmental goods and services.

### OVERVIEW OF THE PROJECT

This project promotes voluntary conservation agreements implemented by the Amazon Institute of Scientific Research (SINCHI) in Spain. This model aims to transform production practices of the farmers of the Amazon region and promote sustainable land use, with a focus on three main components: 1) land planning; 2) establishment and evaluation of agroforestry systems; and 3) capacity building.

The conservation agreements recognize the direct relationship between the conservation and the sustainability of agricultural activities. They aim to reduce deforestation by promoting the adoption of more sustainable crops, reducing the demand for additional forest areas. The process included not only the improvement of existing practices but also the implementation of more sustainable production systems, appropriate to the Amazon ecosystem and soils, such as agroforestry.

The REM-AMP categorized the risks associated to these activities as Low Risk (safeguards E and F) or of Cancun conservation/obligatory diversity and displacement of emissions, respectively) using its own system for management of social conflicts. The agreements are signed by the communities and the landowners, with the objective of zero deforestation and promotion of forest and biodiversity conservation, as well as the articulation of all of the activities.

### FOLLOWING THE HEART OF THE AMAZON PROJECT

Following the Heart of the Amazon Project, the REM-AMP includes "conservation agreements" as a requirement for the beneficiaries of projects under the Agro-Environmental Pillar. These agreements considered aspects such as land use planning and the promotion of agroforestry systems in areas where agriculture frontier among others, and will have to advance in the incorporation of community oversight.

### IMPLEMENTATION OF THE PROJECT

In Guaviare, a total of 67 farms were selected: 31 in Caquetá municipality and 36 in San José del Guaviare. In Vaupés, a total of 58 farms were selected: 29 in the municipalities of San José de Uchiza and San Juan de Uchiza, and 29 in the municipality of Puerto Asís. In Caquetá, 64 farms of Cartagena de Chía municipality in four types of landscape were selected, which are those referred to previously in Guaviare, and the landscape of Iega.

### ACTIONS TO ADDRESS THE RISKS OF THE PROJECT

130 conservation agreements have been signed by communities of Caquetá as a result of a process that involved social consultation meetings with representatives of social, community and producer organizations. However, during the implementation of the project some signatories withdrew the agreements. Currently 141 conservation agreements remain in force: 75 in San José del Guaviare, 30 in San Juan de Uchiza, 29 in Puerto Asís, 28 in Caquetá and 6 in San José de Uchiva and Calamar, where 150 conservation agreements were subscribed, representing a total of $4.543 million. The REM-AMP has reported that the majority of signatories have complied with the conservation agreements; however, the REM-AMP has reported that the majority of signatory farmers have complied with the conservation agreements.

For addressing the consent safeguard F, Colombia has accomplished the following:

### UNDERSTANDING THE SAFEGUARD

F.13 Environmental and territorial management: Under a forest conservation and sustainable management approach, REDD+ actions support territorial and environmental planning of Colombia in accordance with the national legislation. REDD+ initiatives must recognize, respect, adapt and strengthen measures and instruments of territorial management and land use planning, as well as the promotion of participatory processes in the formulation and adjustment of planning instruments according to land use. The territorial planning and organization of land use is key for the conservation of forests and biodiversity.

F.14 Sectoral Planning: REDD+ sectoral action types must be implemented based on environmental and territorial management instruments, as well as national legislation of forests and biodiversity conservation. The trans-sectorial vision of forests is key to guarantee the accomplishment of the conservation and deforestation goals of Colombia.


---
**Page 33**
---

To do so, the projections of sectoral development must be recognized to ensure the long-term sustainability of the PAMA and its territory. In particular, they must also be aligned on respect for the environment with existing environmental and territorial planning instruments.

### ii) Legal and institutional framework: The most appropriate instrument to address the issue of reversibility is the expropriation of land (at a compensation level) regulated by Law 385 of 1997. The territorial planning instruments integrate environmental planning of ecosystems, risk management and sectoral development. These instruments establish limits to sectoral development and provide safeguards to prevent negative impacts on ecosystems.

Law 385 of 1997 establishes responsibilities to local environmental authorities to set guidelines for identifying environmental areas and the need to respect these areas in land use plans. The law also provides for expropriation, thereby granting environmental licenses. Urban and construction licenses (sectoral actions) are aligned to ecosystems management and territorial planning. This ensures long-term conservation and proper management of ecosystems, reducing the risk of reversibility.

In order to respect this safeguard, Colombia has accomplished the following:

#### ii) Implementation of tracking tools: The Pilar 2 of Amazon Vision. Sustainable sectoral planning and development, seeks to articulate processes between the sectors of transport, infrastructure and mining in the light of current and future environmental conditions. This is done through three main components:
- Harmonization of environmental management with land uses in the agricultural and livestock frontier of the Amazon.
- Strengthening of territorial entities responsible for the conservation and sustainable use of natural resources.
- The development of sectoral activities in areas of high environmental sensitivity.

#### iii) Strengthening of sectoral activities through the Incentive Strategy for Green Municipalities, and. iv) Strategic activities and capacity development.

The Pilar has the challenge of going beyond good practices to ensure the deforestation activities adopt a deforestation-free strategy. The Amazon Vision also seeks to strengthen the implementation of the Regional Land Management Model for the Amazon, designed to favor the integration of actions related to mining and energy extraction with those aimed at the conservation and sustainable use of natural resources in the region.

The REM+AVP supported the establishment of the Amazon Planning Administrative Region (RAP in Spanish) as a mechanism to contribute to the definition of the agricultural frontier, promote sustainable production systems, and strengthen the capacity for environmental management. The RAP also seeks to align sectoral agreements among other initiatives in the region.

On the other hand, based on the GIRESA the AVP carried out a risk analysis carried out in 2016 and the first half of 2017, which identified 85% of the risks associated with the Project (1) of Environmental Governance; 8 of Sustainable Sector Development, 5 of Agro-Environmental Development, 16 of Environmental Governance with Indigenous Peoples, 3 of Territorial Planning, and 4 of Land Use. The GIRESA AVP generates low or no environmental and social risks, but the remaining 6% must be attended to avoid the medium and high environmental and social risks.

Activities under Pilar 2, Sustainable Sector Development, presented the highest incidence (80%) of activities categorized as "too risky". For the actions that generate risks classified as medium and high, there is evidence that they are present in the sectoral plans. The main reasons for this are: lack of alignment between the sectoral plans and what is not consistent with the needs and expectations of the interested parties; low ownership of the processes in the territory; inadequate execution of resources; improper use of economic or other resources, as well as those related to corruption.

### iv) Operationalization of SIS. In order to address the risks, in 2016 and the first half of 2017, the AVP reported advances in:

- Structuring an intermediate transport plan for the Amazon.
- Supporting the definition of the agricultural frontier as part of the Colombian Peace Accord, through the environmental governance with indigenous peoples.
- Articulating the sectoral plans of different actors under the strengthening of the Administrative Planning Region of the Amazon.
- Strengthening the capacity of territorial entities to manage natural resources and land use.
- Detailed scale mapping of national protected areas that are in areas of high hydrocarbon contract dynamics.

- Work to exchange information and establish spaces for dialogue regarding projects of new National Parks or hydropower blocks that are already in exploration and production phases.
- For the pilot program, Colombia proposes deactivating 40% of ER available to GCF as an interim mechanism to manage risk of reversals (please refer to Annex 5 to see the details of the methodology used for estimations).
(vi) Actions to reduce displacement of emissions.

Comments: For addressing Cancun safeguard G, Colombia has accomplished the following:

(i) Understanding: The interpretation of this Safeguard for Colombia's framework is one element:
- G14. Emission displacement: REDD+ initiatives must incorporate actions to reduce the displacement of emissions. The emission displacement takes place when the causes of deforestation spread throughout the territory (e.g., due to climate change, population growth, and poverty) and are not addressed by the project (SMBC in Spanish). Additionally, Colombia articulates the national early warning system of deforestation with communities and other stakeholders through the protocol for prevention and control procedures against deforestation and prevent extension to other areas.

To facilitate knowledge generation, decision-making, education and social participation for sustainable development, SIAM has several systems that respond to the needs of users. The National Forest Information System (SINIFOR in Spanish) is one of these systems.
- National Forest Information System: Considers the information related to timber and non-timber products, main species endangered at the national and regional level; the current and potential use of forest species, the most important threats to forests, and the areas with high conservation value. #### GREEN CLIMATE FUND | PAGE 32 OF 104

**Strengthened technical capacities of MAIS, IDEAM, IA, and PNIN professionals, as well as Regional Environmental Authorities. A technical event was held for the exchange of experiences on the lifting permanent plots and conglomerates of the IFN.**

A new methodology has been developed to support the implementation of conglomerations and permanent plots in forest inventory in the Colombian Amazon.

Technical assistance has been provided to PNIN, particularly with the Forest and land management plans especially the harmonization of methodologies for plotting and their spatial location, agreeing to establish pilot units under the IFN sample design.

The REM-VP established a mechanism to manage risks and uncertainties. According to this mechanism for each ton of rewarded emission reduction, an additional ton is retired. This seeks to address items of shifting deforestation to avoid emissions (‘leakage’), the permanence of the avoided emissions (‘permanence’) and uncertainties associated with the estimation of emission reductions, e.g. with respect to activity data, emissions factors, etc.

The REM-VP also provides for the step-wise and continuous improvement of monitoring systems to mitigate these risks and to review it. Annual assessments are conducted by PNIN on risks, including on their management and monitoring.

Complementarily, in 2016 Colombia attached a Technical Annex in the Second Biennial Update Report, with the NREF, whose contents were discussed at the National Forum in April 2017, and concluded about the complying and the transparency requirements of data and information provided by the Country, and in general, related to those established in decision 3/CP.14 of the UNFCCC.

On the other hand, during 2015 to 2017, REM-VP contributed to respect this safeguard through the following results of SBYMIC:

* The quarterly Early Warning deforestation bulletin, corresponding to the fourth quarter of 2016 (October–December), for the first quarter of 2017; include information at the national level (regional and Early Deforestation Alerts), Regional, departmental, Regional Autonomous Corporations and municipalities.
* Reductions in the number of Forest Environmental Violations of the Amazon region were generated and validated by the UNFCCC.
* Improvement on the technical performance through the adoption of High Performance Systems for Digital Processing of Remote sensor images, and new data storage capacities for the IDEAM Data Centre. Additionally, improvements in data processing capabilities at the Regional Environmental Authorities, such as Corporación para la Sostenibilidad del Amazon (CORPOMAZONIA) in Spanish) and the Amazon East (CDA CORPOAMAZONIA) in English), Corporation for Sustainable Development of South of the Amazon (CORPOMAZONIA in Spanish) and the Corporation for the sustainable development of Mocoa area (CORPOMAZONIA in Spanish).

### C.12.3

**Please describe and provide evidence that the Cancun safeguards information was made transparently available to stakeholders**

ECICDBG: Participation and stakeholders involvement

Participation in the process of construction

The construction of the ECICDBG is a continuous process of lifetime of LIF, through a process of participation and early dialogues, led by the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development (MAIS) since 2010, within the framework of preparing the country for the implementation of REDD+. This process opened spaces with actors that have an impact on the conservation of forests in Colombia. These actors include indigenous peoples, Afro-Colombian communities, farmer organizations, organized civil society (NGOs), the productive sector, women's organizations, and the academic community.

This participation process, MAIS as the leader of the formation of the ECICDBG opened dialogue spaces to discuss the importance of forests and the problems associated to deforestation and degradation and identify the main causes and drivers of forest loss. The process also aimed at identifying the benefits that the implementation of LIF has from the perspective of different stakeholders. Additionally, the process contributed to the identification of capacity-building needs for each stakeholder group in order to effectively participate in the design and implementation of LIFs.

The actors identified and evaluated the risks and benefits of the implementation of actions to reduce deforestation and forest degradation as well as the potential impacts on forest conservation. Between 2010 and 2013, MAIS held 191 workshops with the participation of more than 5369 people.


---
**Page 36**
---

In 2015, participation guidelines were implemented with the different actors of national, regional and local order with the support of cooperation programs for REDD + such as the UN-REDD Program, the FCPF - World Bank, and the GEF Forest Carbon Partnership Facility (FCPF). These guidelines aimed to improve the capacity of indigenous peoples and other affected communities to participate in decision-making processes related to climate change. They also sought to increase their access to information on key stakeholders about global and national discussions on climate change and REDD+, generating new knowledge, experiences and lessons learned from these processes. The process was initiated with the participation of more than 1650 people mainly from the Amazon and Pacific regions.

The described process resulted in the improvement of stakeholders' access and understanding of available information to face deforestation and its implications on climate change and generated greater awareness of their role in implementing actions that can reduce emissions. The process also strengthened the capacity for dialogue between organizational processes and articulation instances between indigenous and afro-descendant organizations and the National Government, as well as with other national and international actors.

The Amazonian Indigenous Board for Environment and Climate Change (MAIACC, Spanish) is one example of the space created by this process. The MAIACC was created in 2015 to establish a dialogue channel between the national government and the indigenous peoples. The MAIACC was created with the purpose of socializing and providing feedback on the National Climate Change System (SICLIMA) and the National Climate Change Plan (PNCC). This process has led to deforestation, deforestation and climate change. Other example is the strengthening of indigenous national organizations (e.g., Confederación de Organizaciones Indígenas del Ecuador - COIE in Spanish), as well as the creation of the Permanent Delegation with the Pueblos y Organizaciones Indígenas), which has improved capacities for information management, participation and understanding of national and international regulations related to climate change and forests.

With respect to institutional actions, the ECOBIDG formation process promoted construction spaces (workshops, exchanges, etc.) that allowed indigenous peoples and other affected communities to participate in processes aimed at defining work plans and identify action lines to reduce deforestation in their territories. As part of ECOBIDG capacity building activities, training was provided on climate change issues for indigenous peoples organizations, as well as for afro-descendant and indigenous communities, as well as other national and regional institutions. Moreover, the training programs were developed with the support of the National Government, through the Ministry of Environment (MINAM) - a Colombian public institution focused on professional formation, a government initiative to develop education and formal employment- to reach hundreds of apprentices throughout the country.

In 2016, Colombia organized a national workshop for indigenous peoples' organizations to coordinate climate change actions. The National Climate Change System (SICLIMA) and the National Climate Change Zones (NRCZ) in addition to government to support national, regional and local authorities.

Regarding the national interpretation of safeguards, the National Government socialized in different spaces of participation with indigenous peoples, as part of the process led by MAIS since 2009. For example Colombia organized a panel and a working session on the national approach to safeguards in the X Congress of Latin American and Caribbean Indigenous Peoples (X Congreso de Pueblos Indígenas de América Latina y el Caribe) with the participation of delegates from indigenous peoples organizations, afro-descendant communities and experts from Brazil, Bolivia and Peru.

As part of the participation process with indigenous peoples, the National Government organized a National Indigenous Safeguards Workshop on July 16, 2016 with delegates from the Permanent Board of Indigenous Peoples (MPCI in Spanish), the National Organization of Indigenous Peoples of Colombia (ONIC in Spanish), the Colombian Amazonian National Organization of Indigenous Peoples of the Colombian Amazon (ONICAP in Spanish), Territorial Council of Indigenous Peoples of the Pacific Region (COTIPAC in Spanish) and the National Government. Additionally, the Government socialized the safeguards in the ECOBIDG capacity building activities with indigenous peoples organizations and communities in the Pacific region (Choco, North of Caucu, Cost of Cauca and Narino) during 2016.
