# REDD-plus RBP Funding Proposal

## Stakeholders' Involvement in the Implementation EICEDG

At the regional level it is important to mention the operation of Forest Development Boards as an instrument of inter-sectoral coordination and dialogue between the MADIS and IEDAM carried out to establish a technical proposal for institutional arrangements relevant to MRV. Finally, it is important to highlight the efforts of the MADIS to address the need to strengthen reference communication channels and spaces with other sectors such as agriculture, transportation, mines and defense.

The involvement of the aforementioned stakeholders is essential for the EICEDG implementation process in an integral way, recognizing their role in the conservation and sustainable use of forests, including indigenous peoples, people-also-descendants-and-the-farmer population and promoting participation of the civil society reserve networks, and forest communities in the design and implementation of the project.

Based on this, actions planned under the EICEDG will seek to maximize environmental and social benefits for indigenous peoples, local communities and other stakeholders conserving the conservation, restoration and sustainable forests management efforts already made by these stakeholders.

The EICEDG also recognizes the important role of the private sector in the sustainable management of forests. Therefore the MADIS in close coordination with other relevant Ministries has led discussion spaces with sectoral organizations (e.g., Cámara de Comercio, Asociación Colombiana de Productores Forestales, Agroforestry agreements (seed, milk, oil and wood production) and private sector commitment to reduce deforestation. Additionally, the Government of Colombia has signed a Memorandum of Understanding with the World Bank in order to collaborate schemes to reduce deforestation associated to commodities supply chains. Thus the EICEDG will promote market-based mechanisms such as carbon credits, payments for environmental services (PES), and other non-timber forest products and the ecosystem. Similarly, the private sector will be involved in other national initiatives that support sustainable forest conservation processes, such as BancoCredito Payment for Environmental Services, and No Cause of Carbon Tax 133, etc.

REM - Amazon Vision Program

### Stakeholder Engagement

Framed in the country's commitment to developing a National Subsystem for REDD+ in Colombia, and complying with the Colombian Forest Law (Do no harm), the REM Amazon Vision Program has developed an Integral Management of Socio-environmental Risk (GIRSA), which is applicable to each of the Pilots and projects.

Under this system, each of the investments are reviewed and assessed applying a methodology created to evaluate implementation of safeguards. The GIRSA includes the following components: Environmental Impact Assessment (EIA) annual reports and national safeguards information reports. All the reports are available at the AVP web site: http://www.amazonvisionprogram.co/. The annual report 2015 is available for public consultation at the documentation module in this link: http://www.amazonvisionprogram.co/annual-report-2015

For local activities the GIRSA established obligations to the implementing entities of the REM-AVP, which includes Patrimonio Natural Fund (FPN) as the financial mechanism. FPN was selected by the Government of Colombia as the entity responsible for the implementation and management of the funds destined to the financing of the projects of the REM-AVP, including the PVA. FPN subscribes specific co-execution agreements or sub-agreements with other implementing entities such as: Corporación de Desarrollo Agropecuario (CDA), Corporación de Desarrollo Forestal (CDF), Corpocauca, Corimacara, CTA, ONIF, Andina DIF and Finagro, as well as indigenous organizations or NGOs implementing projects in the Amazon region. The FPN is responsible for the implementation of the REM-AVP and has responsibilities to perform the methodology and apply the tools and reporting mechanisms for the implementation of the safeguards. In this sense, the FPN will be responsible for the execution of the co-execution agreements and contracts. Agreements subscribed by the REM-AVP are of the public access in this link: http://www.amazonvisionprogram.co/contracts

The planning and processing of GIRSA is fundamental to ensure the accomplishment of safeguards. In this sense the REM-AVP has created a set of tools that allows identifying, documenting and publishing the safeguard measures to be included in the project instruments. The process starts with the identification of the risks based on these the disbursement investment planning cycle (PID) is elaborated and analyzed for the compliance with the safeguards. This process also includes the identification of the actors involved in the project, including the participation of focal points, implementing and strategic partners to identify environmental and social risks and define the safeguard measures to be involved in the planning instruments. This process is documented in a standard


---
**Page 38**
---

form that summarizes risks and prioritized safeguard measures, which is included as an annex in the PIP and updated for public access in the REDD+ RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL web page.

At the project level, the leader of each Pillar jointly with the rural and indigenous communities benefited by the Program, leads the analysis of compliance with the legal frameworks and agrees on a set of measures that need to be executed by the Project. These measures are agreed upon at the start-up phase of the project and must be approved by the selection committee and prior to signing the sub-agreement for its implementation. During the project's start-up phase, the implementation team will conduct an analysis of the potential environmental, social, economic, cultural, health, safety, and social and environmental risks and the prioritization of the safeguard measures. The outcomes from this participatory analysis will be included in the Co-execution Agreement or sub-agreement of each project. This information will also be included in the Co-execution Agreement or sub-agreement of each project. Projects made by each project in the application phase, the PIP document is updated and approved by the Project Leader and the National Secretariat - that is the Colombian Government reports under the UNFCCC, which are also updated in the PIP web page.

Indigenous participants (Pilar 4 - PIVA in Spanish): Pillar IV of the PIPA establishes a participatory indigenous governmental process respecting traditional practices, guaranteeing cultural diversity and conserving forests in their territories. This Pillar recognizes the fundamental role of Indigenous Peoples in the conservation of forests, values their knowledge systems as a fundamental tool for understanding the territory and its resources, and promotes the participation of Indigenous Peoples in decision-making processes related to the processes of construction, definition and implementation of specific policies and actions. Applying the Colombian constitution, the PIPA establishes the principles that guide the participatory process of indigenous peoples and supports specific instances and mechanisms that have been built to promote and guarantee the participation of Indigenous Peoples in the REDD+ Program. The PIVA is a participatory governmental process that has been developed by indigenous organizations in various topics that were defined in close coordination with the indigenous authorities and peoples. This topics include: own governance, land rights, territorial management, natural resources, health and family, health and traditional medicine, own education, support for strengthening languages among others.

Activities and priorities of the PIVA were defined in a participatory process agreed and developed jointly with the Amazonian Indigenous Peoples at the national level under the National Secretariat of Environment and Sustainable Development of the Amazon. The National Organization of Indigenous Peoples of the Colombian Amazon (DIPAC in Spanish) and the National Association of Indigenous Women of the Colombian Amazon (ANIMACO in Spanish), are the main representatives of Amazonian Indigenous Peoples at the national level. Under the MRA and with the purpose of building the foundations of the REDD+ Program, the PIVA was developed by the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development on Board on Environment and Climate Change (Mesa Indígena Amazónica Ambiental y de Cambio Climático - MIACC).

Results of workshops and other participatory activities resulted in a proposal of structure, scope and activities for the implementation of the PIPA. The first meeting of the PIVA was held in Bogotá on 18th June 2015. In 2016, the MIACC approved the preliminary structure of the PIVA document and provided recommendations on technical lines, sub-project selection criteria, map of actors and its priorities for each one of the six departments of the Amazon. The final version of the PIPA was developed in a participatory process with the participation of indigenous organizations in coordination with IP organizations and MIACC representatives. Table 6 summarizes the milestones of this participatory process.

## Colombian Environmental Information System – SIAC

The Colombian Environmental Information System (SIAC) is conceived as the integral system of advice, policies, processes, methodologies, tools and standards for the generation of environmental information in the country. To facilitate the generation of knowledge, decision-making, education and social participation for sustainable development. The SIAC aims to provide a comprehensive set of services that allow the generation of environmental information through consultation process, led by the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development (MADS) and the Environmental Research Institute (IRENA). The system is organized in three main areas: data management, dissemination and communication platforms. SIAC integrates platforms in five main areas: forest natural ecosystems, water, air, biodiversity, soil and human beings socio-economy. SIAC also offers the platform to articulate information related to forest monitoring and the sustainable use of forests.

- Forests management: Information on forest, timber and non-timber products (exploitation, mobilization, seizures, production and protection plantations, timber referrals from commercial or production plantations and free forests), as well as the corresponding data and documentation. The platform is accessible at http://hired.idealn.gov.co/, which is the set of processes, methodologies, protocols and tools to integrate and standardize information on forest resources in Colombia. This platform allows the generation, storage, transmission and consultation of data, databases, statistics and documentary material. In order to guarantee the efficient, timely and quality management of the information provided by SIAC, it offers a series of services such as: training, technical support, etc.

- Deforestation: The platform of the Forest and Carbon Monitoring System (http://sintec.idempiere.co.gov) platform offers information on deforestation in Colombia. This platform is based on the data collected from the census of 2013 of IDEMPIERE defines beforehand warnings for public access, which have been reported quarterly since then.

- Hotspots monitoring: This platform complements the forest monitoring system since provide information of the dynamic changes that occur in the territory and the areas where deforestation is taking place. The system uses tools to decide to guide strategic intervention actions in the territory and reduce the impact of environmental degradation processes. Information is accessible at the platform http://portaldeforest.idempiere.co.gov.

## C2. Use of proceeds and non-carbon benefits

### C2.1 General description

Following the guidelines, the funds will be reinvested in activities consistent with the country's NDC national REDD-plus strategy and/or low carbon development plans and policies. The description should also include how the proceeds will be used in a manner that contributes to the long-term sustainability of REDD-plus activities, including non-carbon benefits.

The proceeds of the RBP will be used in a consistently with the provisions of paragraph 14 of the terms of reference for the REDD+ HSP Pilot Program adopted by Decision 18/07. As set out in this section the proceeds will be invested in supporting the active items of the ECGSBI (National REDD+ Strategy) and consistently with the objectives of the GCF.

### National Context

Since 2015, as part of the provisions of the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC), the National Process for the Reduction of Emissions derived from Deforestation and Degradation (REDD+) was launched with the support of the GCF and other international cooperation partners (including Gordon & Betty Moore Foundation, GIZ, FCPF, UN-REDD program and others).


---
**Page 40**
---

As a result of this process the National Government formulated the Forest Management and Interal Deforestation Control Strategy (Estrategia Integral de Control a la Deforestación y Gestión de los Bosques-Bosquejos Territorios de Vida) or EICDBG, which is part of the National Strategic Plan for Sustainable Development. The strategy was founded on the multi-sector co-responsibility of the Colombian State. The purpose of the EICDBG is to curb deforestation and forest degradation in Colombia by 2030, with a view to preserving the socio-cultural, economic and environmental importance of forest ecosystems, as well as their potential as a development alternative for rural communities.

This public policy document establishes the actions required to reduce deforestation, forest degradation and associated GHG emissions. through an integrated approach to managing forests as the country's development alternative. The objective is to achieve the national goal of reducing deforestation by 40% in Colombia by 2030, with a view to improving the socio-cultural, economic and environmental importance of forest ecosystems, as well as their potential as a development alternative for rural communities.

The EICDBG establishes the following Five Action Lines to reduce deforestation, forest degradation and associated GHG emissions, through an integrated view of the forests and their role in the country's development: (1) Socio-cultural management of indigenous territories and other protected areas; (2) Strengthening the territorial governance frontier; (3) Cross-sector management of territorial planning and of environmental determinants; (4) Permanent monitoring of forest cover, deforestation and forest degradation; (5) Institutional, regulatory and financial adjustments that provide the State with the necessary instruments for forest conservation and sustainable development.

The Action Line (1) encompasses a group of measures and specific actions needed to achieve the objective of each AL. The EICDBG achieves a complete reduction in deforestation by the year 2030, with forest management evolving into a Sustainable Development Strategy that contributes to achieving the national goal of reducing deforestation by 40% in Colombia by 2030, while improving the well-being and livelihoods of local communities and the society in general, while promoting the conservation of biodiversity and the ecosystem services, including adaptation and mitigation to climate change. Specifically, by the year 2030:

* Have a positive trade balance of the forestry sector.
* Have zero gross deforestation.
* Have reduced emissions of CO2eq from avoided deforestation.
* Have strengthened the territorial governance of ethnic groups, rural and urban communities that live and depend on forests.
* Have increased the quality of life of the local population in forest areas.

In line with the EICDBG and considering reduction of deforestation as a means to achieve its national mitigation goal of 4.6 Tons CO2eq per year by 2030, Colombia has committed itself to reducing gross deforestation in the country and preserve important ecosystems such as the Amazon, given its enormous potential of contributing to stabilize GHG emissions. The EICDBG is aligned with the National Climate Change Strategy (NDC) for 2015 – 2030, which sets a target of 647,000 hectares, which corresponds to the historical average of the period 1990 – 2015 plus a 15% of national circumstances associated to a post-conflict scenario.

The incorporation of the EICGDB is included in the National Development Plan 2018-2022, specifically within the chapter "The Past for Sustainability: produce conserving and conservation of natural resources" with four main areas of action:

1. Implement strategic and economic instruments for the productive sectors to be more sustainable.
2. Strengthen the capacity of local communities, indigenous peoples, and authorities in forest management, deforestation and environmental offenses based on territorial control and new sustainable economic opportunities at the local level.
3. Promote participatory processes that allow the inclusion of local communities in decision-making on the territory and,
4. Strengthen environmental institutions, research and public sector line in dialogue and consultation with civil society.

Within its strategies and instruments, the NDP considers actions to close and stabilize the agriculture frontier[1], improve environmental zoning and implement a Community-Based Forest Policy. These instruments would be mainly focused on areas of high deforestation risk (AHP) and areas where forest cover has been lost or degraded in higher incidence (POET municipalities). Also the NDP includes specific responsibilities to the Ministries of Environment, Agriculture, Livestock, and Rural Development, as well as the National Forest Service[2], consolidating financial mechanisms and promoting sustainable forests enterprises involving schemes for community use of forests, plantations with native species and value chains. (ii) development of a forestry research agenda including a commitment to promote the implementation of forest-based products in the productive sector, especially for business plans, and (iii) build a strategy to guarantee the integrity of the productive value chain of legal timber and its derivatives.

The NDP defined specific activities to strengthen capacities for governance, financial management and administration of national funds for development, recognizing that indigenous authorities, afro-descendant communities and local smallholder farmers are key actors in forest conservation. The NDP also includes a commitment to take into account that indigenous territories represent important areas of forest and have a traditional experience in agrobiodiversity. Specifically, the NDP includes specific responsibilities to the Ministry of Environment, Agriculture, Livestock and rural development, as well as the National Forest Service[2], consolidating financial mechanisms and promoting sustainable forests enterprises involving schemes for community use of forests, plantations with native species and value chains.

Specifically, the NDP includes specific responsibilities to the Ministry of Environment, Agriculture, Livestock and rural development, as well as the National Forest Service[2], consolidating financial mechanisms and promoting sustainable forests enterprises involving schemes for community use of forests, plantations with native species and value chains. Specific activities include: (i) strengthening the capacity of indigenous organizations and authorities for the conservation and sustainable management of forests and other strategic ecosystems; (ii) supporting the implementation of forest-based products in the productive sector, especially for business plans, and (iii) build a strategy to guarantee the integrity of the productive value chain of legal timber and its derivatives.

To achieve the objectives and action lines of the EICGDB, the strategies of the NDP and the NCDS Colombia is implementing the AVP, an umbrella program formulated by the national government that seeks to reduce emissions from deforestation in the Colombian Amazon. As described in previous sections, the AVP promote a sustainable development model based on the conservation of natural resources and the reduction of poverty, while empowering local communities and indigenous peoples and generating zero-deforestation productive alternatives. If successful, this program will contribute to the achievement of the EICGDB objectives for Colombia accompanied by substantial co-benefits in the form of improved smallholder farmer livelihoods, biodiversity conservation, and climate change mitigation.

At the regional level, the AVP represents the prime mechanism to support regional implementation activities to reduce deforestation in the Amazon basin and constitutes an umbrella for the operation of other international cooperation projects that are being implemented by different actors. The main objectives of these projects are presented in the 4. These projects have made important investments in strengthening the management effectiveness of protected areas, promoting the participation of local communities, indigenous peoples and authorities, support the national forest monitoring system, promote sectoral programs to involve private companies in sustainable land-use and natural resources management that contribute to reduce pressure on forests and reduce deforestation. Examples of these projects have supported the achievement of results proposed in the EICGDB and provided the country with national capacities, instruments and tools.

## Projects under the umbrella of the Colombian Amazon Vision Program

Specifically, the AVP REM project is the first Results-Based Payments scheme that is being implemented with the objective of inducing betterestation in the Amazon Region. The strategy of the REM AVP considers five areas of work as presented in the Box 1.

| BOX 1: Short Description of the Pillars of the Amazon Vision Program – REM Project |
| --- |
| **Pillar 1:** Strengthening the institutional strengthening of management of forest resources and effective land use planning. |
| **Pillar 2:** Sustainable sectoral planning and development. It is oriented to articulate processes with the transformation of the economy, in order to reduce its impact on forests and land and development and planning. |
| **Pillar 3:** Forest-based economic diversification. It addresses the direct causes of deforestation through two strategies that include (i) zero net deforestation agreements with local associations and (ii) the promotion of environmental services and carbon credits. |
| **Pillar 4:** Environmental governance with indigenous peoples: it seeks to strengthen indigenous communities' capacity for territorial governance and their traditions, knowledge and sustainable productive practices. |
| **Pillar 5:** Enabling conditions: this transversal pillar aims to develop a set of activities that facilitate the implementation of the other four, including the strengthening of the SMBCI and IMF. |

**Description on the use of GCF proceeds, by Output and activities**

**Output 1. National and local capacities for monitoring and control strengthened**

Under Output 1, the project will use GCF funds to support the implementation of the Actions Line 4 and 5 of the ESDCOSA. These actions aim at strengthening national and regional forest information systems with consistent, timely and quality information on the status, pressure and dynamics of the forests, as support for decision-making processes at national, regional and local levels in forest management systems. The contribution will be made to National Forest Authorities, responsible for administration of forest resources and follow up on the application of social and environmental safeguards.

The Action Line 5: Generation and strengthening of legal, institutional and financial capacities for sustainable forest management and control of deforestation in Colombia. This action aims at supporting the national forest monitoring system (SINIFOR) to strengthen its capacity as a key instrument for forest management and the effective reduction and control of deforestation in Colombia.

Activities will complement those being implemented under the Pillars 2 and 5 of the AVP. Pillar 2 aims to support implementation of the National Forest Monitoring System (SINIFOR), which is the main national information system, as well as supporting instruments to help prevent. Pillar 5 has the objective of monitoring deforestation and carbon stocks in the Amazon, supporting the development of a national forest inventory for REDD+ and the generation of information to support making decisions processes, control deforestation, report results and monitor conservation efforts.

Specifically, the REM-AVR has supported the regional environmental authorities to increase their capacities for monitoring forests in their jurisdictions, having supported the development of seventeen national deforestation early warning systems (SINIFOR) and the strengthening of the SINIFOR system with more frequent and accurate data. Also the program has supported the National Government to strengthen its capacities for regional planning and providing technical assistance in need of deforestation and manage the national forest and carbon monitoring system (SYMBICAR).

**Activity 1.1 Strengthening of the existing national forest monitoring of early warning reports in deforestation hotspots**

GCF funds will be used to support the national forest monitoring system led by IEDAE to generate reports and more frequent early warnings in deforestation hotspots of the amazon region, according to the implementation plan defined for Mexico. The project will also support the development of a regional early warning system (SINIFOR) that will support local governments, regional environmental authorities, indigenous peoples organizations and resguardos, village councils and other stakeholders in the Amazon region to monitor deforestation hotspots and actively participate in the definition of strategies and initiatives to reduce deforestation.

Taking into account that activities under Output 2 will be focused in promoting a forest economic investments under Output 1.2, this activity will support the implementation of the SINIFOR system for managed forests. This activity will support the SMMBC by improving methodologies for monitoring carbon stocks in managed forests.

**Activity 1.2 Support the implementation of a strategy for Green Municipalities**

As indicated above, the project will also support the design and implementation of a national government and the AVP. GCF funds will support the design and implementation of a green municipalities program targeted to those municipalities with high deforestation risk and need support to strengthen their capacities in forest management to reduce deforestation. The REM-AVR has made progress on the conceptualization of incentives for green

### Activity 1.3 Support the implementation of rural environmental cadasters as a measure to monitor deforestation at farm level

As identified in the ECOGCB, land tenure conflicts are a relevant deforestation driver. Measures 3.2 and 5.1 of the ECOGCB aim at addressing this issue by addressing it as a way to reduce deforestation rates. The project will also provide solutions to land tenure conflicts and implement regulations to include climate change and zero deforestation in the National Land Agenda for the definition of its strategy of regularization of rural property in the Amazon, which will benefit 500 thousand families. This measure will be implemented through strengthening of the capacity of local governments and zero-deforestation commitments by landholders by promoting rural environmental cadasters for small landholders. This instrument is a tool that allows the registration of land tenure rights and provides information on the use of land in agroforestry and reforestation of degraded areas. Investments of the project will include strengthening of the National Forest Monitoring System (SINIFOR) to allow the implementation of rural environmental cadasters, as well as support for rural environmental cadaster, taking into account existing national initiatives such as the National Multipurpose Cadastre.

### Activity 1.4 Implementation of the Deforestation Control Protocol (monitoring, forest traceability, operational and administrative)- including the reinforcement of deforestation control actions and other associated crimes in the municipalities with higher deforestation rates. # REDD-plus RFB Funding Proposal

## Output 2: Forest areas sustainably managed and contributing to close the agriculture frontier

GCF funds will support the implementation of the Action Line 2 of the ECOBAG (Development of a forest economy and closure of the agricultural frontier) in Colombia. The objective is to contribute to the sustainable management of forest goods and services in order to boost integrated rural development and close the agricultural frontier (Res 261/2018).

Taking into account the contribution of a forest economy to reduce emissions, mitigate climate change and support the sustainable use of natural resources, the project will promote the establishment of community-based forest units through a community-based forestry program targeted to design and establish at least six community-based forestry units (60,000 ha). A Community-Based Forest Unit will be considered as a unit managed by a local association or indigenous organization that manages forests in an area of 10,000 hectares. The forest units will consist of several species of timber and non-timber forest products and agriculture produce derived from the transformation of existing conventional agricultural systems into agroforestry systems. The project will promote the establishment of a landscape management approach that integrates forest management and agriculture systems, and provides other services to rural communities.

As the forestry units are conceived as a tool to stop deforestation in areas where there are some risks of expansion of the agriculture frontier, it is expected that forest management activities be complemented with forest conservation agreements for natural forest surrounding areas, which could modify the impact of forestry units in terms of avoided deforestation.

It is expected that labor force to develop forest management activities come from the local communities benefited by the Project. The project will promote the establishment of a network of community-based forest units through producers associations, Communal Action Groups (CAG), indigenous peoples organizations or other community-originated organizations. The project will also support the development of a system for the sustainable use of resources to those areas where community organizations are in place; however, based on national experiences it is expected that the project will not be able to reach all the communities interested in forest management activities due to the lack of sustainable forest management in areas that contribute to closing the agriculture frontier. Activities of design and implementation of forest management plans will be carried out in close coordination with regional environmental authorities, which are responsible of granting forest management permits and provide specialized guidance to those persons interested in managing natural resources in their areas of jurisdiction.

This output will support community-based forestry units in line with the Pillar 1 of the M-REVM+ Improving Forest Governance and Management. The project will promote the development of a system for the sustainable use of forest environmental practices. The Project will build on the previous efforts of Pillar 1 to promote the sustainable forest management through the elaboration of the Planes de Ordenamiento Forestal (POF) in the areas where the Piller's goals is to support the regional environmental authorities in the elaboration of "Planes de Ordenamiento Forestal" (POFs). The POFs are studies that are elaborated by the Regional Environmental Authorities (CARs) to ensure that those persons interested in managing natural resources have a clear vision on the conservation, protection and sustainable use of the resource. CARs are responsible of the elaboration of the POF based on description of the basic, social, economic and aspects in areas under their jurisdiction.

### References

1. **Forest Management Units**: Areas managed by local associations or indigenous organizations.
2. **Community-Based Forest Units**: Managed by a local association or indigenous organization covering 10,000 hectares.
3. **POFs (Planes de Ordenamiento Forestal)**: Studies developed by Regional Environmental Authorities to guide forest management practices.

### Notes

- Forest units will cover approximately 60,000 hectares in total.
- The project aims to establish at least six community-based forestry units.
- Labor force for forest management activities is expected to come from local communities.

The project will start operation during 2020. The Pillar also seeks to leverage centers for the transformation of timber and non-timber products that allow a more dynamic forestry sector, seeing this as an opportunity to add economic value to timber and non-timber forest products. The REM-AVP+ also has designed a capacity building strategy for local communities in the design and establishment of sustainable forest management units.

The project area covers 2,834 families and covering 96,925. In this context, activities of the Project will also be aligned with investments of the PNP II, which is focused on the development of a market-based approach to the conservation of natural forests and has been supporting sustainable supply chains of cocoa, rubber, milk, beef and amazon fruits and funding projects that are contributing to the reduction of deforestation.

Based on the preliminary results of the REM-AVP+, GCF investments will be focalized to support the establishment of community-based forest units, prioritizing those located in areas where PNP II are formulating. Also the program will consider the possibility of supporting the development of market-based approaches for the conservation of natural agreements or located in POET municipalities with little investment from other projects being implemented under the AVP umbrella.

Criteria to identify intervention areas will be agreed upon with the MADS taking into account intervention areas of the AVP, other development priorities and the following criteria that have been used for supporting forestry units in other areas of Colombia:

1. Areas of high deforestation, with high deforestation and/or socio-environmental conflicts.
2. Areas with high potential of generating benefits for avoided deforestation.
3. Communities interested in forest management that require sustainable production alternatives to avoid control deforestation.
4. Communities' interests in forest management with unsatisfied basic needs (e.g. food security).
5. Existence of existing community forest management units.
6. Sites/communities where there is informality or unfair trade of timber products.
7. Areas with high potential for illegal logging.
8. Regional Autonomous Corporations with interest in promoting sustainable community forest management.
9. Protected areas and other conservation areas (e.g. Reserva Natural de la Sierra Nevada del Cocuy, Áreas Forestales de Leyes 2 daa de 1959). In this case, the Association figure can be implemented to access the use rights of these areas, especially in the case of indigenous communities.

GCF funds will also be used to increase market access and identify potential incentives for sustainable forest management building on previous efforts. The REM-AVP+ is implementing an incentive for forest conservation in order to support the project being part of sustainable forest management units. The project will evaluate this instrument and identify other complementary incentives according to the characteristics of each Community Based Forest Unit.

Activity 2.1 | Support local community-based organizations in the design and establishment of sustainable forest management units

GCF funds will be used to support local associations in the design of community-based forestry units including identification of timber and non-timber forest products with market potential as well as other income generation activities (e.g., a sustainable supply chain for cocoa, rubber, milk, beef and amazon fruits). The project will also develop market strategies for each unit, involving participatory methodologies and training to create local capacities for the operation of these units. These units may include timber harvesting, non-timber forest products with market potential and non-timber forest products (e.g., acacia, canagucha, palms leaves for handicrafts and construction), sustainable agriculture activities, and other income generating activities. Sustainable forestry activities will be complemented with forest conservation agreements that will cover natural surrounding areas.

Forestry units may include agricultural areas, taking into account that currently livelihoods of farmers living close to the agriculture frontier are based on cattle ranching and other extractive activities with low value added; not sustainable


---
**Page 50**
---

The project will build on existing learning experiences resulting from the implementation of the Project “Technical Assistance Model for the Implementation of the Community Forestry model in Colombia” which is being implemented in several areas of the country (e.g., departments of Antioquia, Cauca, Valle del Cauca, Tolima, Bolívar, Huila, Putumayo), and that has been operational (both government and general operation). According to this, the establishment of forestry units includes:

a) Use of timber and non-timber forest products.

b) Restoration actions for species conservation, use and management.

c) Enrichment actions of native species in degraded natural forests.

d) Natural regeneration processes.

e) Participatory community monitoring and control and surveillance actions.

Under this activity, GCF funds will also support local associations in the elaboration of integrated management plans for each forestry unit to ensure sustainable management of species and habitats. These plans will include forest inventories, land use planning, and other relevant information.

Business plans and financial analysis will guide operation of the forestry unit in a term of 25 years, identifying the risks and strategies to ensure financial, social and environmental sustainability of the forest management activity. These instruments will also be used for the identification of the necessary resources (human, material, financial) and access mechanisms and others needed to make feasible the business in the long term.

Activity 2.2 Implementation of training programs for sustainable forest management GCF funds will support the implementation of training programs that will train local communities and representatives of local community-based organizations for sustainable forest management. Training programs will include those aspects that are key for long-term sustainability, such as: the identification of timber species and their characteristics; the use and marketing of forest products; monitoring activities and traceability; financial management; entrepreneurial capacities; market access and other business opportunities; and others. In addition to training local communities in these areas, they will develop capacities and transfer learning experienced to other forest areas. GCF resources will also be used for promoting learning experiences with other actors involved in the sector (e.g., private sector, government agencies, research and management projects, and other more experienced associations or projects). Implementation of training programs will involve collaboration between local communities, national and regional environmental authorities, national and regional universities and the National Service of Learning (SENAL).

Activity 2.3 Market access and strengthening of product value chains of timber and non-timber forest products and other sustainable products derived from forestry units GCF funds will support the identification of market opportunities for the products and services provided by the forestry units and support stakeholders along the supply chain in improving their capacities to meet these market demands. This will include: the development of marketing strategies; the identification of market linkages and other activities to incentivize private sector investments beyond the project lifetime. Special consideration should be given to the creation of value chains for timber products (e.g., establishment of native species nurseries, harvesting, monitoring, etc.). Implementation of market strategies will include participation of the National Secretariat of Environment and the National Environmental Authorities, as well as the Ministries of Agriculture and Commerce.

Supply chains and forestry units will be supported through the application of the methodology Market Analysis and Development (MAD). This tool is a participatory process that involves local communities. The objective is to connect communities to a process of identification of promising products and services that could be derived from their forests, and support them in developing business plans for these activities. In addition, this methodology will assess the feasibility of the management of identified products and services such as timber and non-timber forest products.

### REDD-plus RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL

#### GREEN CLIMATE FUND | PAGE 48 OF 104

**Activity 2.4 Strengthening regional environmental authorities in the implementation of monitoring and control mechanisms and instruments to assure sustainable forest management in selected forestry units**

Building on the work already done under Activity 2.3, this activity will support the project in the formulation of a capacity building program, to strengthen regional environmental authorities in the implementation of monitoring and control mechanisms and instruments for sustainable forest management.

The activities will be aligned with the efforts for the establishment of the eight community-based forestry units in such a way that procedural and institutional systems that protect sustainable forest management are improved and adjusted to the need of the forestry units.

**Activity 2.5 Design of incentives and financial instruments to promote sustainable forest management investments**

Investment in sustainable forest management is critical to the success of the project, attracting other international cooperation or private sector funds and providing funding for future communities interested in establishing Community-Based Forestry Units. Incentives will be designed based on experiences of national institutions such as the Forest Stewardship Council (FSC) and other organizations implementing REDD+ projects such as Carbon Unit AfOLU (CUA), which is currently supporting 19 local communities in the Peruvian Amazon.

The design of incentives will take into account the need to ensure that the project’s activities are aligned with the national policy framework, and the design of mechanisms for assuring mid-term financial sustainability of the forestry units established under Activity 2.1.

**Activity 2.6 Implementation of long-term community-based monitoring systems in forestry units**

The implementation of a long-term community-based monitoring system is an essential approach to preventing negative effects of forest management on the natural resources of the managed forests and the surrounding ecosystems. The design of this system will be based on lessons learned from other projects, and will include specific science-based methodologies for assessing and monitoring managed species and evaluate the impact of forest management activities.

The implementation of a long-term community-based monitoring system in forestry units will follow forest stewardship and traceability criteria in such a way that communities benefited by the project will have the necessary tools to accessing forest certification in the near future.

**Output 3. Territorial governance and capacities of indigenous peoples strengthened for forest management and conservation**

The NDP recognizes the importance of indigenous territories as a strategy to reduce deforestation, therefore, this output aims at strengthening territorial governance and the capacity of indigenous populations to promote restoration in indigenous territories, advance an update assessments to identify the causes, agents and impacts of deforestation and facilitate the implementation of climate change adaptation and mitigation actions in the context of the ESGCBA.

In line with this mandate, Measures 12.3, 13.1.4 and 1.5 of the Action Line 1 of the ESGCBA and the progress made by the AVP, this output aims to consolidate indigenous peoples’ governance capacities by supporting subprojects formulated in collaboration with indigenous peoples’ organizations and communities that are recognized as support to subjects that contribute to the creation of a forest economy as an instrument to improve local governance, knowledge management and capacity building.

Activities of this output will be aligned to the procedures of Pilot 4 of the AVP: Environmental governance with indigenous peoples (PIVA). As described previously, these procedures applicable to the projects implemented under the umbrella of the Ministry of Environment authorities (MAACO), after the AVP followed a consultation process agreed upon and driven by MADG and OPRAC.

**Activity 3.1 Strengthening REDD+ implementation and forest governance in Indigenous Territories**

In line with this mandate, Measure 12.3 of the Action Line 1 of the ESGCBA, this output will support sub-projects targeting indigenous peoples’ organizations and communities in five priority areas:

* **1. Territory and environment**

The selection of sub-projects to be supported will be undertaken through public calls that include the following steps:

- Definition of terms of reference and conditions to support projects.
- Calls for project proposal and dissemination activities.
- Review of proposals.
- Selection and prioritization of projects.
- Support to the execution of selected projects.
- Definition of implementation arrangements.
- Evaluation of results.
- Support to the execution of selected projects and technical support.

According to the previously agreed procedures, activities will be defined in close coordination with IPs organizations and authorities (BIACC) and specific terms of reference and procedures for each public call will be agreed with the IPs authorities. The selection criteria will be based on the capacity of the IPs organizations to implement the project with the beneficiaries. Each route will include legal procedures, strategies for the generation and strengthening of capacities, identification of resources, definition of implementation arrangements, improvement of the proposal and a follow-up-and technical assistance plan for the execution of the project.

In order to complement activities for the support of IPs projects, a specific strategy will be designed to ensure participation in decision-making processes, the selection of IPs as implementers of projects, and define specific activities to provide additional support to projects implemented by women groups.

Piva has developed two public calls and supported 36 projects proposed by indigenous communities. In the first call (PIVA-2014), 5 projects were selected with a total budget of approximately $8 million dollars (6 billion pesos) and were implemented in Amazonas, Putumayo, Caquetá, Guaviare and Vaupés, plus a regional project in Choco. The second public call (PIVA-2017) was launched on 30 November 2017 with commitments of approximately $5.5 million dollars ($18 billion pesos) in Amazonas, Guaviare, Putumayo, Vaupés and Caquetá. The selection process for this call is ongoing (December 2019). According to the information being generated 2020 and expects to invest around 20 billion Colombian pesos, the selection criteria for this last call are:

- Initiatives contributing to the objectives of Vision Amazonia in reducing deforestation, forest conservation and biodiversity.
- Initiatives responding to local situations identified as priorities directly related to genetic problems of the Amazonian people.
- Projects that contribute to existing policies in legally constituted indigenous organizations and its capacity building in communities and their organization.
- Projects that are innovative and/or participatory aimed at promoting sustainability of existing processes.
- Initiatives that have broad territorial and thematic coverage generate efficiency in the implementation and cover the needs of a large number of IPs.
- Initiatives that contemplate strategic alliances with private and public actors, with contribution of technical and financial resources.
- Initiatives that are inclusive and enhance the participation of women and the elderly.
- Initiatives that include actions that strengthen the culture of indigenous peoples.
- Initiatives that contribute to the strengthening of the relationship between IPs and the State
- Implementing entities must meet minimum experience requirements, transparency, capacity and administrative efficiency in order to ensure the quality of the project.

### Activity 3.2 Empowerment and participation of indigenous women

In line with Activity 3.1 GCF resources will be invested in supporting projects that directly impact on indigenous women's wellbeing and empowerment. The focus is on strengthening the role of women as key actors in decision-making processes by women's groups that have great impact in strengthening women participation in sustainable management and conservation activities, including forest management. These activities also include initiatives that support forest conservation and other activities that bear education potential involving youth groups and children.

# REDD-plus RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL

## Benefits distribution and non-carbon benefits

In line with the achievements of Colombia, the proceeds of the results-based payments are expected to be used to contribute to a long-term vision of AVP, especially with the activities that have been evaluated as effective within the framework of the EICDGB, which has been the subject of public consultation. Payments received from GCF will be invested in Output 1 and Output 2 according to the approved project document and the EICDGB. Resources that will be invested in Output 1 will complement activities to assure implementation of monitoring and carbon accounting systems for REDD+ and other forest-related projects, as well as investments in the transition to a forest economy in the region as a measure to close the agriculture frontier according to the EICDGB and contributing to achieving the goals of AVP. Resources that will be invested in Output 2 (which is aligned with the approach taken with indigenous peoples) will support implementation of short-term measures to increase forest governance in indigenous lands and generate local livelihoods and capacities for reducing deforestation and promote sustainable forest management, as well as long-term measures to strengthen the capacity of communities in non-forested areas. Environmental benefits of the project include conservation of biodiversity associated to manage forests and neighboring ecosystems, as well as strengthening the capacity of communities to manage resources through provision of environmental services, protection of genetic resources of forest ecosystems, watershed integral management and monitoring of forest carbon stocks.

A direct environmental benefit derived from the project is the provision of timber, non-timber and sustainable agriculture products that offer additional social and economic benefits. These benefits will improve local livelihoods, strengthen local economies and contribute to the achievement of the goals of AVP. The project also contributes to strengthening governance and management at the national and local levels.

In this context, the benefits distribution is founded in the importance of promoting forestry and strengthening of indigenous peoples' capacity for sustainable forest management as part of the National Plan as actions that could generate multiple benefits at the national and local levels. According to this, proceeds will be distributed taking into account beneficiaries of each of the three project's outputs as shown in the following figure:

The Amazon Biome includes 10 departments (Amazonas, Caquetá, Guaviare, Guainía, Putumayo, Vaupés, Meta, Arauca, Casanare, and Cundinamarca) and at least 25% of the population lives in 370 villages. In this region, 31% of protected areas outlined by the National System of Protected Areas of Colombia are grouped into three categories: two national parks (one of which is a UNESCO World Heritage Site), one sanctuary of Flora and Medicinal Plants, and one Sanctuary of Flora and Wildlife. Investments of the project will benefit:
  - Rural and urban population of 15 municipalities of three Departments that will implement mechanisms to promote green municipal programs.
  - Indigenous peoples in four departments of the Amazon Region (Amazonas, Caquetá, Putumayo, Guaviare, Guainía and Vaupés).
  - Rural communities located in forest areas where Community-Based Forestry Units will be established.

According to the general national census of 2018¹ the Amazon Region has a very low population density of about 2% of the total population. The indigenous population is mostly settled in the department of Caquetá (42% of the total population) and Putumayo (295,286 indigenas over 37% of the total population). The largest indigenous group is the Wayúu people who represent about 10% of the indigenous population. The second largest group are the country people of the ethnic origin in the Amazon, representing 17% of the inhabitants of the region. Vaupés has the largest indigenous population (25%) and Putumayo follows with 14%. The remaining departments have a very small percentage: Guainía (3%), Caquetá (2.2%). With respect to the number of self-recognized indigenas, such as afro-colombianos, raizal and palenqueros people, they represent only about 0.5% of the total population in the region. The largest populations of NARIP (Spanish acronym: afro-colombianos, raizal, and palenquero peoples) are found in Putumayo (18%), Vaupés (17%) and Guainía (16%). The remaining departments have a very small percentage: Amazonas (2.4%) and Caquetá (1.5%). NARIP population for the region is considered a minority in comparison to indigenous population.

Although, the detail of the beneficiaries and the selecting criteria will be identified during the initial phase, it is important to highlight that rural populations in the Amazon located in areas that are part of the Amazon Reserve (Law 2. 1951) are indigenous peoples who have a special regime of management represented by the National Indigenous Organization of Colombia (ONIC). According to the current regulations, forest management activities are not allowed in areas of national natural parks.

However areas that are overlapped with resguardos indigenas have a special regimen of management, represented in agreements between ONIC and the Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources. The indigenous peoples who are involved with GCF funds are locate in areas with this overlapping the project will apply the national regulation and will respect existing agreements with the specific communities.

## Impacts of COVID-19 on and how to respond

The negative impacts of the COVID-19 crisis on the production and trade of forest products, as well as the disruption of value chains and markets, will affect forest services will put important livelihoods at risk, which depend members of forest communities to meet income needs. Food insecurity and a decrease in household incomes tend to increase the reliance of forest communities on forest resources for food security and income generation. This situation can result in overharvesting of natural resources. Following the COVID-19 outbreak, the use of wild plants and other herbal medicines has increased significantly. In order to incorporate this into an opportunity for the implementation of the project, the following actions will be reinforced in the project:

* **Promote long-term and reliable sustainable products and nature based solutions** to counter any short and longer-term impacts on livelihoods, particularly those more vulnerable people in rural communities that depend on income from land use.
* **Design payment schemes considering alternative employment opportunities such as employment for forest restoration and monitoring, expending cash transfer options through a combination of payments for ecosystem services, and facilitate improved access and use of wild/medicinal foods which are readily available.
* **Maximize the potential of existing markets** to ensure that the products generated by the project will ensure that sustainable production is economically viable (i.e. requirements are not so costly that Forest Communities are forced to sell at a loss).
* **Strengthen national and regional markets for forest products**, in order to maintain access to legally harvested wood and non-timber forest products, and to support local communities in the implementation of their projects.

### C.5.6 Implementation modalities

List and describe the institutions involved in the activities that will be funded with resources from this pilot programme, and explain their anticipated roles and interactions with one another, including the flow of funds.

The Government of Colombia, through the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development (MADS) requested to the Green Climate Fund (GCF), through the Financial Advisor (FAA), for technical assistance for the design and implementation of a REDD+ Result Based Payments Project framed in the GCF REDD+ Pilot Program and act as executing entity (EE). The Government of Colombia, through MADS, is also the Lead Party (LP) to leading the formation of this project and will serve both as Accredited Entity (AE) and Executing Entity (EE).

GCF and FAA will enter into a Funding Activity Agreement (FAA), under which FAA shall administer the relevant GCF Proceeds in accordance with the terms set out in the Agreement. The Financial Advisor (FAA) was appointed by MADS entered into the Party on 8 June 2018 and effective as of October 4th, 2018. As Executing Agency, FAA shall spend the GCF Proceeds in the project directly and act to be implementing this under FAO's Direct Implementation modality.

In the case as the EE, FAA may also into enter agreements with other organizations and entities (Implementing Partners) that will carry out projects or activities provided for by the Project. These arrangements are designed to facilitate and enhance the effectiveness of the implementation of the project; therefore these Implementing Partners are accountable directly to EE and will be selected by FAO and the MADS, as per FAO Rules and Regulations.

FAO may use the following instruments when entering into agreements with other partners:

- UN Bi Agreement: used when a need exists of transferring funds from FAO to another UN Agency for the purpose of implementing a project.
- Letter of Agreement: is a contractual arrangement with academic or non-profit organizations to obtain specific technical services (e.g., research, training) in accordance with applicable national regulations.
- Contracts: a contractual arrangement with private institutions to purchase goods or specific technical services (e.g., equipment, software).
- Operational Partner Implementation Modality (OPIM): after mid-term review, the opportunity to amend the implementation modality may be considered. Under OPIM, FAO will not be required to purchase all of the specific activities would be assessed.

As the Green Climate Fund and the FAA shall enter into a FAA in due course, the FAA shall (A) incorporate the relevant terms and conditions of the Terms of Reference for the REDD+ RBP Project Programme adopted by Decision 5/18PT ("TOR"), and (B) the terms of the Accreditation Master Agreement ("AMA"). The arrangements to be entered into by the

## Scheme of the Proposed Implementation Arrangements under the REDD+ RBP Project

The Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development (MADS) is responsible for the general management and technical guidance of the Program and will be responsible for circulating project proposals implemented under the AE and EE. The MADS will not be responsible for executing financial resources as an executing agency, therefore, FAO shall not transfer any funds to the MADS. The MADS will provide oversight and quality assurance through its Headquarters and the Chillean Regional Office. FAO will carry out both operational and administrative functions including advisory and technical support functions during the implementation of the Project.

As AE and EE, FAO Colombia Country Office will carry out operational and administrative support activities which include the provision of the following services:

- Establishment of the Project Management Unit (PMU)
- Payment of project-related expenses
- Recruitment of staff, project personnel, and consultants.
- Procurement of goods and services
- Organization of training activities, conferences, and workshops, including fellowships.

- Travel authorization, visa requests, ticketing, and travel arrangements.
- Shipment of customs clearance, vehicle registration, and accommodation, among others.

**FAO will require certification of the completed Environmental and Social Risk Management Training Module. With the aim of ensuring project staff is in the capacity to identify and evaluate environmental and social risks, as well as promote the improved environmental and social performance of the project.**

Upon receipt of MADS inputs, the Project Board will be through the Direct Implementation modality with FAO providing direct project services, such as procurement and hiring of consultants following best value for money, transparency and effective competition principles. The Project Board will also be responsible for approving all procurements in accordance with request of the Government. FAO will also provide technical backstopping during the implementation of the project. The costs corresponding to this technical support towards project execution will be recovered following FAO’s policy.

Following the structure of the AUP, FAO as Enabling Entity could involve community associations, local institutions, NGOs and other relevant stakeholders in the implementation of activities or sub-projects as well as the development of specific products required by the Project.

## Project Board

The Project Board is the governing body that will provide overall guidance and direction to the project and approve the Annual Work Plan (AWP). The Board is responsible for making management decisions by consensus or majority, including but not limited to: approving the AWP; reviewing and approving project progress reports; and addressing grievances. To ensure FAO’s ultimate accountability, Project Board decisions should be made in accordance with applicable laws, regulations, policies, and procedures of the Government, as well as international best practices for transparency and effective international competition. The specific responsibilities of the Project Board include:

- Provide overall guidance and direction to the project, ensuring it remains within any specified constraints;
- Approve the AWP and other project documents;
- Provide guidance on new project risks, and agree on possible countermeasures and management actions to address them;
- Agree on project manager’s tolerances as required;
- Analyze the annual progress report of project activities and recommend changes as required based on project monitoring and evaluation processes and products and in line with FAO policies;
- Discuss and approve any amendments to the AWP, including budget adjustments;
- Appraise the annual project implementation report, including quality assessment rating reports, and make recommendations for the work plan;
- Provide ad hoc approval for exceptional situations when the project manager’s tolerances are exceeded;
- Discuss and approve the Progress Reports and Final Report of the Project;
- Analyze Project achievements and assess their use for performance improvement, accountability and learning; and
- Settle any disputes originating on any matters within the scope of the project that cannot be resolved by external bodies.

Following the structure of other institutions reporting to the Region, the Board shall also be composed of MADS, IDEAM, Sinchi Rengar, the Project Pillars Unit, the Regional Authorities, and a representative of Indigenous Peoples Organizations. FAO will participate of the Board as its technical secretariat, supported by the Project Manager. FAO will also provide backstopping to the Project Board in accordance with the terms of reference.

The MADS as President of the board may consider the inclusion of other relevant actors such as the Ministry of Agriculture, the Ministry of Environment and Tourism and other Ministries and agencies that are already part of the Intersectoral Committee to Control Deforestation.

## Technical committee

The project will be supported by a Technical Committee (TC) comprised by representatives at a technical level of the same institutions that are part of the Project Board. Sectoral Ministries and National Agencies (DNP, Agriculture, and Tourism) shall also participate in the TC as well as other relevant actors from the private sector and civil society in the region. The Project Team, the leaders of the AUP Pillars and relevant technical agencies could also be invited. The TC will provide more regular periodic guidance to the implementation of the Project Outputs assuming articulation with existing policies and strategies at national level. The TC will also advise FAO and the Project Board about the planned activities to facilitate making decisions.


---
**Page 61**
---

Project Management Unit (PMU)

Under the overall guidance of the Project Board and following FAO procurement rules, the Project Management Unit (PMU) will be responsible for: (i) project planning, implementation, monitoring and evaluation of the Project activities; The PMU will be in charge of, among others: (ii) operational planning, managing and executing the project including the direct supervision of all project staff; (iii) ensuring the effective use and management of financial resources and procurement; (iv) reporting on the application of resources and results achieved; (v) preparing annual work plans for the Project Board approval in accordance with FAO’s procedures; (vi) providing inputs to the Project M&E plan, as required by the Project M&E team; (vii) ensuring that all project activities are aligned with the GCF resource allocation; (viii) developing and implementing a risk management framework for the adaptive management of the Project, if required and based on inputs from the Project M&E plan; (ix) promoting information sharing among stakeholders.

The Project Manager will lead the PMU and be responsible for reporting to the Project Board. Three Technical Leaders (TL), one for each output, one General Specialist, one Monitoring and Evaluation Specialist and one Administrative Specialist will report directly to the Project Manager. The Project Manager will also be supported by a Team Leader of GCF resources. The TLs of Output 2 will be the Project Safeguarding Specialist and will be responsible to oversee the implementation of the ESF and the Indigenous Peoples Planning Framework (IPP).

The Project Manager (PM) will be responsible for the overall management and implementation of the project’s activities and operations, including the preparation of work plans. The PM will be recruited by and reports to the Budget Holder (FAO Representative in Colombia) on operational and managerial matters. The PM leads the management team and is responsible for ensuring that all project activities are aligned with the GCF resource allocation. The PM prepares detailed annual project work plans in collaboration with the project management unit and according to logical frameworks, and ensures that these are approved by the Project Board.

The Project Manager has the authority to run the project on a day-to-day basis for management and decision-making on behalf of the Project Board. The Project Manager’s prime responsibility is to ensure that the project produces the results as specified in the project document, to the standard required of quality and within the specified constraints of time and costs.

Under the Project Manager's lead and guidance, the PMU team will head up the preparation of the Annual Work Plans (AWPs) for each output. The PMU will be responsible for the implementation of the AWP, including but not limited to: drafting or overseeing the development of Terms of Reference for consultants, subcontractors and partnerships; ensuring consistent project management across all outputs; preparing progress reports for the PB, technical meetings and other appropriate spaces.

The TL will lead the implementation of activities under each of the proposed Outputs, channeling technical inputs and guidance from the Project Manager. The TLs will be responsible for leading the project team in their respective areas and will report to the PMU. The TLs will also serve as a focal point for the PB and the Project Board Committee. To this purpose the PMU will keep close coordination with MADS, IDEAM, Sinchi Institute and other partners as needed, to assure inter-institutional articulation and ensure consistency between the various project elements.

TLs will be supported by a team of professionals that will work locally leading implementation of activities in the territories. In the case of Output 2, a group of professionals will be hired to support local communities in the design and establishment of community-based conservation areas. The TLs will also be responsible for monitoring the life of the project. For the Output 3, following FAO’s procedures, it is expected to hire a group of consultants that will support the implementation of the project activities. In this case, the PMU will work with MADS, IDEAM and Sinchi Institute including indigenous peoples representatives and organizations to facilitate training, formulation and implementation processes.

Implementing partners

Implementation of project's outputs need the participation and support of partners that are related to each of the areas of work in order to ensure support implementation of specific activities according to the previous experience in the region or the opportunity to strengthen on going processes. Thus Implementing Partners are those entities that will collaborate with FAO in the design, formulation and implementation of project's outputs. These partners may include institutions such as IDEAM, Sinchi Institute, National Parks Unit, Regional Environmental Authorities, Fondo de Cooperación para el Pago por Servicios Ambientales (FCPSA), Universities, NGOs, private sector companies and local communities experiences. The associations, institutions, organizations and entities mentioned in this paragraph shall be engaged by FAO in accordance with FAO's procurement rules.

### Beneficiaries
Beneficiaries are all those institutions and people who would be benefited by the implementation of project activities as explain in the section 2.1.1.

### Articulation with the REM-AVP
As presented above, the AVP funds coming from the REM Program are paid by the Government of UK, Germany and Norway, and administered by KFW. The government of Colombia receives the financial resources, conceived as a trust fund, to implement the project activities in accordance with the terms established for the establishment of an Executive Board that provides recommendations and approves the operative plan. The implementation arrangements of the REM-AVP are described in detail in the section 2.1.1. The UER is managed by a Managing Director and composed by technical leaders for each of the Pillars, International Advisers and other professionals. The Fondo Patrimonio Natural support the UER and the Program in general with the financial coordination, procurement and overseeing contracts, agreements and legal instruments needed for the proper implementation of the Program.

### Figure 7: Implementation arrangements of the REDD Early Movers project of the Amazon Vision Program
In line with the implementation arrangement of the AVP, MADS as the leader of the AVP will head the Project Board. And concerning the implementation of this CCF project, it is important to note that all the institutions that are part of this REDD+ PPR Project Board are also part of the AVP Executive Board; additionally the AVP Executive Board will be responsible for the coordination and harmonization between the different projects. Regarding implementation of activities, FAD or Executing Entity will coordinate financial and administrative management with the UER and the Project Board. The MADS will provide technical advice to the project board on a variety of activities and may subscribe agreements with those institutions for the implementation of some activities. Also the Project Board will be responsible for the REDD+ PPR Project by providing technical advice and guaranteeing articulation and complementarity of activities to be implemented under each of the outcomes.

### C.2.6 Non-carbon benefits
Provide information on the non-carbon benefits associated with the implementation of REDD+ activities, explaining their environmental, social and economic importance for the long-term sustainability of REDD+ projects and provide evidence to this regard.
Colombia's EICGB was formulated with the objective of maximizing its environmental, social and financial benefits associated with forest management and these activities continuing to reduce and control deforestation. In this context, the set of measures prioritized for each Action Line of the strategy generates a set of environmental benefits that are expected to be achieved in the medium term through the implementation of actions such as: (i) regulation of vital cycles that occur in the soil; (ii) services provided by biodiversity; (iii) potential users of forest products and their economic value; (iv) reduction of greenhouse gas emissions related with degradation processes and (v) landscape and connectivity related benefits. All these benefits not only generate an improvement in the quality of life of the communities according to their availability and quality, represent a cultural and economic importance.


---
**Page 63**
---

The identification of multiple benefits in the EICOGB construction projects involved a differential approach which considered and recognized existing views of relevant stakeholders, ethnic groups, rural communities and institutions. The process evidenced the importance of forests for indigenous peoples, Afro-descendant, rural communities and institutional actors. These benefits are related to forest conservation and production based on traditionally used forest resources and the significance for their culture and health. Under this perspective, multiple benefits for indigenous communities are strongly linked to cultural aspects and environmental services of forests services.

In the case of Afro-descendant communities, multiple benefits of forests go beyond carbon-associated services, and are related to biodiversity conservation, water regulation, poverty reduction, economic opportunities from forest products, soil erosion prevention, natural scenic beauty, and agroforestry systems. For rural communities perceptions about multiple benefits are focused on those related to the contribution of forests for food security, income generation, and cultural aspects (territory). For regional entities, such as CANS and sectoral ministries, the benefits are focused on the development of alternative livelihoods based on non-timber forest products, transparency in project execution and increased civil participation and local governance.

In the context of EICOGB multiple benefits were identified and mapped as a tool for driving decisions in the territories. A summary of the benefits identified of forest management, conservation in community lands and agroforestry systems as measures to reduce deforestation are presented in the Figure below.


| Forest Management | Conservation in community lands | Agroforestry systems |
|---|---|---|
| Biodiversity | Protection of species and habitats | Carbon captures agro-diverse systems |
| Water Regulation | Protection or areas at risk of loss of water regulation services Improvement of management of water practices Reduction of evapotranspiration | Improvement of agricultural livelihoods |
| Carbon removals | Reduction of pressures on natural forests Carbon sequestration through forests | Improvement of agricultural productivity |
| Natural scenic beauty | Provision of leisure benefits Leisure and recreation opportunities and improving the living conditions of rural communities Economic opportunities and improving the living conditions of rural communities Local income for services associated with forest use (e.g. tourism, non-timber forest products) | Improvement of livelihoods derived from sustainable production |
| Poverty reduction | Opportunities for generating income from sustainable use and marketing of environmental services | Reduction of the effects of climate change through improvement of nutrient cycles and carbon contents |
| Soil erosion prevention | Maintenance of soil's properties |
