# Figure B. Non-carbon benefits identified in the context of the ECOGIB

In this context, the implementation of the REDD+ project activities will seek to maximize benefits beyond the mitigation of climate change due to the reduction of emissions by preventing deforestation in the Amazon region and the generation of biodiversity-based products and services.

In relation to the possible social impacts of the Project, it is expected that the promotion of a forest-based economy will have a great impact on the generation of incomes to complement local economies. Regarding Output 1, zero-deforestation areas will be established in the project area. The creation of these areas will contribute to the conservation of biodiversity and the preservation of species. In addition, the establishment of zero-deforestation areas will be improving increasing local incomes and generating institutional and communities capacities. Currently the AVIP have been developed a set of guidelines for the implementation of the ECOGIB in the project area. These guidelines include the promotion of forest-based economic activities that involve forests and agriculture production systems and products and sustainable livestock will improve the local community's incomes and thereby help to reduce pressure on forests and other natural resources.

Regarding Output 2, I am convinced that the creation of zero-deforestation areas will contribute to the participation of all the relevant stakeholders in decision-making to reduce deforestation and promote low-carbon rural development strategies.

Investments under Output 2 will generate benefits by offering other forest-based economic activities for local communities. These investments especially offer the delivery of products and services that involve forests and agriculture production systems within an integrated approach. These activities will also generate benefits in terms of biodiversity conservation, water quality improvement, soil protection, and climate change mitigation. The project will contribute to the generation of biodiversity-based products and services. Expected social benefits are related to the increasing of local community's incomes and thereby help to reduce pressure on forests and other natural resources.

The activities related to building local capacities especially on indigenous communities would strengthen territorial governance through the Output 3. The indigenous Peoples will gain greater influence when addressing decisions that affect their territories, as well as in decision-making processes at all levels. This will be achieved by increasing the benefits generated by the Project. Also IP organizations will strengthen their capacities for formulating and implementing projects related to forest conservation.

As the Amazon biome is a strategic ecosystem rich not only in biological resources but also genetic information, threatened by deforestation processes of the region, general environmental benefits are associated to the conservation of the project area as a biodiversity corridor that serves as a habitat for a wide variety of species of both flora and fauna. According to the SINCHI Institute, 1625 useful plants, 1186 species of birds and 193 species of amphibians have been reported in the region.

Finally, the strengthening of forest governance is foreseen as a result of the REDD+ BIP project, given expected results. The project will contribute to the empowerment of indigenous peoples and other local communities people, providing better conditions to access information, fostering efficient and effective use of monitoring systems, consolidating institutional frameworks and promoting wider participation of relevant stakeholders in major decision-making processes.

### D.2. Paradigm shift potential

The paradigm shift that emerged after the Peace Treaty with the TARC is to be fully legitimized with legitimate local governance and law-enforcement structures in place. This will allow for more effective implementation of the AVP, as well as other activities within the project portfolio.

GCF resources will allow the Government of Colombia to meet national commitments regarding progress from deforestation and boost a forest economy at the national level while redesigning a paradigm shift towards improving forests and promoting sustainable models to address deforestation risks and improving local livelihoods for local populations located in post-

# REDD-plus RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND | PAGE 63 OF 104

conflict areas. The Government of Colombia through the ECOCID has launched an ambitious action plan which envisions a transformation from unsustainable and inefficient development models to more sustainable land uses without addressing the social and economic needs that local populations face daily. In this context the ECOCID is proposing a REDD-plus RBP proposal for the Amazon region, which will contribute to the creation of a forest-based sustainable rural development, which contributes to improving the quality of life of rural communities, preservation of ecosystem services derived from forest conservation and contributing to the reduction of GHG emissions and climate change mitigation.

For this purpose, the Government of Colombia with the financial support of the Governments of Germany, Norway and the UK, has launched a REDD-plus RBP proposal for the Amazon region. The Government of Colombia has invested more than US$90 million benefitting 7,400 families, transforming 14,183 ha of existing forest into productive land in the municipalities of San José del Guaviare and Puerto Asís. The country also has made significant efforts to support key enabling conditions for national and local government to address the delivery of REDD-plus benefits. In this context, the Government of Colombia has focused on supporting forest planning at the regional level, strengthening regional environmental authorities capacity in forest management and supporting community-based managements plans in 18 thousand hectares.

Despite the progress made by the RREP-AVM addressing deforestation drivers in areas close to the agriculture frontier as presented above, there is still a significant number of communities that are still exposed to the high poverty burden expected to develop a real transformation at the local level. Given this situation and the goals of the ECOCID and the NSP, the Government of Colombia has decided to propose an integrated model for forest-based rural development based on the goods and services from the forest, integrated to low-carbon rural models. In this context, the paradigm shift proposed by the REDD-plus RBP proposal is to address deforestation drivers based on reducing impacts of agricultural production, land use change and other high impact activities in the Amazon region. This will be achieved through a combination of actions such as: strengthening forest management, provision of sustainable agro-commodities and services, monitoring and information management and capacity building for local communities and civil society organizations. The proposal is designed to address the region while generating local benefits, increasing participation of local communities and civil society, strengthening national and regional institutional capacities for control and management of forests and promoting a more active private sector involvement.

This integrated model will mean complementing ongoing efforts in forest monitoring involving relevant stakeholders at the local level through the implementation of the REDD-plus RBP proposal. The proposal will also support community-based forest units, increase capacity building for local communities and civil society organizations, strengthen forest management and control, develop and access specialized markets for products derived from a forest economy (timber and non-timber forest products), increases indigenous governance and increase participation of all relevant actors.

The implementation of this strategy will provide lessons and experiences to scale-up forest-based models at the national level. The proposed model will also address the social and economic needs that local populations face daily, as well as social problems that represent a risk for the conservation of natural forests. Also the strategy will build on experiences and lessons learned from the implementation of the REDD-plus RBP proposal in the Amazon region, which is being implemented by FAO, entity that will assist in the implementation of this scaled up action in the Amazon region.

It is expected that this model will have a significant impact on income-generation at the local level through the development and marketing of forest-based products. This will be achieved through the provision of goods such as timber, non-timber forest products, as well as the provision of services such as ecotourism, cash and release fishing, scientific tourism, among others. Achievement of this goal is expected to contribute significantly in reducing poverty levels at the local level while increasing the feasibility of forest-based business; thus the project will need active participation of private sector companies interested in generating income from forest products and services. The proposed model will also provide a strong financial component that will allow local communities and supporting institutions to analyze the most feasible production models, as well as identify the market opportunities for the goods and services that are currently available, as well as other factors that could determine the economic, environmental and social feasibility of the forest-based business.

Another key feature of the holistic approach is the integration of actions at various levels, from forests units to local, regional and national institutional levels. The proposed strategy will involve participation of stakeholders at all decision levels. Local communities and indigenous people will lead projects at the local level specifically those related to forest management, establishment of sustainable production systems, ecotourism enterprises and promotion of social

### D.4. Needs of the recipient:
Describe the vulnerability and financing needs of the beneficiary country and population.

Colombia is one of the countries that presents higher vulnerability to climate impacts worldwide, which implies social development challenges in the medium term. The government has made significant progress in recent years to promote equity, resilience and prioritize measures available to each region or sector, including forests in the Amazon region.

In that sense, Colombia has made progress in recent years in the management of climate change in terms of adaptation and mitigation, through the articulation of strategies both at the sectoral level and at the national and territorial levels, which have been complemented by the development of public policies and the implementation of projects. The processes of public and private planning and investment in the territories and sectors, such as the development of the ECOICOB, have allowed Colombia to stand out as one of the leading countries in the face of climate change management in the region.

Despite these great advances and the recognition of the problem regarding the future of the country, there are still some challenges. These include the need for greater coordination between public and private sectors from a management and control, local government governance, strengthening of local communities and indigenous peoples' capacities to manage natural resources, as well as the need to increase the institutional capacity of the State to increase the installed technical and financial capacities, the generation and implementation of public policies and the generation of mechanisms for financing the processes associated with climate change, of the most vulnerable populations, territories and sectors.

Reducing the vulnerability of fragile ecosystems as well as increasing resilience based on the implementation of the action measures of the ECOICOB, avoiding degradation, deforestation and establishing new forests is directly related to and depends on the strengthening of local communities and indigenous peoples' capacities for management. This highlights for the country the severe impacts it faces on its resources and ecosystems, in particular for fisheries and agriculture.

The vulnerabilities and impacts have also been documented in national communications to the Secretariat of the UNFCCC and are an essential element in the formulation of strategies for managing the processes associated with climate change, of the most vulnerable populations, territories and sectors.

Colombia has managed to mobilize significant financial resources, including the REM-AIMV and other projects. However, they do not manage to cover all needs to fully address the diversification and forest degradation in the Amazon region.

As described in B2, the post conflict has had important impacts in increasing deforestation in the Amazon Region, in part due to the lack of economic alternatives for local communities, such as exploitation of natural resources and the establishment of illegal economies based on illegal mining. The presence of informal economies based on illegal opportunities and the presence of illegal economies based illegal mining; land grabbing and illegal crops create a scenario that generates social conflicts around forest areas. In this context the national government is pursuing the implementation of the Amazon Regional Environmental Management (REM-AVP) project, which aims to reduce deforestation from illegal and informal economies to a more sustainable model based on the sustainable management of forests.

Although deforestation rates increased significantly in 2017, during 2018 Colombia experienced a reduction of deforestation that indicates that the investments in the Amazon region are generating results, despite the influence of the post-conflict dynamics. In this context, the country needs to reinforce the current AVP strategy by enhancing forest governance and promoting alternative livelihoods based on the forest, as well as strengthen IP governance to reduce existing threats on indigenous lands.

## D.S. Country ownership

Describe the beneficiary country ownership of, and capacity to implement a funded project or programme (policies, strategic initiatives and institutional arrangements).

The project is consistent with national environmental policies, and the agreements acquired by the country with institutions such as the UNFCCC and UNCBD. The Project, in turn, contributes to the reduction of CO2 emissions by avoiding deforestation, committed by the country in its NDC. In 2016, at the technical committee of the CICB, the forest emission reduction target was established for Colombia, which is equivalent to 32.4 Mton CO2e per year and the reduction of 32.4 Mton CO2e by 2030.

The ECGOB, to which this Project contributes, is part of a broader vision of the country for the peace process and sustainable development. This Colombian vision includes four main goals by 2030: i) reduce emissions from deforestation and contribute to increasing the benefits of biological diversity and ecosystem services; ii) register a positive balance in the carbon stock of forests; iii) achieve a significant reduction in the incidence of forest fires, illegal deforestation and land grabbing; iv) strengthen territorial governance of ethnic groups and rural communities that live and depend on forests.

Complementing the current National Development Plan 2018-2022 issued by Law 1956 of 2019 established within the "Pact for sustainability, produce conserving and preserving" seven targets to be accomplished by 2022. Therefore, the NDC is aligned with the Colombian vision and the National Development Plan 2018-2022, which has a cumulative reduction of GHG emissions with respect to the national reference scenario; i) areas under Payment for Environmental Services (PES); ii) areas under sustainable forest management systems; iii) areas under sustainable conservation systems; iv) reduction of the growth trend of deforestation projected by IDEAM; v) zero deforestation agreements for the productive chains of the agricultural sector under implementation; and vii) percentage of implementation of the National Forest Strategy.

On the other hand, the NDA has been directly involved, along with the MADS, as REDD+ Focal Point Entity and IDEAM in the definition of the project components and its strategic approach. These institutions have led the development of this funding proposal, which is based on the results obtained from the previous phase of the REM-AVP project, and providing technical recommendations through the GCF College Body's review draft the proposal, as per their respective mandates. The College Body recommended that the NDA issue a non-objection letter (Annex 1). As the approach of this proposal is framed in the context of the National Development Plan 2018-2022 and the National Climate Change Strategy, the evaluation supports the prioritization of the proposed activities, thus the coordination and Pillar leaders of the REM-AVP

## Contribution to the Colombian NDC

In September 2015, Colombia presented to the UNFCCC its NDC, which contains goals in terms of mitigation adaptation and means of implementation. Colombia's mitigation goal is reducing 20% of national GHG emissions with respect to a baseline scenario (BAU) by 2030.

The government has established a multi-sectoral framework for the implementation of the NDC, based on sectoral milestones (articles 170 and 171 of Law 1753 of 2015), as sector leaders are consolidating the mitigation actions that under their respective sector (see approval of emissions) would fulfill the commitments agreed in the NDC.

## Figure 9. BAU and Mitigation Scenario of Colombia for the period 2010-2030

Due to the complexity of causes and drivers of deforestation (e.g., direct cause: deforestation by the road building and indirect deforestation: derived from socio-economic dynamics, such as, agricultural colonization and expansion from the adjacent areas), it is not possible to identify a single sector that is fully responsible and have the capacity to contribute to reduce GHG emissions (Energy and Mining, Agriculture, Transportation, Housing, etc.). However, the NDC establishes a multi-sectoral framework for the implementation of the reduction of deforestation and its associated emissions within the Colombian NDC framework.

In 2016, the CICB established a preliminary distribution of the unconditional NDC targets between the sectors. In the case of the agricultural sector, it is not possible to identify a single sector that is fully responsible for deforestation since the country is currently updating its NDC; a formal allocation of mitigation goals by sector has not yet been made. Part of this process included improving the national GHG inventory and identifying the most important drivers of deforestation.

The actions that will contribute to the emission reductions of 22% include activities that have a direct impact on forests, targeted at reducing emissions from deforestation and forest degradation (EDD), as well as those with an indirect impact through sectorial mitigation actions. In this sense this project contributes to the achievement of GHG reduction of these multi-sectoral goals, which are part of the national government's accomplishment of the Climate Change National Policy goals and the sectorial low carbon strategies of the country.

It is important to note that with regard to the AFOLU sector, the mitigation options contemplated by the national government to achieve the goal of reducing emissions by 2030 include:

- Implementation of 554 thousand hectares of intensive silvopastoral systems.

## Project Contributions of the Integrated Strategy for Control of Deforestation and Forest Management - EICDBG

In 2016, the government of Colombia built the EICDBG, aiming to reduce deforestation and forest degradation by promoting sustainable management of forests and their services in the Amazon region through a National Carbon Strategy. This strategy constitutes the main guidance instrument for implementing REDD+ activities at the National Level.

The EICDBG established four main goals, which are nested in the general goal of reducing emissions from deforestation within the framework of the Paris Agreement: 1) Reduce deforestation and forest degradation; 2) Promote sustainable management of forests and their services; 3) Strengthen the conservation and sustainable use of forests; and 4) Promote the role of forests.

The following main goals contribute to increasing the benefits for biological diversity and ecosystem services:

- By 2025, Colombia has a positive trade balance of the forest sector.
- By 2030, Colombia has zero gross deforestation.
- In 2030, 94.2 Mton CO2e emissions have been reduced from avoided deforestation.
- By 2030, the quality and quantity of goods and services that live and depend on forests has been strengthened.
- By 2050, the quality of life of the local population in forest areas has increased.

The project contributes to the achievement of these goals and the established activities in the EICDBG's action lines as follows:

**Project contribution to the National Development Plan 2018-2023 (Law N°595 of 1999):**

Furthermore, this project will contribute to the National Development Plan 2019-2022 by implementing the strategy to reduce and control deforestation, promoting comprehensive environmental interventions with communities and supporting their capacity for sustainable forest management and payment schemes for ecosystem services related to natural capital. Within "For a future sustainability: produce conservation and preserving producing", this project contributes to seven goals to be accomplished in 2022, among them: i) cumulative reduction of GHG emissions with respect to the national baseline; ii) increase of the forest cover area; iii) improvement of the quality of the forest management systems; iv) reduction of the growth trend of deforestation projected by IDEAM; v) zero deforestation agreements for the project areas; vi) inclusion of the project areas in the National Forest Inventory System; and vii) inclusion of the project areas in the National Climate Change Information System. The Project will also support the Government objectives of consolidating the indigenous peoples' territories, strengthening their governance systems, and promoting intercultural dialogue to account the importance of these territories in terms of climate change mitigation and the need for generating sustainable alternative livelihoods.

### D.E Efficiency and effectiveness

Describe the economic and, if appropriate, financial soundness of the programme.

In the design of the programme for the use of the proceeds, recommendations made from a review of the strategy and operational plan of the project were taken into account. These added elements are specifically designed to enhance the efficiency and efficacy. These elements include:

- A more forest-based approach to program interventions in which aspects of forests, community and economy are well integrated.
- The inclusion of a new land use option for communities that allows them to choose between the existing land use option for communities is expanded and the possibility to choose the most efficient option is improved.
- Improving the targeting of projects that are part of the development of program interventions, thereby enhancing the efficiency of the funds used.
- Improving the targeting of actions on deforestation threats, increasing the efficacy and efficiency of the available resources.
- Incorporating monitoring and evaluation components of the impact of the actions, enabling learning from implementation.

### E.1.2. For the use of proceeds

Provide adequate and sufficient information describing how environmental and social risks and impacts will be avoided, reduced, managed or mitigated in line with the project’s objectives and the relevant risk categories. This includes the determination of the relevant environmental and social risk category of the proposed activities and the appropriate environmental and social management framework (ESMF) to be used.

The environmental and social risks and impacts will be identified, screened, assessed and managed using the created instruments: i) FAO guidelines for the Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF) and ii) the risk management instruments of the AVIP.

#### EAF’s ESMF guidelines

According to the Environmental and Social Management Guidelines screening to identify specific environmental and social risks at the project level is the process of identifying and classifying E&S risks associated with individual projects. For each project, a list of potential environmental and social impacts will be developed using the checklist that has a number of questions intended to guide the user in classifying project risk. Based on the project activities and on the assessment of the likelihood of potential environmental and social impacts, three categories for field projects are given: low, moderate, and high.

As a result of the assessment, the project was classified as moderate risk; the following key FAO’s Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF) will be used:

- ESS2 - Biodiversity, Ecosystems, and Natural Habitats
- ESS4A - Animal (livestock and aquatic) Genetic Resources for Food and Agriculture
- ESS5 - Water
- ESS8 – Gender Equality
- ESS9 – Indigenous Peoples and Cultural Heritage

Based on the assessment carried out and its results the Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF) was developed, in which the potential social and environmental risks identified were addressed, as well as the measures to avoid, reduce and mitigate risks and impacts that will be incorporated in the project activities.

Annex 3 contains the Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF) elaborated to provide information on how the project will address the relevant E&S issues. The ESMF is aligned with the IFC Performance Standards and GCF/IFC Performance Standards. The management of environmental and social risk is integrated into the development cycle of the project, from design through implementation and monitoring and evaluation phases. This ensures that appropriate implementation based on potential new risks and impacts might arise. Thus, the ESMF is intended to serve as a practical guide for the identification and mitigation of environmental and social risks associated with proposed investments, and serve as a platform for consultations with stakeholders and potential project beneficiaries.

## Risk management of the Amazon Vision Program

The AVP addresses risk management as a strategic component for achieving its objectives. The five Pillars of the program comprises social and environmental dimensions that must be considered comprehensively and systematically to avoid negative impacts on the environment, society, and indigenous peoples. Therefore, it is necessary to adopt a different risk management approach to better guide the actions and the performance of the Program. That is why the AVP proposes to manage social and environmental risks, called Integral Management of Socio-environmental Risk (GIRSA in Spanish).

This tool seeks to facilitate the identification, analysis and decision-making regarding the management of the risks that may be generated by the implementation of the AVP. The GIRSA tool provides a framework for risk management for the AVP based on a broad conception of the concept of risk and is addressed in three approaches:

- **External and internal risks:** includes the external elements that affect the program performance and the risks of project execution, as well as the internal risks related to the implementation of the program and its components. GIRSA gathers the results of a set of participatory spaces with regional and local stakeholders of the project to identify possible impacts that could occur due to the interventions of the Program.
- **Multi-level Risk:** includes the identification of the risks at each level (program, project, and each pillar), however, the implementation of activities and projects in the territories requires a further detail in risk analysis and the definition of mitigation measures. The GIRSA tool provides a framework for managing these risks. This approach has two levels: program and project.
- **Step-by-step Risk:** includes the specific steps designed for the analysis of the two level of application. Although the steps are applicable to the different levels, the tools applied are differentiated between programs and projects.

### Figure 16 Scheme of the Integral Management of Socio-environmental Risk of the REM-AVP (GIRSA in Spanish)

Based on the GIRSA scheme created and implemented by the REM - AVP the environmental and social risks will be managed in accordance with the established mechanism, tools and procedures of GIRSA. further detail in the extended document.

The System of Safeguards of the GCF is based on the Performance Standards on Environmental and Social Sustainability of the International Financial Corporation of the World Bank Group. The social and environmental indicators are used to monitor compliance with the design and use of REDD+ policies in the period prior to the work of the WFCP (years 2014, 2015 and 2016). In addition, a preliminary analysis was carried out through an exploratory study of the main documents associated with the REDD+ implementation process; the consultations with the parties involved in the project, including national legislation, and interviews with representatives of the government, civil society, indigenous peoples and NGOs.

### Risk management for project implementation

The management of the social and environmental risk of the implementation of the project comprises a set of tools and established mechanisms that ensure that the environmental and social issues are addressed properly in accordance with the project's objectives. The Environmental and Social Action Plan (ESAP) is one such mechanism. The ESAP will help the project culture of transparency with built-in feedback systems and implementation units in identifying and addressing gender-related issues.

Within the ESMA, the ESA/Environmental and social screening check will be applied to identify possible environmental and social risks in the project sub-activities. As the Project was classified as Moderate Risk, according to the FAO's Environmental and Social Assessment (ESA) methodology, the project will develop an Environmental and Social Commitment Plan (ESCP). The sub-activities of the project will be identified during the inception phase in Year 1.

For each sub-activity, the project will identify potential environmental and social risks. This information will be shared with stakeholders through engagement specific information to each site. The ESCP will include a set of mitigation measures with monitoring mechanisms that will be implemented by the project team and reported to FAO.

Within the ESMA unit at Headquarters endorses the pre-implementation documents with ESCPs, the safeguards specialist at HQ will endorse ESCPs are being followed up on, along with stakeholder engagement specific information. This information will be shared with stakeholders through engagement specific information to each site. The project team is responsible for implementing and monitoring any potential risks that may emerge through the implementation phase. This information will be compiled in progress reports and templates will include a section on ESAs risk management, where the above information will be reported.

The Project will build on the previous consultation activities of the REM-AP and the progress made in involving relevant stakeholders through diverse participatory strategies implemented under each of the Program’s Pillars. The formulation of the PIVAs will be based on the results of these consultations and the lessons learned from them, including the CDA (Cooperación, Participación, Corresponsabilidad), territorial governments, local communities’ representatives, sectoral organizations, indigenous peoples, and other relevant actors.

The PIVAs will be structured in three phases: 1) Formulation of Pillar 4, separately involving participation of relevant actors that were interviewed and consulted to identify the main challenges and opportunities for REDD+; 2) Formulation of Pillar 5, separately identifying strategies to address these challenges and opportunities; and 3) Formulation of Pillar 6, bringing together scenarios and capacities between natives and the government institutions, promoting commitment and responsibility of the parts in the decisions to reduce, mitigate and prevent deforestation and degradation of the Amazonian forest.

### PIVAs consultation process

The PIVAs consultation process will have two phases that include the cycle of construction and implementation of the Indigenous Pillar. The Readiness phase seeks to determine the action lines and sub-action activities with the indigenous people and legitimate representatives, as well as the institutional framework for the formulation and structuring of the projects for each of the investment lines, implementation, monitoring, and evaluation of projects.

#### Figure 11: Scheme of the participatory process of construction of the Pillar 4 of the REM-AP (V)

For the consultation with stakeholders, a three-step methodology was designed: i) to collect and analyze information of background, ENPROF, Amazonía, etc., ii) to identify the main challenges and opportunities for PIVAs in meetings or workshops in each department according to the previous criteria identified; and iii) to socialize these priorities, problems, and feedback the final document. Within the construction process of the PIVA 13 workshops were held in the departments of Caquetá, Putumayo, and Vaupés with more than 950 people from 64 ethnic groups. In November 2016, for the first step, results of the workshops with the documentation analysis were consolidated, as well as the arising proposals from the meetings of experts and the contributions of the Amazonian Indigenous Environmental Organizations (EIOs). These inputs were then analyzed by the MRA and MAACC. The results were presented to feedback in 4 workshops, in which the delegates of the MRA, MAACC, some leaders of each department analyzed the final document. The main conclusions drawn from these discussions are:

The following four areas have been identified as priorities for PVA actions: 1) Territory and environment; 2) governance; 3) economy and production and 4) cross-cutting issues such as gender, education, among others.

As foreseen in the participatory construction route, the PVA document was approved in March 2017 by the MRA. In addition, the MRA has been working on a technical harmonization between the investment guidelines and the political decisions required for projects to have an impact on the exercise of the rights of indigenous peoples, the constitutional guarantees and the objectives of the AVP.

Addressing challenges:

a) The implementation of the PVA should promote the articulation of state institutions, so the investment, decisions and orientation of the public policy should be effectively achieved to program objectives.

Commitments agreed in the PIVA document assure continuous involvement of stakeholders. The activities that will be carried out are:

#### 1. Territory and environment
* Indigenous Territorial Planning: Prioritizing the management of the territory based on indigenous knowledge systems and articulation with territorial planning and management instruments that are prioritized.
* Evaluation of existing land use plans: Supporting the evaluation of existing indigenous communities, Afro-descendant communities and farmers for the control and management of natural resources.
* Support to the implementation of the Amazon Indigenous REDD initiative.
* Community bio-cultural monitoring
* Guardianship
* Alignment, extension and constitution of safeguards.
* Supporting the implementation of measures for the integral protection of sacred sites, occupied or abandoned.

#### 2. Indigenous Self-government
* Strengthening of indigenous government systems, indigenous institutions and spaces of participation, coordination and consultation at all levels.
* Formulation of National Development Plans and supporting the development of the environmental component of the Indigenous Safeguard Plans and their articulation with public policy.
* Call for the implementation of the 2016 Constitutional Reform.

#### 3. Economy and production
* Supporting indigenous autonomy, Sovereignty and Food Security.
* Strengthening and promotion of knowledge and practices that ensure food autonomy.
* Improving the economic conditions of indigenous peoples in areas that are culturally, environmentally and productively sustainable.
* Strengthening of indigenous women in issues related to conservation incentives.

#### 4. Strengthening of Indigenous women
* Developing a strategy for autonomous women to make their contribution visible in the resolution of specific problems in participation, governance, improvement of their livelihoods and differential development.

#### 5. Transversal Lines
* Strengthening of Education, Transmission and Conservation of Indigenous Knowledge
    * Recovering and exchanging of the research and transmission of traditional knowledge and its practices.
    * Strengthening of own languages and support for own education processes.
    * Strengthening of skills and knowledge of politics, administration, indigenous legislation to indigenous authorities.
    * Supporting the regional exchange of experiences and learning.

### Process for grievance redress mechanism

The grievance redress mechanism for this Project will implement similar mechanisms to those used by the National Government. The main components of the mechanism is framed in the national policy of citizen service, part of the Open Government Model that manages three fundamental principles: transparency, participation and citizen collaboration. These principles are enshrined in the Constitution of the Republic of Colombia (2014) and in Spanish of citizens at all national level and are applied by all government entities. At the National level operated the Citizen Service Center (CSC), which is a public service that provides information on the services provided by the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development is applying this mechanism with the objective of informing how the citizen can access the services, as well as to provide the necessary support for the resolution all PQRS related to the application, respect or omission of safeguards during the design and implementation of REDD-plus projects. The grievance redress mechanism will be applied in the existing procedures the Project will also define a platform to complete all PQRS received regarding the project.

Currently CORSO of the REM-AVP are managed through a citizen assistance module that is available on the Amazon Vision Program website and addressed by [correos@minambiente.gov.co](mailto:correos@minambiente.gov.co) email to Deputy Minister Office. The PQRS treatment process can consist, in once received, the Pillar Leader prepared the response, which is then sent to

The document is a proposal titled "REDD-plus RBP Funding Proposal" under the Green Climate Fund (page 83 of 104). It outlines procedures for handling complaints related to the Organization's environmental and social standards.

### Project-level grievance mechanism

The project will establish a grievance channel at level two for complaints during project inception phase. Contact information on how to file a complaint will be disclosed in all meetings, workshops, and other related events throughout the life of the project. It is expected that all awareness raising material to be distributed by the project will include contact details for the grievance mechanism.

The project will also be responsible for documenting and reporting as part of the safeguards performance monitoring on any grievances received and how they were addressed.

### Responsibilities

1. The claimant has the right to file a complaint directly with the Project Management Unit (PMU). The process of filing a complaint will consider anonymity as well as any other measures that may be necessary for the protection of the complainant's self-governance system.
2. The PMU will assess whether the complaint is eligible and confidentially forward it to the Project Manager to assess whether the complainant is eligible. The confidentiality of the complainant must be preserved during the process.
3. Eligible complaints will be addressed by the Project PMU, which will prepare an answer in close coordination with the project's partners and FAO and other implementation agencies.

### Internal Process

#### 1. Project Management Unit (PMU)
- The complaint could be written or orally to the PMU directly.
- If resolved, the complaint will be registered, investigated and solved by the PMU.

#### 2. If the complaint has not been solved and could not be solved in level 1:
- Then the Project Manager elevates it to the Project Board (Board).

#### 3. FAO Regional Office
- The assistance of the FAO Representative in Colombia is requested if a resolution was not agreed in levels 1 and 2.

#### 4. FAO Regional Office for Latin America and the Caribbean:
- FAO Representative in Colombia will request it to the FAO Regional Office to receive a grievance or, will transfer the resolution of the grievance entirely to the regional office, if the problem is highly complex.

#### 5. The FAO Regional Representative will request only on very specific situations or complex problems the assistance on the Office of the Inspector General who pursues its own procedures to solve the problem.

### Resolution
- Upon acceptance a solution by the complainant, a document with an agreement should be signed with the agreement.


| Review Level | Responsibilities |
| --- | --- |
| Project Management Unit (PMU) | Project Manager must respond within 10 working days with support of the PMU and other relevant implementation agencies. |
| Project Board | Complaint responsible/complainant does not accept the resolution: Project Manager must send the information to all Project Board members. The Project Board must meet within 5 working days and should be sent within 5 working days after the meeting of the Project Board. |
| FAO Representation in the Country | Complaints that Project Board considers must be addressed at a higher level, such as the FAO Regional Office and FAO must respond within 5 working days in consultation with FAO's Representative. |
| FAO Regional Office for Latin America and the Caribbean | The complainant will not accept resolution from FAO representation in this country: FAO COL sends to FAO regional office, which must respond within 10 working days in consultation with FAO’s Representation. |
| Office of the Inspector General (ORG) | To report possible fraud and bad behavior by fax, confidential: (+39) 06 578 55550

#### Project execution risks

With respect to the overall governance structure, low risk is also addressed by national laws and by the ratification of international instruments, such as the United Nations Convention against Corruption (UNCAC) on 27 October 2007. The legal framework for public sector integrity in Colombia was strengthened with the passage of Law 1476 of 2011, when the Colombian Congress approved the Law 1476 of 2011, which dictates rules aimed at strengthening mechanisms to prevent corruption and improve transparency in the public sector. In addition, the law on public management control in Colombia, besides, the CONPES 167 established in 2013 (National Strategy for comprehensive governance) and the Law 1495 of 2011 (Law of Public Procurement) was adopted to ensure that accountability becomes part of the culture of public and private organizations.

Colombian law establishes mechanisms to enable citizens to be part of accountability exercises such as Veedurías Ciudadanas (citizen oversight) and citizen control councils (Law 849 of 1998, Law 850 of 2003 and decree 1714 of 2003).

#### Environmental and Social Risks

In order to accomplish the mandatory requirements for managing environmental and social performance of FAO field programs and the GCF Risk Guidelines for Funding Proposals, it was applied the FAO's corporate Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF). The ESMF is based on the IFC Performance Standards. The ESMF and the Environmental and Social Management Framework (Annex 3) reports that the project has been classified as moderate risk (Code 2). It should be noted that the project is expected that the project activities will trigger five out of the nine FAO's Environmental and Social Safeguard Policies:

a) ES52 – Biodiversity, Ecosystems, and Natural Habitats;
b) ES53 – Water Resources;
c) ES57 – Aquatic Genetic Resources for Food and Agriculture
d) ES57 – Desert Work;
e) ES59 – Indigenous Peoples and Cultural Heritage.

In the implementation phase, the Management of the Social and Environmental Risk will be carried out to ensure that the environmental and social risks are managed in accordance with the FAO and GCF Policies. The ESMF incorporates FAO's environmental and social screening checklist to identify possible environmental and social risks associated with project activities. The ESMF also includes a risk matrix for each activity. It is also considered the GRM and the Gender Action Plan to help the project in culture of transparency with built-in feedback systems and implementation units in identifying and developing activities for greater environmental and social co-benefits.

## Mitigation Measure(s)
These risks will be mitigated through pre-implementation risk assessments in the areas where activities of the project will be undertaken. According to these assessments, the project will implement activities to reduce risks including conservation agreements with local communities, early warning reports, community-based forest management plans, and training for IPs and local communities to reduce risks related to the development of illegal activities in their territories.

| Description | Risk category | Level of impact | Probability of risk occurring |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |

#### Mitigation Measure(s)
The AVP program has made significant progress involving local communities and IPs in the implementation of projects and activities to reduce deforestation in the region. Building on this experience, the project will mitigate these risks as follows: a) training program for community members, community leaders, and civil authorities will raise awareness about the project and good practices; b) IP coordination meetings will be held as measures to reduce deforestation and increase local livelihoods; c) training program will directly involve IPs and community workers who will provide technical assistance and facilitate information to local communities.

## E3. Gender considerations

### E3.1 For the period of the achieved results
Provide a summary of the information in the assessment describing the extent to which the measures undertaken complied with the GCF gender policy.

FAD's Environmental and Social Standards (ESS) were adopted in 2015. At the project and field level, nine ES standards were developed for each activity, including gender equality. The ESS was used as a reference point for project design and implementation based on outcome-based approach. A project alignment review was conducted to assess the extent at which activities implemented by the heart of the Amazon Project were consistent with FAD ES standards, and consequently with REDD+ safeguard requirements.

The ESS B Gender Equity analysis comprised identifying the advances and actions in regards to:
- Providing equal access to and control over productive resources, services and markets.
- Strengthening women's capacity for decision-making at all levels of project policy processes.
- Ensuring that all stakeholders benefit equally from development interventions and that inequality is not reinforced or exacerbated.

In compliance with the ESS B Heart of the Amazon Project achieved during 2015 and 2016:
- The women participated in specific workshops to address their needs and aspirations with regards to food security and productive alternatives in the chagras in Miriti Parana.
- SOA provided training on gender equality, providing women access to conservation-friendly livelihood activities.
- The project supported traditional food security systems (chagras) managed by women.
- Women were strengthened in the entire process around the chagras that include their traditional planting systems and knowledge of how to maintain them. Women are maintaining their crops, but also their traditions, passing knowledge from one generation to another and conserving the environment.

The EGCOR gender action plan takes a center focus on identifying the role of women as producers and conservation agents, identifying knowledge with women's productive potential and observing the differential effects of programs from a gender approach. The gender approach is mainstreamed as a guiding principle of the ECGCOR, and highlighted in:
- The active participation of the Platform of Black Women Afro-Colombians, considered as key actors in the conservation of forests and biodiversity, as well as the implementation of the national forest policies.
- This Platform has been working with the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development on issues related to the causes and impacts of climate change, deforestation and the ECGCOR's measures and actions.
- The Platform is also involved in the development of a gender action plan for the REDD+ project and related to gender-related studies.

### Promotion and developing community participation in environmental protection, sustainable development and adequate management of natural resources programs, taking into account a participatory and gender approach, generating participation of women and youth.

The thematic focus areas within the EICDBG include gender differentiation.

The EICDBG implementation and the map routes are developed jointly with indigenous peoples, black and rural communities under a gender focus.

During the EICDBG formulation process, women took a special role in participatory processes. In order to generate conditions of equity in access to knowledge and capacity building processes organizations were asked to include women representatives on their boards. During the dialogue sessions, women participated actively in all thematic calls to spaces; there was one specifically for the participation of women to identify the different needs, both from the perspective of indigenous peoples and black rural communities. Women also took part in the EICDBG participatory spaces. At the National Indigenous Workshop on Sustainable, a woman's delegation was invited by each organization to participate in the dialogue sessions. The proportion of women in the National REDD+ Board (now the National Forest Board) the participation of men was 58.3% and women 43.7%.

Within the process of construction of the EICDBG strategy (UN-REDD Program), a total of 80 participatory spaces were organized, with 2132 participants, in 68.4% men and 31.6% women. During the eleven events of Dialogue and Consultation on Sustainable Development, 59.7% of the participants were men (1291) and 40.3% women (821), a total of 666 people participated in the 23 events of information and dialogue events, 30.3% women.

Especially in the participatory process of indigenous peoples in the construction of the EICDBG, promoting participation in dialogues and consultations on sustainable development, women were active participants in all thematic calls to spaces processes. In general, women participation was active through proper institutional platforms such as secretaries, dialogue facilitators, etc., and in some cases, they took part in decision-making processes. The representation rates disaggregated by sex in the developed spaces with indigenous peoples in each region are the following:

### E.3.2 For the use of these data

Provide adequate and sufficient information on how the AE will undertake activity-level gender assessments and action plans for each project.

The goal of the FAO Goal on Gender Equality is to achieve equality between women and men in sustainable agricultural production and rural development for the elimination of hunger and poverty." As it is central to FAO's mandate, this goal is a key element of the organization's strategy. The achievement of this goal will contribute to the achievement of the strategic programs (SP) at two levels: within each SP and across all of them. Specifically in Colombia, the implementation of this approach has generated important lessons learned.

* **The gender approach in indigenous communities goes hand in hand with the participatory approach, which builds on stakeholder participation and consultation processes to ensure that women's voices are heard (and that mediation that involves major male actors).**
* **There is a harmonious transformation of gender roles without men involvement. Work with male farmers and/or producers is relevant to gender equity. In the area of sustainable rural development, gender-sensitive mainstreaming is essential for the achievement of the SPs. This approach has been applied in the design of projects and quotas and opportunities (in the form of affirmative action) that Projects may provide to women.**
* **Projects have been designed with a participatory approach, which includes the participation of women working with organizations such as resguardos, cabildos or community councils. It is essential that the projects define specific gender objectives and indicators for each project.**
* **Local technical teams must be sensitized and trained to implement project activities with a gender perspective so that gender equality becomes a factual fact, which can be traced and technical measures defined. Technical teams and partners involved in the project implementation need to be part of the gender action plan.**


---
**Page 94**
---

FAO’s work will systematically examine, assess locally and address women’s and men’s needs, priorities and experiences as part of the development of the project and knowledge capacity building activities. This is to do ensure women are not only equal beneficiaries but also active participants in the project. The project will engage with planning and implementation processes on various levels for gender-equitable outcomes and ensuring a gender-sensitive approach. It will work closely with the Gender Unit (GU) within the Environment, Social and Rural Institutions Division (ESR), regional gender officers and a network of Gender Focal Points (GFPs) working at Headquarters and in the field. The project will also engage with the FAO Country Office (CO) staff. The GU consists of Gender Officers stationed in Regional gender offices and GFPs in sub-regional and country offices plays a central role to ensure that the project is implemented in line with a global approach.

As a principle, prior to making any gender perspective (as the process of assessing the implications for women and men of any planned action), prior to project implementation two actions will be developed:

1. Conduct a baseline survey and local assessments in the early stage of project implementation, including gender-aware questions and indicators. This will therefore set the long frame results framework as indicators of outcomes, outcomes and impact to be tracked by the project.
2. Establish a participatory and gender-sensitive monitoring framework to assess progress and impact on men and women. The framework should also allow for both genders to make suggestions for changing certain activities or adding new ones, with a self-evaluation by participants.

The expected results for gender equity can be categorized as follows:

- **Instruments:** The Project will monitor sex-disaggregated indicators to identify women's participation in activities such as the implementation of conservation agreements, local monitoring and making decision processes. Control protocols, Local Zero Deforestation Agreements, elaboration of Forestry Units' Management Plans, implementation of forest management, leadership of social initiatives and others related to planning and management and governance of land and resources.

- **Women farmers:** The Project will implement specific activities to strengthen women's knowledge and capacities in the areas of sustainable forest management. These activities include training programs for women as part of the Project such as implementing good forest management practices and other environmental sustainability actions, access to credit facilities, support to women’s associations, promotion of women's participation in local businesses, increasing access to financial instruments and incentives that promote environmental sustainability.

- **Indigenous women:** In order to recognize and enhance women's traditional knowledge in forest management, project activities will include the implementation of gender-sensitive participatory processes. These processes are formulated by women and women groups, and will undertake other activities to strengthen capacities related to the formulation of conservation agreements, local monitoring and making decision processes (with special attention to the collective ethnic context) and access and participation to IPs decision-making bodies.

- **Gap analysis:** The Project will conduct a gender gap analysis in order to identify the specific causes of gender inequality and the potential risks of the project. Based on this assessment the project will adjust the gender plan for each area of intervention and execute activities accordingly.


Regarding the Gender Action Plan, the Project will strengthen women's participation in two perspectives: peer participation and incident participation. To this end, differential quotas of 40 per cent will be adopted in the most relevant processes and decision-making bodies. The project will also promote the development of women’s leadership confidence, gender-sensitive leadership and assertive communication, among others, in dialogue with their own cultural dynamics, and methodologies with gender content and women's empowerment will be incorporated. In addition, after the rising awareness work, a series of institutional arrangements should be addressed that are related to equal opportunities and the eradication of gender-based violence, whose scenarios are impacted by the project are the following: establishment of a national gender focal point; ensuring that women and girls are adequately responsible for the issues at the level of national government counterparts; ensuring the participation of young women and boys in the design and implementation of the project; identification of gender-sensitive strategies and action plans and recommendations; promotion of the transformation of roles in forest conservation, transcending stereotypes; market strategy to ensure that women and indigenous peoples are not left behind; addressing the existing knowledge gaps; financial instruments and mechanisms that reach women directly; recognition of indigenous women's own knowledge and practices related to forest management, traditional conservation practices and natural resource governance systems; and a dialogue table for project improvement.

Following national experiences, the Project will create a working table with female farmers either and/or producers' leaders and indigenous women who, together with the leader's gender referent and the Ministry of Environment's delegate, will make recommendations on how to ensure that the project is implemented in accordance with gender conditions. This will be in place from the first moment of project implementation and will meet at least once a year, guaranteeing fulfillment of one of the main requirements for a gender approach and this is the dialogue with the women who receive the impact of the project.

### E.4. Interim policy on prohibited practices

#### E.4.1. For the period of the achieved results

Provide appropriate and sufficient information including control measures that assure that the projects will be used in a manner compliant with the Interim Policy on Prohibited Practices, such as undisclosed Prohibited Practices, including money laundering, financing of terrorism, and other illicit activities.

The Financial Information and Analysis Unit (UIAF in Spanish) is an intelligence agency of the Colombian State, created by Law 1024 of 2006. The UIAF has a specific unit to prevent, detect and fight money laundering and financing of terrorism, being the body responsible for our country's financial intelligence, complying with international standards and protocols such as the FATF (Financial Action Task Force) and the AML/CFT (Anti-Money Laundering/Combating Financing of Terrorism) - FATF. The UIAF has an intelligence unit with operational, technological and human capacity to identify and prevent threats related to money laundering and financing of terrorism.

The UIAF has the Suspicious Operation System Records IROS in order to identify and manage information of money laundering and terrorist financing activities in Colombia. The private sector and government entities report information to the UIAF through the Suspicious Operations Report (SOS) system, which is used by the UIAF jointly with the National University of Colombia for the analysis of suspicious operations.

On the other hand, in 2015 and 2018 the REM AVP was implemented jointly by KfW Development Bank and Deutsche Gesellschaft für Internationale Zusammenarbeit (GIZ), and commissioned by the German Federal Ministry for Economic Cooperation and Development (BMZ), therefore the financial management and expenditure accomplished the standards of the Interim Policy on Prohibited Practices. The Memorandum of Understanding (MoU) was signed in August 2016 between the Government of Colombia and the KfW Development Bank.

The Financing Implementation Agreement contains the requirements, procedures and rules for the financing implementation of the project, which are based on the provisions contained in the Interim Policy of the General Working Plan and the Annual Working Plan, which include in detail the execution schedule, costs and financing of activities. The agreement also includes the terms and conditions for the monitoring and evaluation of the distribution of resources. Additionally, annual external audits were covended according to recognized international standards of financial management and the IWF.

As per article 9.03 par. (a), of the Accreditation Master Agreement between FAO and GCF, FAO will apply its own fiduciary principles and standards relating to "any 'know your customer' checks, anti-corruption, AML/CFT, fraud, financial crime prevention and control" as set out in the Policy on Prohibited Practices.

The Policy on Prohibited Practices of FAO establishes:

**Fraud and other corrupt practices pose a grave threat to the effective implementation of the Organization's policies and objectives. The Organization is entrusted with significant resources and commensurately significant responsibilities in this regard, and it is imperative that all resources allocated for this purpose, both human and financial, are not diverted from their ultimate goal. The Organization has therefore adopted a zero-tolerance policy on fraud and other corrupt practices. This means that it expects that all operations are free from fraud and other corrupt practices, and to be held accountable to donors and beneficiaries for the proper implementation of its programs. To this end, the Organization has adopted a zero-tolerance policy in respect of fraud and other corrupt practices.**

The Policy on Prohibited Practices is consistent with the Organization's efforts to combat fraud and corruption and is aligned with the principles contained in the United Nations Convention Against Corruption and the United Nations Convention Against Transnational Organized Crime.

The policies and procedures outlined herein are to be read and understood in conjunction with the following documents:

- Manual 501: Definitions and Rules
- Standards of Conduct for the International Civil Service (Appendix A to Manual Section 304)
- Manual 502: Code of Conduct
- Manual Section 507: Letters of Agreement
- Chapter 6.1 of the Administrative Instructions Manual (Appendix A to Manual Section 107)
- Guidelines for Internal Administrative Investigations by the Office of the Inspector General Administrative remedies and sanctions

FAO Vendor Sanctions Policy (Administrative Circular 2014/27) and

Whistle Blower Protection Policy (Administrative Circular 2011/55).

Scope of this policy:

This policy applies, regardless of their location, to all activities and operations of the Organization, whether funded by Regular Programmes or Extra-Budgetary Funds; administrative, technical or operational in nature; or implemented by the Organization on behalf of an implementing partner, including any government agency. This policy applies to all staff personnel I and all contractual arrangements between the Organization and third parties, including contractors and other third parties for administrative, technical or operational purposes.

Measures to avoid double payment or financing:

As a guarantee of transparency and exhaustiveness in the GCF accounting emissions and mitigation initiatives in the Colombian Amazon, according to Resolution 1447 (2018), see F.6 for [detailed information], there is a mechanism for identifying overlapping initiatives and mitigation projects that allow respecting the ownership and the entitlement to claim CERs and other credits.

According to Resolution 1447 of 2018, the following is established for managing the overlapping of initiatives:

- The overlap can occur when an initiative intends to register in REDD+ activities GHG reduction or removals in execution periods and/or geographical areas for which there is an initiative previously registered in implementation phases.
- The types of overlapping are:
  - Compatible type when there is no overlap between different initiatives in the implementation phase, such as the coexistence of forest conservation and GHG mitigation activities,
  - Non-Compatible type when there is an overlap of initiatives in the implementation phase for the same period geographic areas of intervention and GHG mitigation activities.

**Law 21 of 1997 that approves the ILO Convention No. 169, the United Nations Declaration on Indigenous Peoples**, and the United Nations Declaration on Rights of Indigenous Peoples.

### 4. Indigenous peoples under voluntary isolation: Activities of the project will also be implemented in close coordination with local valid representatives, following the procedures agreed in the framework of the AVIP. In the case of indigenous peoples living in voluntary isolation, or remote groups with limited external contact, the project will seek to support their right to remain isolated and to live freely according to their culture.

### 5. Participation and traditional knowledge: The project will promote active participation of indigenous peoples and their representative organizations recognizing, respecting, and valuing their contributions to the implementation of the project. This will be achieved through a participatory approach in the design of work plans, strategies and instruments promoted by the project. Output 3 specifically will be targeted to support actions proposed by indigenous people’s organizations that would be selected under a set of criteria that include the respect and recognition of traditional knowledge as well as the improvement of local livelihoods.

### 6. Capacity building: Activities will be designed and agreed upon according to the procedures implemented by the AVIP within the framework of the project. The capacity-building activities will focus on issues related to monitoring, project formulation, implementation of conservation agreements and improvement of forest management, among others. In order to facilitate access to resources and capacity building for indigenous peoples’ organizations, the project will provide technical assistance in the formulation and implementation of projects, including financial and management capacities for local leaders, organizations and communities.

The use of funding from this project is subject to the FAO’s Indigenous Peoples Policy as well as FAO’s Policy on Indigenous and Tribal Peoples and FAO’s Environmental and Social Management Guidelines. In particular subject to: FAO Environmental and Social Safeguard (ESS) 9 on Indigenous Peoples and Cultural Heritage as indicated in the ESMF, Herd, and other relevant guidelines. The project will ensure respect and compliance with national and international policies, applicable laws and regulations as referred below:

- Respect the UN Declaration on the Rights of Indigenous Peoples.
- Promote the recognition and protection of the inherent rights to self-determination and identity of indigenous peoples (right to decide when kind of development that takes place among their people and on their lands and territories, in accordance with their own needs).
- Ensures the application of the principle of Free, Prior and Informed Consent (FPIC) of indigenous peoples affected by projects.
- Recognize: respect and preserve the rights, lands, natural resources, territories, livelihoods, knowledge, social fabric and cultural heritage of indigenous peoples.
- Protect cultural heritage and avoid its alteration, damage or removal.

**ESS 9 recognises indigenous peoples’ traditions and knowledge present opportunities for many of the challenges that humanity faces today. It also highlights the role of indigenous peoples in relation to indigenous food systems in the face of increasing food demand and traditional knowledge with respect to climate change adaptation vulnerabilities and impacts.**

### E.E. Monitoring and evaluation

Please note that monitoring arrangements that will take place for providing annual monitoring reports based on the information provided for the use of proceeds in clauses C.2.3 and C.2.4.

In line with the Activity 1.1, monitoring of Project Outcomes will be developed by DEAM (SMBYC) using the processes, methodologies and tools described in the Monitoring Plan. The indicators to be monitored are: i) forest cover change; ii) amount of carbon stocks in natural forest; and iii) emission and removals of GHGs related to deforestation. As stated above, DEAM has the overall responsibility for the operation of the SMBYC and the INF, including its administration, coordination and operation.


---
**Page 100**
---

Regional environmental authorities (CARs) will carry out monitoring activities in their jurisdiction with the technical support provided by IDEAM. The gathered information will be introduced to the Platform or delivered to the PMU team in the case of a project under implementation. One of the outputs of this activity is the preparation and submission of a monitoring report on the performance of the Project. The time frame of the monitoring will be from 2020 to 2030, with annual reporting.

The PMU and the FAO Country Office will carry out the M&F Plan in accordance with FAO and GCF procedures. The project inception phase will include updating of the Theory of Change describing the causal relationship between ecosystem services and human well-being, as well as the identification of indicators for monitoring the performance of effective and operational impact monitoring and measurement system to calculate causal and attributable changes and progress towards achieving results set out in the Performance Management Framework (PMF). The M&F Plan will be explored with other activities of the project as: MRV systems; fires control, forest management monitoring tools, among others. The M&F Plan will also include a description of the indicators to be used for measuring the project implementation and planning activities. Progress will be measured against baselines, targets and indicators.

At the project level impact, two indicators are proposed in line with GCF investment criteria (impact potential): 1) Total number of direct and indirect beneficiaries; and 2) Ton of carbon dioxide equivalent (tCO2e) reduced. The monitoring and reporting framework will be developed during the inception phase and finalized at the end of the implementation plan.

FAO will perform monitoring and reporting throughout the reporting period. FAO has a country presence and capacity to perform M&E, including implementing tools and methods to facilitate performance monitoring of the project. The methods are support vertical monitoring, from the beneficiaries to management, and will facilitate comparative and standardized monitoring across projects. The PMU will be responsible for ensuring that all project activities are reported in the PB that combines financial reporting and progress toward achieving results set out in the Performance Management Framework.

The monitoring system will be comprehensive and entail the Project work plan as well as the action plans for gender, indigenous people, biodiversity and the social and environmental framework.

The day-to-day project monitoring and implementation responsibility rests on a nationally recruited Project Manager that will have full authority to manage all aspects of the project. The PMU will provide technical support in areas such as: the development of the M&F Plan; the preparation of the Monitoring and Evaluation Unit; the M&E Specialist will develop an M&E plan, according to FAO Colombia’s template, explaining how the indicators will be measured and reported on a regular basis (quarterly) during the project life cycle with the respective exercises of M&E, as well as the responsibilities of the project team regarding M&E. This M&E plan will be submitted to the PMU for approval by the end of each reporting period. The PMU will monitor the progress of the project implementation and ensure that the M&E plan is appropriate and corrective measures can be adopted. The PMU will ensure that all project staff maintain a high level of commitment, motivation and accountability in order to achieve the project’s objectives. The PMU will also support the PM as needed, including through annual monitoring missions. Additional M&E and implementation quality assurance and troubleshooting will be provided by IDEAM.

A project inception workshop will be implemented in order to: a) agree on the project's theory of change; b) build a common understanding on the project strategy and discuss any changes in the overall context that might influence implementation, including climate change impacts, political instability, socio-economic conditions and communication lines; c) review the results framework and discussion, reporting, monitoring and evaluation roles and responsibilities. The PMU will provide technical support to the workshop. The PMU will also be responsible for scheduling ESG meetings; and f) finalize the first year’s work plan. The Final Inception Report will be approved by the PS and GCF.

The PM and FAO will provide inputs to the Annual Report for each year of implementation: The PM and the M&E Specialist will ensure that the indicators in the results framework are monitored annually. The Annual Reports will be shared with the PMU and other stakeholders. The annual performance reports are due to GCF 60 days after the end
