# REDD+ RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL

## Project Inception Workshop

The Project Inception Workshop will be held within the first 3 months of the project organization structure: FAO Country Office and, where appropriate, with relevant government counterparts, donors, NGOs, local communities, indigenous peoples, and technical advisors as well as key stakeholders. The Inception Workshop is crucial for building ownership of the project results and to plan the three-year annual work plan. The Inception Workshop will address several key issues including:

- Assist all partners fully understand and take leadership of the project.
- Define roles, responsibilities and reporting relationships among FAO Country Office and Regional staff as it aves the project team.
- Discuss decision-making functions and responsibilities within the project’s decision-making structures, including reporting and communication lines, as well as conflict resolution mechanisms.
- Agree on the M&E plan, which will be reviewed and agreed on the indicators, targets and their means of verification, and recheck assumptions and risks.
- Present a draft work plan that meets monitoring and evaluation (MME) requirements. The MWE work plan and budget will be agreed and scheduled.
- Discussion of financial reporting procedures and obligations, and arrangements for payments.
- Planning of project implementation. Roles and responsibilities of all project organization structures will be clarified, and meetings planned. The first project Board meeting will be held within the first 12 months after the project start date.

An Inception Workshop Report will be a key reference document and will be prepared and shared with partners to summarize various actions and plans decided during the meeting.


## Annual Project Report

This important report is prepared by the Project Technical Advisors, consolidated by the Project Manager, reviewed by the Inter-ministerial Technical Committee on Climate Change and finally approved by the Project Board to monitor project progress throughout the reporting period.

The format and content of this report will be adjusted based on the simplified reporting regime, which will be established for RBP in the GCF.


## Mid-term evaluation (MTE)

The project will undergo an independent mid-term evaluation at the midpoint of project implementation. The mid-term evaluation will assess progress towards achieving outcomes and will identify corrective actions if needed. It will focus on the effectiveness, efficiency and timeliness of project implementation, highlighting issues requiring decisions and actions, and will present initial lessons learned about project design and management. Based on the findings from this evaluation, the Project Board will review the Project with regard to the national protocols and methodologies to monitor tons of GHGs avoided / reduced, cost per ton of CO2 equivalent reduced or sequestered.

As described under Activity 1.1 The project will identify needs for strengthening of the national system for the generation of local/regional reports that support decision-making processes at these levels. Therefore the Project will strengthen the capacity of the National Forest Monitoring System (NFMS) and transfer technologies at the local level, building on the existing forest, carbon and fire monitoring systems.

The Project will also design a specific mechanism to monitor and report on other areas such as gender equity, livelihoods, poverty reduction, etc. The project will also focus on knowledge management, development, innovation and scalability, which will provide innovative tools and methodologies to feed the mid-term and final evaluation reports and support national monitoring systems


## Final Evaluation

As an independent Final Evaluation will be launched within six months prior to the project’s actual completion date. NTE it will aim at identifying project outcomes, their sustainability and actual or potential impacts, including inter alia global environmental benefits. It will also have the purpose of indicating future actions needed to assure continuity of the process developed through the project.

# REDD-plus RBP Funding Proposal

## Legal Framework

### Title III: National Registry Results

#### F. Legal Framework

**Title III: National Registry Results**

Provide an analysis with respect to legal title to REDD-plus results in the country. This should include an explanation of how the registry will be implemented.

The Law 173 of 2015[4] created the National Registry of Reduction of Greenhouse Gas Emissions (RENAIE) [in Spanish], which included the national registry of REDD + programs and projects and regulated disputes about who has the right to claim results. The law also established that the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development will regulate and administer the RENARIE and under the article 173 establishes that any natural or legal person, public or private who intends to opt for payments based on results or similar compensations as a result of actions that generate emission reductions of GHG shall obtain prior the Registry in accordance with the regulations issued by the MADS.

Regarding the entitlement to claim for results payment, article 175 of the same law established that any natural or legal person who intends to opt for payments based on results or similar compensations as a result of actions that generate emission reductions of GHG shall obtain prior the Registry in accordance with the regulations issued by the MADS. More information is available at: http://visionamazonica.minambiente.gov.co/contenidos/uploads/2018/2/Reglas_interno-2013.pdf

Finally, the Law 173 pointed out that the emissions reductions registered by the MADS in the frame of such national or regional programs and projects of REDD+ will no longer be available for other parties to claim the results thereof.

Consistent with any other party has the right to claim the results proposed to the GCF in accordance with national policy, legal or regulatory frameworks.

Following the mandate of the Law 17453, the MADS issue the Resolution 1427 of 2018 to regulate the Monitoring, Reporting and Verification (MRV) System for the National Registry of Greenhouse Gas Emissions Accounting of GHG System (MVR System). This Resolution also regulates the RENARIE which includes the national registry of REDD + programs and projects.

RENAIRE is being managed by DEAM as part of the Environmental Information System for Colombia (SIC in Spanish), this technological platform: i) consolidates the mitigation projects in accordance with the GHG accountability rules established; ii) accounts and records the GHG emission reductions and removals; and iii) consolidates the results of the national registry of REDD + programs and projects.

The platform was launched in October 2019 and currently is operational, capable of registering the projects and programs such as: Nationally Appropriate Mitigation Actions (NAMAs), Low Carbon Development Programs, REDD+ programs, and other mitigation initiatives. The platform also allows to register the results of other mitigation initiatives defined by MADS or the UNFCCC within the framework of its GHG mitigation mechanisms.

The Article 12 of this Resolution states that any holder of a GHG mitigation initiative in the national territory that intends to opt for payments based on results or similar compensations as a result of actions that generate climate change goals established under the UNFCCC, must register their mitigation initiative in the RENARIE during its feasibility phase.

# REDD-plus RFB BUNDLING PROPOSAL

According to the Resolution, the MADDS registered the emissions reductions achieved on the Amazon biome for the period of 2016 against the sub-national FREL (approved by the UNFCCC in Decision B.1/3) based on technical assessment and analysis under the UNFCCC.

Specifically the Resolution 1447 of 2018 and the RENARE accounting units included in projects REDD+ in the event that the same geographic area period and mitigation activities of other REDD+ program enrolled in the feasibility phase, in the same geographic area period and mitigation activities of other REDD+ program enrolled in the feasibility phase, the overlap will be considered on a case-by-case basis.

The cancellation of GHG emissions reductions or removals refers to the desactivation of the GHGs reduced and/or removed in RENARE by the holder of the initiative. Once GHG reductions and/or removals are cancelled, the holder will not be able to claim any credits for these reductions and/or removals. The holder may still be eligible for result based payments or a similar compensation against (paragraph 1 Article 17, Resolution 1447 of 2018).

In addition, Colombia will record in the information hub on the UNFCCC web portal the quantity of results obtained between 2016 and 2035 for which payments will be received under this project program. In tons of carbon dioxide equivalent per year, which will be recorded in the National Registry System.

In addition, Colombia will transparently record that GCF is the entity paying for results, and corresponding result-based payments will be made to the holder of the initiative.

Therefore, the Government of Colombia covenants that no other party will have a competing claim to the results proposed to the GCF in accordance with national policy, legal and regulatory frameworks.

# Secretariat’s assessment of FP134

## Overall assessment of the Secretariat

### Project background

The project presents Colombia’s REDD+ results for the period 2015–2016, with an estimated reduction in emissions of 6.95 million tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (CO2e) derived from reducing deforestation. These results have been reported to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) and are subject to a technical assessment and have already been published on the UNFCCC Lima REDD+ information hub.

The results presented here are based on the outcomes of Colombia’s first national REDD+ pilot programme; they are fully compliant with the eligibility criteria set out in the terms of reference for the implementation of the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) standards. The results have undergone analysis based on the scorecard as shown in the annex.

Colombia will use the proceeds from RBP to invest in additional activities that support the implementation of the National REDD+ Strategy, including:

- Implementation of the Integrated Strategy for Deforestation Control and Forest Management (ESECIF). This will focus on the following outputs:
  - Output 1: Forest areas sustainably managed and contributing to close the agriculture frontier; and
  - Output 2: Forest areas sustainably managed and contributing to close the agriculture frontier;
- Strengthening national governance and capacity of indigenous peoples for forest management and conservation.

### Scorecard results

As per decision 18/107, the proposal has been assessed using the scorecard for REDD+ RBP. The assessment has been conducted by the Secretariat with the support of land use, land-use change and forestry (LULUCF) experts. The final score was discussed and agreed with the independent expert reviewer.

| Scorecard section | Results |
| --- | --- |
| Carbon elements | Score: 20/20 All criteria “pass” |
| Cancun Safeguards | All criteria “pass” |

1 See <https://rbiid.unfccc.int/>

### Strengths and points of caution

#### Strengths
- The use of proceeds will be reinvested to deepen implementation of the ambitious Integrated Landscape Restoration Control and Forest Management

#### Points of caution
- As per the terms of reference, GCF will make the payments in a single disbursement.
- Payments for results-based payments under REDD+ Early Movers Programme would mostly be supervised directly via the accredited units of account, annual reporting will be provided to GCF.
- The lack a globally agreed definition on HFLD under GCF could lead to confusion with existing HFLD definitions for qualifying as a result. This may allow varied interpretations that would need to be further discussed facing a continuation of REDD+ Forest-based payments under GCF.


---
**Page 115**
---

1. The Secretariat invites stakeholders and partners to recall that the objective of the request for proposal (the REDD+ BIP pilot programme) is to gather experience to further improve the procedural and technical elements of REDD+. BIPs using GCF resources in a learning context are expected to be designed with a view to being scaled up. However, there is further room for improvement of the technical instruments in any future configuration of REDD+ BIP under GCF. This is to be designed as reflected in the mid-term review presented for consideration at COP23 (GCF/REDD+/Addendum 1).
2. The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions set out in the respective term sheet and addendum XIII, titled "List of proposed conditions and recommendations."

## Assessment of performance against investment criteria

### 2.1 Impact potential Scale: High

- Colombia consistently maintained high forest cover and low deforestation rates until 2014, resulting in EDRs of 31.474 tCO2e to 65.8 tCO2eq between 2015 and 2016. Of these emissions 22 per cent were attributed to the country’s REDD+ readiness interventions.
- Colombia has received other REDD-plus payments for the results of 2015–2016, mainly from the REDD+ Early Movers Initiative (EMI) and the UN-REDD Programme. The EMI is a mechanism that provides financial incentives to countries for the achievement of their national REDD-plus readiness interventions in the country, which promoted a favourable framework for the approval of the 2015–2016 results.
- Colombia has achieved significant emission reductions through its REDD+ BIP pilot programme and other REDD-plus readiness interventions in this period, including those proposed in the REDD-plus BIP request for proposal. These results are under technical assessment by the UNFCCC secretariat. These results have undergone technical assessment in accordance with the decisions of the UNFCCC Conference of the Parties (COP) on REDD-plus.
- The achievement of these pilot programme results is expected to continue and lead to further emission reductions, resulting in a continued reduction in emissions from deforestation and forest degradation as well as an increase in carbon stocks and conservation.

### 3.1 Forest reference emission level (Amazon region)

- Colombia demonstrated ambition to scale up to the national level, mainly through the implementation of ECDICB and the link to the commitments of the LULUCF sector under Colombia’s NDC. The 10 departments that are part of the Amazon biome in Colombia include: Antioquia, Arauca, Cauca, Guaviare, Guainía, Putumayo y Vaupés, as well as parts of Meta, Narino, Vichada and Casanare.
- These departments cover 40 per cent of the total area of Colombia. They include 125 million hectares of the protected areas outlined by the National System of Protected Areas of Colombia and represent 49 per cent of the country’s forested area. The Amazon biome is home to indigenous peoples of the country living in the Amazon, representing 17 per cent of the inhabitants of the region.
- The country continues to build an ambitious and powerful approach to REDD-plus based on the activities considered in the ECDICB, the NDCs and the Amazon Vision Programme (AVP).
- In the context of AVP, Colombia aims to reduce deforestation to net zero, while in the context of

*REDD supports BIP plus pioneering countries, such as Early Movers, that are taking the initiative in forest conservation. The EMI is a mechanism that provides financial incentives to countries for the achievement of Early Movers and promotes sustainable development for the benefit of small-scale farmers as well as non-forest communities. These results have undergone technical assessment by the UNFCCC secretariat. Colombia has achieved REDD-plus emission reductions under REM in the Amazon biome for the periods 2013–2014 and 2015–2016.
*See <http://www.unredd.int/en/activities/government/columbia>.


---
**Page 116**
---

In NDCs, Colombia’s commitment is to focus on reducing emissions from deforestation. Moreover, ECOGIG establishes ambitious goals such as regaining a positive trade balance of the forest sector, reaching zero gross deforestation and achieving an emission reduction of 32.4 million tonnes of CO₂ equivalent by 2030. The project will be implemented in phases, necessary and transformative in nature. The use of processes offers the opportunity to support Colombia in transforming the forest sector and achieving additional REDD-plus results in the future.

## Paradigm shift potential

* **Scale:** High

16. The initiation of ECOGIG helped Colombia to streamline and coordinate REDD-plus efforts by bringing together a diverse and strong approach. This has helped and is expected to continue to support the country in reducing emissions from deforestation, forest degradation, the enhancement of carbon stocks, sustainable land management, and conservation through recording and reporting results in full compliance with UNFCCC and GCF. The project will support the implementation of AVP, which is designed to be flexible enough to undertake and allow for financial complementarity and coherence with other initiatives.

13. The reinvestment of the GCF proceeds into ECOGIG through supporting AVP will help Colombia in implementing the strategy. In addition, the establishment of a national fund strengthens linkages with the private sector and indigenous peoples, which will further help the country to achieve its goals under the NDCs and REDD-plus.

The strategic investments from GCF for implementation of AVP seek to complement existing funding sources (e.g., Fondo Verde del Amazonas) and facilitate Colombia’s ability to attract other donors and financial mechanisms to blend in the implementation of ECOGIG.

14. ECOGIG establishes ambitious actions required to reduce deforestation, forest degradation, and enhance carbon stocks through a multi-faceted approach that addresses all three pillars of forests and their role in the country's development via the following action lines: (1) socioecological management of forests and public awareness; (2) development of a forest economy and closing the forest sector gap; (3) strengthening institutional capacity, territorial planning and environmental determinants; (4) permanent monitoring and control; and (5) generating economic benefits for local communities. The project will support Colombia in achieving these results by promoting and establishing forest management in the Colombian territory through the implementation of AVP. The GCF proceeds will mainly support the implementation of ECOGIG action lines 1,2,4 and 5.

15. Despite Colombia being considered a high forest cover and low deforestation country, it is still vulnerable to deforestation due to its geographical location in close proximity with neighbouring Amazonian countries. In this sense, the GCF proceeds will complement the implementation of AVP by supporting the development of an integrated strategy that builds on AVP efforts, integrates sustainable forest management, provision of sustainable agroforestry systems, and promotes monitoring and information management, and increases capacities for territorial governance.

16. Finally, the benefits-sharing mechanism established for the implementation of AVP has been built into the project design to ensure that all stakeholders benefit from the project and will allow further implementation of the strategy by defining and implementing mechanisms aligned to performance-based incentives schemes at subnational and local scales.

## Sustainable development potential

* **Scale:** High

17. The project will create an enabling environment for sustainable development by achieving systemic change at the local, regional and national level. ECOGIG focuses on

**Page 117**

# Needs of the recipient

## High

The focus of use of proceeds will target strategic action lines of ECOGIR, complementing other projects and leveraging existing sources and thus partially covering the financial needs for implementation of the strategy.

The action lines of ECOGIR targeted by this project all have a strong focus on capacity-building at different levels (individuals, communities, organizations, NGOs, local communities and government at the subnational and national level, including research governmental institutions). There is a strong focus on strengthening the operational management of projects and programs. This project will not only provide direct support but also provide a strong platform for the entire strategy and indirectly support the implementation of all other action lines.

Although Colombia has made some advances in matters related to climate change mitigation and adaptation when compared with neighbouring countries in the Amazon basin, they require further attention and investment. The lack of adequate policies and strategies could exacerbate Colombia's vulnerability in the years to come. Colombia is home to many fragile


---
**Page 118**
---

25 The main beneficiaries of these payments include the rural population and indigenous peoples living on the territory of the Amazon region. Indeed, among the most vulnerable groups in the country are indigenous peoples living in indigenous reserves, with 63 different ethnic groups represented. Small-scale forest users, including small farmers and landholders are also expected to benefit from the project, including owners of forests and agricultural lands located in community-based forestry units, peasant organizations, farmers and producers involved in the management of forest uses. This has been duly internalized in ECOGF.

25 Country ownership
Scale: High

The project is fully aligned with national policies and NDC priorities, and the proceeds will be used to reinvest in the full implementation of ECOGF.

30 In particular, the National Designated Authority (NDA) has experienced a high level of engagement and country ownership. The NDA and the REED-plus focal point have led the design and development of the project by providing feedback at several stages of the process. The NDA has also provided technical recommendations on organizational arrangements for the programme execution and has provided technical recommendations through its internal procedures. The NDA has also reviewed the proposal in line with its internal procedures. Following its review of the proposal, the technical advisory panel recommended that the project be approved.

30 In the context of the preparation of this proposal and ECOGF, the Government of Colombia undertook a highly consultative process with relevant stakeholders, including indigenous peoples, small-scale forest users, peasant organizations, and other interested parties. The Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development (MADES), a governmental body with 27 years of experience, plays a key role in the implementation of the project.

30 Furthermore, the NDA has identified several potential partners for the selection of implementing partners in the territory at the regional and local level, such as Fondo Acción, Finca Ríos Verdes, and Fundación para la Conservación de la Naturaleza.

32 Furthermore, the project is aligned with the National Development Plan 2018-2022 (" Pact for Sustainability"). It will contribute to the following goals of the plan: cumulative reduction in deforestation; increase in forest cover; promotion of sustainable land uses and conservation incentives; increased participation of the forest economy in gross domestic product (GDP); reduction in greenhouse gas emissions; reversal of the growth trend of deforestation; zero deforestation agreements for production chains of the agriculture sector, and implementation of the National Climate Change Information System.

32 Efficiency and effectiveness
Scale: High

The REED-plus results for 2015-2016 presented to ECAF and the suggested budget both clearly highlight that the project implemented in ECOGF will be cost-effective. The project is cost-effective and in accordance with the TOF of the pilot programme. Furthermore, the payment for these results is being reinvested into a transformation of the forest sector, which will allow the achievement of additional benefits such as the promotion of sustainable enhancement of forest carbon stocks and conservation.

**Page 119**

### Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### Environmental and social safeguards

The assessment process for the AE completed an environmental and social assessment (ESA) that retrospectively reviewed the "Heart of the Amazon" project. The ESA evaluated consistency between the FAO environmental and social standards and measures that were undertaken to prevent environmental degradation, including those related to biodiversity conservation in the review period. It also analyzes policy alignment between the Cancun Safeguards and the national policies, laws, regulations, and other relevant instruments governing the Heart of the Amazon project, which began in 2015 and is expected to continue through to 2022.

The FAO procedures require a screening checklist to be applied to each project. The project screening process involves an initial review by the AE to determine if it aligns with the guidelines of project risk. Based on the specific project activities envisioned and the nature and significance of potential environmental or social impacts, the AE will apply the appropriate level of risk-based screening for the BIP project. If the Heart of the Amazon project was classified as a high-risk project, then the risks are not mitigable by appropriate management measures. The Secretariat confirms the category classification.

## The ESM also provided an environmental and social management framework (ESMF) based on the results of the ESA and the screening process. The ESMF describes the due diligence and E&S risk management measures applied to BIP project activities, consistent with the FAO and IGO guidelines. It includes a description of the mitigation measures that will be integrated into the project development cycle, and the AE notes that this framework is a "living document" to be updated during project implementation as new risks and opportunities are identified, including those related to climate change impacts and potential project beneficiaries. The ESMF includes overview of stakeholder engagement and grievance mechanisms in accordance with the principles of the Performance-Based Payment Framework (PFP).

### AIP is part of an ambitious conservation initiative covering more than 9 million hectares of the heart of the Colombian Amazon. It commenced in October 2013 as a partnership agreement between Colombia and the international community to promote low-carbon development, poverty reduction, and biodiversity conservation. The project area "cannot simply be a large protected area but must also provide additional alternatives for development and integrate with existing land uses." The AIP is described by AE as an opportunity "to move forward in a way that can benefit the country at large." The AIP partnership is supported by a multi-donor strategy with over USD 10 billion of funding. It is being implemented through a public-private partnership between national and international development partners, including by supporting protection of the climate change mitigation potential of forests and other ecosystems. The project area has been reviewed for the BIP project - the Heart of the Amazonian Project - aims to protect a forest economy that contributes to the reduction of deforestation via a community-based forestry program. It also includes the establishment of a sustainable timber supply chain, enabling the enabling environment for the Government of Colombia's forest and carbon monitoring system; and strengthening the capacity of local communities to manage their forests.

### The Heart of the Amazon project was the first pilot of the AIP and therefore contributed to the design of the AIP. It is implemented by the Colombian government in partnership with smallholders, peasants' associations and indigenous peoples of the Amazon region are the intended beneficiaries of this project.

### The EIA draws on multiple resources to evaluate consistency between the AE's safeguard policies and the measures that were undertaken on the ground to address risks and impacts identified during the ESA. It covers the period from 2013 through 2015 and 2016. It analyzes the alignment between the PLRs and the Cancon Safeguards as well as how they were interpreted at the local/country context; and it analyzes the consistency of the AE's ESS with the reviewed project.

### The ESA demonstrated that the Heart of the Amazon project was implemented within a legal and institutional framework that is consistent with Colombia's forest law, and in accordance with the AE's ESS. The Secretariat notes that projects will need to analyze the potential they offer for interest in contributing to equal opportunities. The AE has stated in the funding proposal and the ESMP that it will continue to monitor the ESS against the AE's ESS.

The AE notes that the project will contribute to reducing deforestation by reducing deforestation and sustainably managing forests. ## Gender policy

The AIF has submitted a gender assessment and gender action plan in compliance with the criteria set by GCF.

### In the gender assessment the AIF provided information on the existence of an enabling environment to address gender issues and women's empowerment in Colombia. Colombia is a country where women have been historically excluded from decision-making against Women and through its constitution and different laws at the national level working towards contributing to the reduction of inequalities between men and women, as well as strengthening institutional structures to mainstream gender and provide support in designing policies in the creation, management and monitoring of policies, plans and programmes at all levels. Colombia has also ratified several international agreements that recognize the importance of incorporating gender issues into climate change management to prevent existing inequalities from being exacerbated by climate change. The assessment highlights the different roles, needs and abilities of men and women in the design and implementation of policies, plans and programmes, as well as the need for a participatory approach recognizing the critical role played by women in agricultural production, food security and the resilience of rural communities.

### While efforts to ensure that policies exist, the assessment also demonstrates that women are disadvantaged in many aspects in Colombia and shows female-headed households and women living in poverty are particularly vulnerable. Women's vulnerability is shown in high levels of literacy, for poverty and lack of access to land and credit. The situation is graver in rural areas where land ownership is mostly concentrated on small plots, and where female ownership is lower than male. Furthermore, there are inequalities in access to infrastructure services such as water supply and sanitation. In addition, women-headed households, patriarchal norms dictate the division of labour between women and men, with women engaging in reproductive work, which is not recognized and does not generate much income.

### Risk of double payments

The funding proposal indicates that RENARE has been developed and available to the public in early 2020. The emission reductions submitted under the pilot programme will be registered on the registry and will also be using an interim accounting registry system and details of payments received under other sources of finance will be made available to the public. In addition, the emission reductions achieved by Colombia and submitted for registration with RENARE will be published in a national public registry of Colombia's results. RENARE is expected to serve to increase transparency, identify overlapping claims, and ensure that double payments are not made.

### Use of proceeds

Colombia will use the proceeds from BIP to support the implementation of EICBIR, which was proposed as a policy public instrument to comply with the national climate change law. The project will also contribute to the framework of the country's NDC. The project will support at least eight community-based forestry units and will be implemented in accordance with the pilot programme TOR.

### Competing for carbon ownership

The funding proposal identifies that users who intends to opt for payments as a result of actions that generate emission reductions shall be registered on the registry. It also clarifies that emission reductions or removals registered by MAIS will no longer be available for any other party to claim the results achieved. The funding proposal also states that the Government of Colombia has committed to the cancellation of GHG emission reductions or removals. It also clearly indicates that the Government of Colombia covenants that no other party will have a competing claim to the results registered on the registry, including those registered by the project in Colombia.

### Security concerns

The peace agreement may disrupt project activities due to security issues. It states that the project will adopt workplan measures to mitigate this risk.

### Disbursement plan

The project implementation will be over five years. Accordingly, it is recommended that 20% of the cost of CCF financing is carried out over the five-year period corresponding to the implementation of activities.

Therefore, it is recommended that disbursements by the GCF are made subject to a condition that the certified entity has not been involved in any fraud or expropriation in the country, or has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities.

Columbia has adjusted its proposed FREL upward by 159% compared with the historical average emissions for the period 2000–2012. This is intended to reflect the country’s ongoing assessment and situation analysis of national circumstances that affect deforestation trends, particularly the prospect of a successful outcome of negotiations to enable the REDD+ mechanism.

For a country to apply the adjustment, it needs to have consistently maintained high forest cover and low deforestation rates (be an HF/LD country).

* Does not exceed 0.15% of the carbon stock over the eligibility period in the relevant national or subnational area.
* Does not exceed 10% of the FREL/FRL.

**Qualification of Columbia as an HF/LD country**

The funding proposal states that Colombia reports a net forest area of 6,243,578 ha (9.5% of the total land area), representing 51.6% of the national territory, and in the last 18 years the average rate of deforestation has been 0.02%, which is lower than the lowest value in 2013 (-0.19%) and the highest in 2017 (0.36%). For the reference period of the Amazon biome FREL (2000–2012) this ratio was -0.23%, while for the period 2013-2018 it was +0.45%. Therefore, in the scorecard does not define any thresholds for countries to qualify as HF/LD Countries.

Columbia is considered an HF/LD country given its particular context: (i) they have consistently maintained high forest cover; and ii) they have substantially reduced deforestation rates over a large proportion of their forest areas for decades that has started to change due to the peace

#### Agreement (post-2012)
Under the current circumstances of Colombia, there has been a clear increase in deforestation, particularly in the Amazon biome after the peace accords were agreed on. The dynamics expected to be seen in a HFLD country are currently not being observed and therefore move us to the substantial area covered by the current proposal.

#### Colombia's Submission
Colombia submitted its FREL in December 2014, three years prior to the latest deadline for submission of FRELs in 2017. The submission by Colombia is in line with the existing UNFCCC guidance on adjustments made through the relevant decisions of the Conference of the Parties.

#### Size of Adjustment
The TAR confirms that the adjustment is 10% of the FREL. Colombia has aligned its FREL with the historical average emissions for the period 2000-2012. This is intended to provide a basis for the qualitative and quantitative analysis of national circumstances that affect deforestation and forest degradation, including the unfilled outcome of peace negotiations to end the armed conflict. This analysis involved both review of international evidence and quantitative analysis.

#### Carbon Stock

and accurate description of the construction of the FRL, including the data sets, approaches and their consistency.

For the FRL, All consist of estimated areas deforested in six successive two-year periods, beginning with the first year of the period under consideration and ending at either or both of the years of the two-year period under consideration were excluded from the analysis.

FRL conversion factors for above ground biomass carbon pools are estimated from forest sampling plots using allometry. The ATAR notes that although the carbon densities that determine these conversion factors (which are based on accessibility rather than statistical design), the values used consistently with the FRL, and therefore are considered reliable on the basis of the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) data and other relevant information.

The TAR found no issues related to the consistency of the time series used for the construction of the FRL/FRL.

The TAR noted of the following areas for improvement that could have an impact on the accuracy of the underestimation of emissions and/or removals during the reference period:

• The FRL treats deforestation as removal of tree cover and not as a loss in forest area. This means that areas where tree cover is detected as having fallen below the 10% threshold for forest area, but which are still classified as forest by the definition will be treated as deforested until they allow to reclassify as forest again. The TAR therefore considers that it would be useful to assess whether significant amounts of carbon could have been lost in this way, should, in principle, be possible using existing image analysis because areas of regrowth are identified.


---

- Although the value used for carbon density of the Colombian Amazon biome is consistent with international standards, it has been adjusted to be distributed according to statistical principles. The TAR notes that Colombia will use this information in 2017, to implement its national forest inventory (NFI). This will allow for more accurate and reliable estimates.
- The adjustment has been using expert judgment based on comprehensive post-conflict literature. The TAR recommends a more detailed justification of the adjustments be provided in the NFI, as it is possible given that information on the relationship between deforestation and forest degradation trends accumulates following a peace agreement.

(iii) Are the data and information provided in the technical annex complete? (Has information been provided that allows for the reconstruction of the results?)

In the TGR, Colombia has indicated that Colombia provided the necessary information that would allow for the reconstruction of the results from the implementation of the activity reducing emissions from deforestation and forest degradation. (iv) Are the data and information provided in the technical annex consistent? (Were data and methodologies applied consistently over the results time series?)

The forest monitoring system used by Colombia (National Forest and Carbon Monitoring System (SMBYC)) is a national system covering the subnational level. The SMBYC uses a semi-automatic methodology for deforestation in the Amazon biome by semi-automatically comparing satellite images of the same area taken at least during successive two-year periods in order to produce a change map. The monitoring system has been applied consistently over time and it is used to produce the results of the 2015–2016 estimates are derived using data from 721 sampling forest plots (diametric variables from trees) located throughout Colombia. The SMBYC uses a country specific allometric equation for tropical humid forest to estimate the above-ground biomass and carbon stocks, as well as a single equation to estimate the below-ground biomass and carbon stocks. The Allometric equations used in the SMBYC are based on those in the 2006 IPCC Guidelines for National Greenhouse Gas Inventories and the IPCC Good Practice Guidance for Land Use, Land-Use Change and Forestry to estimate the emissions.

SMBYC enables the estimation of AD and RFs for the deforestation of all the forests in Colombia, and specifically in the Amazon biome aggregated in one representative forest type (Tropical rainforest).


---
**Page 138**
---

(vi) Are the data and information provided in the technical annex accurate? [Does the annex neither overestimate nor underestimate emissions and/or removals?]  
Similar to the TAF, the TA/TEF identified some areas for improvement that could have an impact on the overestimation or underestimation of emissions and/or removals, including:  
• Use the NFI for statistical sampling of above-ground and below-ground biomass in forest and non-forest land use classes. Additional pools and the improvement of stratification with respect to age class and species will increase certainty in the estimation of carbon stocks in forest; and  
• Conduct more in-depth research and analysis to justify the observed trends in deforestation rates, as well as economic and social trends following a peace agreement, including the impact of climate change on which conditions change following cessation of conflict.

## Proposal name: Columbia REDD+ Results-based Payments for results period 2015-2016

The Independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP), with the support of a Land Use, Land Change and Forestry (LUULUC) expert with experience in REDD+ assessment and analysis, and the United Nations Collaborative Programme on Reducing Emissions from Deforestation and Forest Degradation (UN-REDD) roster of experts assessed the proposal "Columbia REDD+ plus results-based payments for results period 2015–2016" against criteria contained in the terms of reference for the REDD+ plus results-based payments (RBP) pilot programme.

## II. Main findings

The following paragraphs describe the main findings on those sections of the scorecard where the score assigned is lower than the maximum score for each criterion (see the annexes for further details).

(a) Consistency between the forest reference emissions level (FREL)/forest reference level and the greenhouse gas (GHG) inventory - in paragraph 49 of the


---
**Page 153**
---

### (b) I.(ii) Adjustment of the FREL for high forest cover and low deforestation (HFLD) countries

Colombia has claimed HFLD country status. The natural forest area in Colombia (as at 2018) was 52.6 per cent of the country's territory, and because the scenario does not provide a clear definition of what constitutes high forest cover, the GCF accepted Colombia's HFLD country status (different programmes/studies use different definitions). The independent TAP assessment noted that the deforestation rate in Colombia was 0.23 per cent in the FREL period (2000–2012) and 0.26 per cent for the results period. The independent TAP accepted that deforestation is not a criterion for HFLD status.

The GCF approved the use of an adjusted FREL, up to 19 September 2017, with an adjustment factor of 10 per cent over the historical average of emissions from deforestation and forest degradation (FDD) in Colombia. The adjustment was justified by the country to take into account national circumstances that could lead to increased deforestation rates in the future.

Deforestation trends in 2017 and 2018 confirmed this assumption when deforestation was at a lower level than expected, which is why the GCF approved for countries to be taken into consideration and the adjusted FREL can be accepted but with no eligibility criteria: (1) The FREL adjustment does not exceed 10 per cent (Colombia is in compliance); (2) The deforestation rate should be below 0.5 per cent of forest area, and 0.1 per cent of carbon stocks over the eligibility period in the relevant national or subnational inventory; and (3) The country must have a recent independent TAP assessment process (see the annex for further details).

### I.(ii) Accuracy of the FREL (the data and methodologies used neither overestimate nor underestimate emissions from deforestation during the reference period, so far as can be judged): In the TAR, the AT indicates areas for further work. The GCF notes that the FREL is based on a statistical model; this AT notes that the values used for land density for the Colombian Amazon biome, although derived from the national forest inventory, are not statistically adjusted according to statistical principles. However, Colombia is expecting to implement its national forest inventory over the period 2015–2017 and the data from this inventory


---
**Page 154**
---

will increase the accuracy of the carbon estimates (TAR, para. 48). Colombia has not included in the FREL pools or activities for which the country is unable to evaluate accuracies and uncertainties. The Colombian authorities have stated that the adjustments to the FREL do not overestimate or underestimate the emissions from reducing deforestation, so far as can be determined by the technical annexes (TAR, para. 48). The Technical Annexes and mentions that reference data, consisting of an independent sample, will be used in the future for the detection and monitoring of forest degradation. It is noted that this application of the adjustment for the current FREL would not apply in the case where an agreement to end the conflict is reached under the peace process. Since the definition of forest degradation includes areas with low levels of uncertainty, the guidelines include uncertainties being reduced as far as practicable, the FREL may not meet this requirement.

### II.(b)(iv) Inclusion of the most significant sources: The FREL is constructed taking into consideration only the activity reducing emissions from deforestation, which the AT considers to be the most significant source (TAR, para. 45). However, the AT notes that forest degradation is potentially a significant source of emissions and that further work on this issue will need to be undertaken to address this topic in a future submission (TAR, para. 27). The AT considers that the inclusion of forest degradation as an activity reducing emissions from deforestation for inclusion in the FREL. In its latest submission FREL (2019), not yet submitted to technical assessment, Colombia has provided information on the development and testing of methodologies for the detection and monitoring of this activity, and that the advances made are expected to reduce uncertainties from forest degradation in the FREL with low levels of uncertainty. The score is 1.

### II.(b)(v) Comparison with previous reference levels: No previous reference levels have been provided.

### II.(b)(vi) Inclusion of information on aggregate uncertainties related to its FREL: The score is 0.

#### R2(vi) Accuracy of data and information provided in the technical annexes: Prior to the submission of the FREL, Colombia submitted a second BIR. The annex to Colombia's second BIR states that the information provided by Colombia allowed the accuracy of the FREL to be assessed and that given the assumptions used, it was concluded that the results are accurate within 10% (TAR, para. 46). However, the AT notes that the technical annex includes information on the uncertainty of the results in the technical annexes in its second BIR, which states that the accuracy of the FREL is ±25%. The score for the FREL was equal to 51,599±87.0 TCO₂eq but this result was wrongly calculated. The correct value for the FREL is 51,643±70.9 TCO₂eq. This mistake was identified when calculating the results of the second BIR and has been corrected in the current submission. This is correctly reflected in the FP. The score is 1 (see the annex for further details).

#### R2(vii) Aggregate uncertainties for results: Colombia has not provided information on aggregate uncertainties related to its FREL. The score is 0.

# Colombia’s FREL (Forest Reference Emission Level) and technical report on analysis of results conducted under the UNFCCC TA process

## Results of the Technical Assessment

### 1. During the review period, five key findings were identified by the independent TAP:

- **Colombia’s FREL pilot project was not eligible for payment under the REDD+ BRP pilot programme**, as detailed in section B.1 (ii) of Annex; and
- The assessment of safeguard risk factors (annex 5 to the funding proposal) based on the Forest Reference Emission Level (FREL) methodology did not take into account that the data were sufficiently accurate and the results would have been much higher if the reference level had been revised.

### 2. During the communication with the accredited entity, both of these findings were resolved as follows:

- The total payment adjustment amount (4,690,874 CO2 eq) was reduced so as to be in compliance with the scorecard eligibility criteria, by 168,183.83 CO2 eq annually for the whole eligible period from 2015.
- The risk matrix of the FREL (Annex 5) was revised and all risks components considered in the matrix were requalified as "high", which led to a maximum score of 40 per cent, and this resulted in a reduction of the payment amount. The total payment adjustment amount under the reversal risks. The 21 per cent initially proposed was increased to 40 per cent; and
- As a result of these deactivations and scoring, the amount of emission reductions offered initially to GEF for payment was reduced to $5,604,024 CO2 eq and the initially requested payment was reduced to USD 183,970.

### 3. According to the analysis, the annual deforestation rate in the Colombian Amazon region considered under the REDD+ BRR pilot programme was quite low with an average of 0.6 per cent from 2015 to 2017 and a maximum score of 40 per cent. The 0.36 per cent deforestation rate was reached during 2017 and 2018 and is associated with post-conflict dynamics which had a substantial impact on the deforestation rates.

### Key drivers of deforestation in Colombia include:

- **Legal and illegal processes ongoing in the country, including infrastructure development, agricultural activities, illegal logging, and land grabbing.**
- The implementation of the National Land Use Plan (Plan de Uso del Suelo) and forest processing keep process.
- Since the signing of the peace agreement with the Revolutionary Armed Forces of Colombia the exponential increase of deforestation in productive areas for cocoa crops and the suspension of the moratorium on new concessions granted to the Colombian government to the deforestation process. Land titling is also considered as a potential trigger for an increase in land use change.

### National readiness process for REDD+ was launched in Colombia in 2009 with support from various international cooperation partners (including the Gordon and Betty Moore Foundation, Deutsche Gesellschaft für Internationale Zusammenarbeit, the Forest

Available at: <https://www.forestpartnership.org/sites/default/files/documents/2018%2629%2630_2018%2631%2632.pdf>

Available at: <https://www.unccd.int/documents/report>

Available at: <https://www.spectator.co.uk/opinion/finaliste-mot-de-la-deforestation-volume-700293- Available at: <https://www.theguardian.com/environment/searing-the-tipping-point-drivers-of-deforestation-in-colombia>


---
**Page 156**
---

Carbon Partnership Facility and the United Nations Programme on Reducing Emissions from Deforestation and Forest Degradation). As the result of this international support the Integrated Strategy for Sustainable Development of Colombia was developed in 2013 by the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development. The REDD+ National Strategy is part of the EU-CELAC agreement, which includes a commitment to develop national policies in support of the implementation of the international agreements ratified by the country. This strategy aims at addressing the complexity of drivers and causes. The legal baseline for REDD-plus was preplanned taking into account the national regulatory framework, where the scope of application and the subjects covered are defined.

ECCBDR envisages a complete reduction in deforestation by 2030, with forest management plans covering all forests in Colombia. This is achieved through a differential, sectoral and territorial approach, improving the well-being and livelihoods of local communities and society in general, while promoting the conservation of biodiversity and the ecosystem services provided to the country in relation to climate change. Specifically, according to ECCBDR, by 2030 Colombia will have:

* A positive trade balance of the forestry sector;
* Zero deforestation;
* A reduction in emissions of 32.4 MtCO2e from avoided deforestation;
* Strengthened territorial governance of ethnic groups, rural and urban communities that live in and depend on forests;
* Improvement of life of the local population in forest areas.

Within the REDD-plus planning process (2009-2017) Colombia has established a forest and carbon monitoring system, national forest information system and national forest inventory in order to ensure the quality of data for the generation of national reports on avoided deforestation. These are tools for generating the information of national reports (nationally determined contributions) that will be submitted to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change. The forest and carbon monitoring system has a measurement/monitoring, reporting and verification system to ensure standardization, and the quality of data. This system is supported by the national forest inventory established by the forest and carbon monitoring system, the national forest information system, the national forest inventory and the national registry of emissions for avoiding double counting and double payment of emission reductions generated from REDD-plus programmes and projects. It establishes clear accounting rules for the calculation of avoided deforestation.

Among the different programmes implemented in Colombia since 2009 (see funding proposals) are the Amazon Vision Programme (AVP) and REDD Early Movers project under AVP are of particular note.

To achieve the objectives of ECCBDR, the strategies of the national development plan and NDC Colombia 2030 are being implemented. The National Development Plan is a government that seeks to reduce emissions from deforestation in the Colombian Amazon. In the case of successful implementation, AVP could reduce significant amount of CO2 otherwise released into the atmosphere. This reduction would be achieved by an associated benefit or co-benefit in the form of improved smallerholder farmer livelihoods, biodiversity conservation and sustainable governance of forest. AVP remains of several ongoing projects

* Available at: https://redd.colombia.org/plan/indicadores_basico_nivel_territorio_de_inicia_web.pdf
* Defined as loss of tree cover below a certain threshold; forest regeneration is not counted.


---
**Page 157**
---

From bilateral and multilateral cooperations. These projects have made important investments in strengthening the management effectiveness of the national parks and their buffer zones, increasing the capacity and accountability of local forest users' organizations and communities, and authorities, supporting the national forest monitoring system, promoting sectoral programs for sustainable use of forest resources, and developing innovative approaches to natural resources management practices that contribute to reducing deforestation and reducing degradation.

The Heart of the Amazon project, financed by the fifth replenishment of the Global Environment Facility for the period 2015–2018, focusing on management and sustainable use of forest resources in the Heart of the Amazon region.

The Connectivity and Biodiversity Conservation in the Colombian Amazon project, financed by the sixth replenishment of the Global Environment Facility for the period 2018-2021.

The AWF REDD+ Early Movers project, funded by Germany, Norway and the United Kingdom of Great Britain and Northern Ireland, for the period 2016–2021 (for further details see: <https://www.awf.org/en/redd-plus/early-movers-project>).

The Colombia REDD+ Results-based Payments for results period 2015-2016, financed by the GCF REDD+ RBP pilot programme, for the period 2020–2025 (current progress).

The Amazon Sustainable Landscapes Programme: A regional project in formulation for the current period of the Global Environment Facility for the period 2011-2016.

The AWF REDD+ Early Movers project is the first BIP under the GCF in Colombia with the objective of reducing deforestation in the Amazon region. Its strategy considers three main areas:

Improvement of forest governance: this activity focuses on the institutional strengthening of the management of forest resources and effective land-use planning;

Sustainable sectoral planning and development: this activity is oriented to design processes with the forest infrastructure and environmental services, which have an impact on current and future land development and planning;

Sustainable use of forests: this activity aims at reducing deforestation as a direct cause of deforestation through two strategies: zero net deforestation agreements with local associations; and the promotion of instruments for forest conservation and the adoption of sustainable practices.

Environmental governance with indigenous peoples: this activity focuses on strengthening the capacity of indigenous communities to manage, conserve, and sustainably use of the forest in their territories, through strengthening their capacity for territorial governance based on their traditions, knowledge and sustainable productive practices;

Enabling conditions: this transversal activity aims to develop a set of actions that facilitate the implementation of the strategy. These include the development of Colombia's forest and carbon monitoring system and national forest inventory.

The AWF REDD+ Early Movers project was developed based on Colombia’s specific agreement with GCF, which was signed in 2015 and the corresponding BIP was signed in February 2016. This programme established BWP, which was conditional on the preparation of a report on emission reduction, verification of results, registration of the reduction in emissions from avoiding deforestation during the five-year period from 2013 to 2017 and the

# Deactivation of Part of the Emission Reductions as a Risk Management Activity

The agreement also establishes that the AVP REDD Early Movers project will participate within the GCF framework in the deactivation of part of its emission reductions only if emission reductions below the historical average of gross deforestation (R2H3 hs/year) will be compensated by an equivalent amount of emission reductions from the forests of the reference region, which represents an emission of 5.661 tCO2eq/ha.

The independent verification of the deactivation of part of its emission reductions below the historical average of gross deforestation (R2H3 hs/year) will be conducted by GCF-approved FIDTs countries to submit an adjusted FIDT if it satisfies the eligibility criteria set by GCF. Therefore, the amount of emission reductions provided by GCF is calculated based on a historical average of gross deforestation (R2H3 hs/year) and not on the actual R2H3 hs/year.

The amount of emission reductions submitted by the country to obtain the RBP is generated in the same period (2013–2017), which is covered by the AVP REDD Early Movers project and belongs to 2015 and 2016 emission reductions. The amount of emission reductions submitted for deactivation must be consistent with the GCF scorecard and eligibility criteria as well as the timing of the reversal of the reversion (the final amount should be equal or less than the initial amount).

The project will also provide a summary report on the RBP to the GCF in accordance with Section 15.4.

Regarding risk management, as part of the REDD Early Movers Agreement Colombia agreed on a mechanism to address the risks of leakage outside the reference area, non-permanence and double counting. The amount of emission reductions that are allocated for each unit of emission reductions paid for by the AVP REDD Early Movers project another verified project or other sources (e.g., voluntary carbon credits) is also registered in the country contribution are also registered by the country in the national registry to avoid double counting.

It was agreed that the project will report every year during the implementation period of the project, following verification.

Transparency in monitoring the emission reductions generated and remunerated has been ensured through a series of activities through the GCF website and other publics. In particular, an interim registry was established where all the reports on emission reductions with respect to the historical average of gross deforestation (R2H3 hs/year) for the period 2013–2017, and the official communication payments of the AVP REDD Early Movers project and related projects are published. The information provided by the project and other donors (except of voluntary market information) are also uploaded to the UNFCCC Lima REDD+ information hub.

The activities planned to be implemented with GCF proceeds will contribute to the outputs and actions of the EBCOR action lines and the pillars of the AVP umbrella program. The results expected from the implementation of the project and the AVP REDD+RBP are well demonstrated in table 7 in the funding proposal. The following activities are planned to be implemented with GCF proceeds:

- Strengthening national and local capacities for monitoring and controlling deforestation;
- Contributing to the sustainable management of forest areas and closing the agricultural frontier; and
- Strengthening the territorial governance and capacities of indigenous peoples for forest management and conservation.

A benefit-sharing mechanism built upon the experience and lessons learned from AVP. From the benefit-sharing mechanism provided in the funding proposal (figure 5)

independent TAP would like to highlight as most important the share (80 per cent: 30 per cent for indigenous peoples; 40 per cent of local community associations; 5 per cent for regional environmental authorities) and the inclusion of the indigenous population at the local level and for the inclusion of the indigenous population. In particular:

(a) Regional environmental authorities will be supported in increasing their capacities for monitoring, reporting and verification (MRV). The implementation of 17 national deforestation early warning systems will also be supported. GCF funds will support the design and implementation of a national MRV system that includes the capacity building of institutions that possess large areas of forest and need support to strengthen their capacities (feasibility study). (b) GCF proceeds will support the implementation of a community-based forestry programme targeted at designing and establishing at least eight community-based forestry units (80,000 ha). A community-based forestry unit will be considered as a suit unit management area that includes the development of sustainable forest management activities involving the commercial use of several species of trees. The project will also include the implementation of a landscape management approach that integrates forest management and agriculture systems, and provides opportunities for local communities to participate in decision-making.

### 2.3 Sustainable development potential Scale: High

The GCF proceeds will contribute to the ECOBAG and REDD+ plus implementation processes, which are expected to have a significant impact on sustainable development by achieving systemic changes at the local, regional and national level.

ECOBAG is a trans-sectoral policy instrument recognizing the sociocultural, economic and environmental values of forests and their role in sustainable rural development option that contributes to the ongoing peace-building process in Colombia.

A significant contribution to sustainable development will be made through Colombia's implementation of REDD+ plans under Safeguards Guidance with UNFCCC decision 1/CP.16. In particular, the implementation of safeguard P7 (be consistent with Parties' national sustainable development and poverty reduction strategies).

#### Environmental and territorial management: REDD+ plus initiatives support the consolidation of instruments of territorial and environmental planning foresters in the context of the ECOBAG project to ensure long-term sustainability of the forest. In order to ensure long-term sustainability, measures and actions articulate their planning with the instruments of existing environmental and territorial planning.

#### Sectoral planning: REDD+ plus actions of a sectoral nature are proposed based on environmental and territorial management instruments, as well as legislation related to the conservation and sustainable use of forests. As Colombia is implementing REDD+ plus actions, these must be articulated with national legislation that protects the rights of indigenous peoples and local communities. In order to ensure the long-term sustainability of the Policies, Actions and Measures (PAMs), it is important to consider the existing instruments for environmental and territorial planning in designing interventions in the territory. They must start from the recognition, respect and articulation with the instruments of existing environmental and territorial planning.

26. With the approach considered in the funding proposal the REDD-plus programme of Colombia will contribute as a minimum to the following indicators under the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs):

(a) SDG 1: No poverty. By 2030, ensure that all men and women, in particular the poor and the vulnerable, have equal rights to economic resources, as well as access to basic services, ownership of land, property, and other forms of assets, credit, financial services, insurance, natural resources, appropriate new technology and financial services, including microfinance.

(b) SDG 8: Decent work and economic growth. Improve progressively, through 2030, global resource efficiency in consumption and production and endeavour to decouple economic growth from environmental degradation, in accordance with the 10-year framework of programmes on sustainable consumption and production, with developed countries taking the lead.

(c) SDG 10: Reduced inequalities. Ensure equal opportunity and reduce inequalities of outcome, including by eliminating discriminatory laws, policies and practices and promoting appropriate legislation, regulations and policies for the empowerment of all women and girls; promote the social, economic and political inclusion of all, irrespective of age, sex, disability, race, ethnicity, origin, religion or any other status.

(d) SDG 12: Responsible consumption and production. Implement the 10-year framework of programmes on sustainable consumption and production, all countries taking action, with developed countries taking the lead in achieving a sustainable pattern of consumption and capabilities of developing countries.

(e) SDG 13: Climate action. Integrate climate change measures into national policies, strategies and operational plans at different levels, including by enhancing national capabilities for early warning systems and meteorological services to reduce the loss of lives and human and economic impacts.

(f) SDG 15: Life on land. By 2020, promote the implementation of sustainable management of all types of forests, halt deforestation, restore degraded forests and substantially increase afforestation and reforestation globally; enhance conservation and sustainable use of alpine ecosystems by 2030.

(g) SDG 16: Peace, justice and strong institutions. Strengthen national institutions, including through international cooperation, for building capacity at all levels in particular at the local level, to prevent violence and combat corruption and crime. Promote and enforce non-discriminatory laws and policies for sustainable development. Ensure responsive, inclusive, participatory and representative decision-making at all levels.

(h) SDG 17: Partnerships for the goals. Enhance international support to effectively implement effective and targeted capacity-building in developing countries to support national plans to implement all the sustainable development goals, including through North-South, South-South and triangular regional and international cooperation.

The independent TIP of the opinion that the potential of the GCF process to contribute to the sustainable development process of Colombia is high.


2.4 Needs of the recipient Scale: High

**Page 162**

30. The independent TAP focused on two key directions when assessing the needs of the recipient: its NDC and the international dimension of the agreement.

### 31. Goals

- **Goal 1:** To set out in the NDC a commitment to reducing national GHG emissions by 20 per cent (67 MCOeq) with respect to a baseline projected for 2030 (335 MCOeq) unilaterally. This target could be increased to 30 per cent if international support is provided, and the country would commit to reducing its GHG emissions by at least 48 per cent (169 MCOeq) for the period 2017–2030, which corresponds to the historical average of the period 1990–2010 plus 10 per cent. This target would be achieved through a combination of measures.
- **Goal 2:** For deforestation an overall budget of at 22 per cent from the NDC target was assigned. Owing to the complexity and diversity of the causes of deforestation, it is not possible to attribute all deforestation activities directly to deforestation but rather as a result of road construction and indirect causes derived from socioeconomic dynamics such as agricultural expansion, urbanization, and infrastructure development. Therefore, the reduction in deforestation activities attributed to deforestation are distributed among the productive sectors (energy and mining, agriculture, transport, household, industry and environment), which are co-responsible in deforestation. The NDC sets out a commitment for each of these productive sectors that these sectors should develop actions to contribute to the reduction of deforestation and its associated emissions.

31. The agriculture, forestry and other land use sector is included in Colombia's NDC as the largest source of GHG emissions in the country due to land-use change from forest lands to pastures and croplands. The NDC sets out a commitment for each productive sector to implement activities to reduce emissions from forest loss by reducing existing pressure on these ecosystems, including through the implementation of sustainable management practices among indigenous peoples. The potential mitigation benefits associated with the activities implemented using GCF provided support will be maximized if additional gathering field data from sustainable forest management activities to address this.

31. It is important to note that with regard to the agriculture, forestry and other land use sector the NDC sets out a commitment by the national government to achieve the goal of reducing emissions by 2030 include:

- Implementation of 554,000 ha of intensive silvopastoral systems;
- Increase in the productivity of pastures and implementation of sustainable management of the cattle herd;
- Rehabilitation of 50,000 ha of pastures in the Orinoquia region;
- Establishment of 1.2 million ha in forest plantations and fruit crops with a value chain approach;
- Ecological restoration of 1 million ha;
- Deforestation avoided in the Colombian Amazon;
- Promotion of better practices for the fertilization of rice in 147,000 ha of crops.

31. As mentioned above, implementation of these actions by the national government will imply greater international cooperation, whose resources will be used to achieve zero net deforestation at the national level and reduce emissions by 5.2 million tonnes CO2eq.

According to the funding proposal, the post-conflict situation has had important impacts in increasing deforestation in the Amazon region, in part due to the lack of economic alternatives for rural populations, which have led to an increase in illegal logging activities and the establishment of extensive pastureslands for new ranching as an alternative to improve livelihoods. This lack of opportunities and the presence of illegal economies based on illegal mining, land-grabbing and cultivating illegal crops create a scenario that generates social

# Green Climate Fund

## Country ownership

### Status: High

The REDD+ BIP roll programme is a part of EICGBI, which is consistent with the National Climate Change Policy, the National Policy for the Internal Management of Biodiversity and Forests, the National Strategy for the Sustainable Use of Forest Resources System of Projects (CONPES 368), the Policy of Sustainable Soil Management, the National Restoration Plan and the National Forest Law.

EICGBI, to which this project contributes, is part of a broader vision of the country for the peace process and sustainable agrarian development. One of the 2030 goals of this strategy is reduction in deforestation and forest degradation by 50% compared with 1990 levels.

During the EICGBI development process, Colombia received technical and financial assistance from a number of international cooperation initiatives. These provided support in enabling the country to develop its national REDD+ strategy, including its implementation processes, design and implementation of GHG monitoring systems and other instruments, development of methodologies for the calculation of carbon stocks and emissions from deforestation projects, formulation and implementation of projects aimed at reducing deforestation in the Amazon region and other territories relevant to achieving goals. Key initiatives implemented under EICGBI are:

(a) The United Nations Programme on Reducing Emissions from Deforestation and Forest Degradation (UN-REDD) programme implemented by FAO in close coordination with the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development and national partners;

(b) The Forest Carbon Partnership Facility of the World Bank implemented by Fondo Acción; and

(c) Protection of Forests and the Climate REED+ programme implemented by Deutsche Gesellschaft für Internationale Zusammenarbeit.

Despite the progress made by the AVP Early Movers project addressing deforestation in the Amazon region, the results achieved so far have not led to a reduction of AVP forest management activities still not having had the public budget allocation expected to develop a real transformation at the local level. Given this situation and the goals of EICGBI and the national strategy for reducing deforestation, it is necessary to complement ongoing efforts by considering a forest economy based on the goods and services from the Amazon region.

Despite AVP not being supported by the public budget, the independent TAP is of the opinion that political ownership from the government is well demonstrated in the programme.

2.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

**Score: Medium**

Taking into consideration that in 2017 and 2018 Colombia lost control of the deforestation process mainly as a consequence of the peace agreement, the independent TAP, in its assessment of the performance of the FREL during the period 2013-2017 and implementation of the GCF proceeds, evaluated the emission reduction generation and payment trends for the whole engine period (2013–2017) of the FREL. The results obtained are presented in this section.

compensated in the next period (see the independent TAP conditions in para. 4b below) through deactivation of the same amount of ERFs.

The independent TAP considers that it is not relevant to rely on the high efficiency of REDD Early Movers or GCF project implementation results under the current national circumstances established after the peace agreement, that is, a vacuum of governmentality accepted by the international community. The independent TAP believes that this will be anticipated once local conditions stabilize. The independent TAP considers that GCF proceeds planned to be implemented with GCF proceed are correctly targeted, particularly at increasing the technical capacity and institutional framework for REDD+.

The independent TAP is of the opinion that the entire REDD plus approach, benefit-sharing concept and target areas for the use of GCF proceeds, decentralization of the capacity-building process, and the vertical coordination of the decentralization process could be considered as a way of risk reduction and regulating control of deforestation.


### III. Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

Taking into consideration the great need and urgency of the country to revert deforestation, the independent TAP recommends that the project should start in 2017 which, in the opinion of the independent TAP is adequately evidenced by the country's efforts in the decentralization of monitoring and early warning systems and strengthening vertical coordination of the decentralization process. The independent TAP also recommends the funding proposal for approval to the Board, with the following conditions:

(a) to ensure that the project does not lead to an increase in deforestation during the implementation period of the project, in such a manner that at least reverses the emission surplus which was generated in the period covering years 2013 to 2017 inclusive (i.e. 3.114 Mt CO2e).

(b) report in the APBs to be submitted to the Fund on the actions taken and progress by the country towards realizing its ambition to reduce emissions from deforestation in accordance with roman (a) above, including how the actions are reflected in the corresponding national registry.
