| Does the total volume of achieved results indicated in the proposal match the results indicated in the biennial update report (BUR) during the periods 11 December 2013 to 31 December 2016? | Yes | The United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) report on the technical analysis of the technical annex to the BUR (TATR) (2019) does not provide an assessment of the whole period (11 December 2013 to 31 December 2016). The TATR confirms that the total volume of emission reductions for the period 2015–2016 is 31,478,933.5 tCO₂e (sum of emission reduction for 2015, equal to 14,441,499.6 tCO₂e and emission reduction for 2016, equal to 17,037,433.9 tCO₂e). |
| Is the volume of achieved results offered to the pilot programme equal to or greater than the total volume of achieved results indicated in the BUR during the periods result? | Yes | It is less than the total volume for 2015 and 2016, since emission reductions have already been paid for by the Amazon Programme. The total volume of emission reduction for 2015 (4,387,955 tCO₂e) and 2016 (3,904,968 tCO₂e in 2016), have been deactivated in 2015 (4,441,499.6 tCO₂e) and 2016 (7,037,433.9 tCO₂e). The total volume of emission reductions already remunerated correspond to 7,990,235 tCO₂e in 2015 and 3,808,000 tCO₂e in 2016. #### Criteria

- **Score**: 1

**Page 168**
---

The Second National Communication. However, they recognized and considered a useful development in the FREL according to which Colombia would submit its GHG inventory for the First National Communication; thus making the inventories consistent with the FREL (in terms of the reference year and the scope of emissions).

The conclusion was based on a comparison carried out by the assessment team (paragraph 3.1 of the TAE) between the historical deforestation data used in the FREL and the GHG inventory submitted to the UNFCCC. Colombia had the most recent GHG inventory submitted by Colombia to the UNFCCC (2nd NCI). In this case, the difference was considered as a "proxy" for the FREL estimate. A large difference was found between the two estimates.

The assessment team (AT) did not consider this further because Colombia explained that a new and improved GHG inventory would be available through the first RER. The AT also noted that the FREL is based on estimates that are fully consistent with the FREL (before adjustment). This includes the use of historical deforestation data, land-use change factors and activity data.

The REDD plus expert of the independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP) has verified the GHG inventory data in Colombia's first RER against the FREL, and the score I was assigned to this question, instead of zero, because it was confirmed that the historical deforestation data used for the FREL is provided as the RER regarding data, methodologies, and definitions consistent with those used by the FREL.

Colombia claimed its status as HFLD (high forest low deforestation) country. Before adjustment, Colombia's deforestation rate was 0.1% per year. The scorecard does not provide any particular threshold for the definition of what constitutes a high forest and low deforestation country. Colombia is a high forest country (existing different programmes/strategies used different methodologies to assess deforestation). The TAP's "TAE" report assessed the adjusted FREL of Colombia.

Regarding low deforestation, the country's deforestation rate that is not so high as to be considered a HFLD country is not provided in the Scorecard. In Colombia, the annual average deforestation


---
**Page 169**
---

rate was 0.23% in the period of the FREL (2000-2012) and for the result period it is 0.26%. The independent TAP accepted that deforestation was in the historical average.

According to the GCF REDD+ RBP Pilot Programme, HFLD countries are allowed to use an adjusted (up to 10%) FREL. The FREL of Colombia assessed by the TAP was 58.4 kg CO2e/ha/year (TAP paragraph 23). This is lower than the historical average of emissions from deforestation for the reference period (2000-2012) and the adjusted FREL would be 64.2 kg CO2e/ha/year under circumstances that could lead to an increased deforestation in the near future due to the economic situation. The analysis conducted by TAP for the years 2017 and 2018 confirmed this assumption when the deforestation was doubled.

The GCF REDD+ RBP pilot programme allows to take into consideration the HFLD status of countries and accepts an adjusted FREL but with two eligibility criteria:
- adjustment of FREL doesn't exceed 10% of FREL and Colombia is in compliance with this requirement from GCF;
- adjustment of FREL doesn't exceed 8.13% of carbon stock over reference period for relevant subnational area.

Colombia's FREL is at subnational level. FREL eligibility period of Colombia corresponds to the period 2000-2012.

The FREL submitted by Colombia was not in compliance with the second eligibility criteria (carbon stocks). In particular, FREL states that the FREL over area and time period is 58.4 kg CO2e/ha/year. The carbon stock per hectare is 56.4 tCO2 eq/ha (TAP paragraph 24). Based on these figures the carbon stock over reference period for relevant subnational area would be 31.7 tCO2 eq/ha and the adjusted FREL should be of 8.13% equals 22,629,121 tCO2eq. ### Implementation of tracking tools that can track each safeguard and elements regarding the operationalization of the Safeguards Information System, which allows for a more transparent and accountable implementation of the safeguards and to inform results in reducing deforestation

This safeguard is addressed by Colombia through the integration of four elements: (i) transparency and access to information; (ii) accountability and transparency and access to information that ensures that REDD+ plus information is transparent, accessible, understandable for all stakeholders involved, and easy to understand for local communities. Another element is the tracking software that allows for the monitoring of the implementation by all stakeholders on the REDD+ plus initiatives, including information on how this address the safeguard. The fourth element is the national forest governance structure which was developed according to the existing forest governance provided by the national forest council board and the environmental policies of the regional and local forest boards.

The Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development is responsible for legally defining the instruments for ecosystems planning and management in the environmental system. This system comprises 33 regional and 4 urban forest reserves, which are managed by the National Forest Service. These institutions are responsible for the implementation of the national forest monitoring system and the REDD+ plus initiatives under the national forest management regime and, consequently, the implementation of REDD+ plus initiatives in Colombia. The Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development government defined the instruments for ecosystems planning and management in the environmental system through discussion spaces on national forest reserves and zoning of protected areas

In order to respect this safeguard, Colombia has accomplished the implementation of the Safeguards Information System and the Safeguards Information System. Several activities have been implemented during the period 2013-2015: training events concerning REDD+ plus actions, expert meetings and webinars, and training events aimed at indigenous and Afro-Colombian organizations in the country


---
**Page 179**
---

Columbia has defined four elements to fully address this safeguard: (1) national consultation mechanisms such as free, prior and informed consent established in accordance with international obligations, national circumstances and laws; and involving ethnic communities must guarantee the rights of communities to effectively participate in decision-making processes; (2) respect for traditional knowledge to ensure that policies and programs are consistent with the cultural values and practices of indigenous peoples when activities reduce deforestation are implemented; (3) benefits distribution and benefit sharing mechanisms that provide a fair, transparent and equitable distribution of the benefits generated by REDD-plus activities to reduce deforestation and forest degradation in order to recognize and respect the communities' rights and their territory.

These four elements are recognized as 28 legal documents (policies laws and instruments) adopted by Colombia in accordance with its obligations regarding the rights of indigenous peoples and local communities, regarding prior consultation. These include: Law No. 169 of 1994, which establishes the National System for the Protection of Indigenous Peoples; the International Labour Organization Indigenous and Tribal Peoples Convention (No. 169), the Constitution of Colombia "Articles 208 and 209", the United Nations Declaration on the Rights of Indigenous Peoples, adopted by Law 21 of 1991, Political Constitution of Colombia "Article 345", the National System for the Protection of Indigenous Peoples, the United Nations Declaration on the Rights of Indigenous Peoples, adopted in 2007; and the National System for the Protection of Indigenous Peoples, adopted through Decree 2948 of 2001.

As for the other safeguards, Columbia included actions to demonstrate that this safeguard is fully addressed by the implementation of existing tools that offer four mechanisms for the protection of the knowledge and rights of indigenous peoples and local communities. These are: (i) a National Framework that includes strategies to ensure the participation of local communities and indigenous authorities in the design and implementation of actions.

Columbia provided information on how it addresses this safeguard and stated that these elements are fully addressed by the national framework for the participation of stakeholders. This is implemented through the deployment of mechanisms of participation in territories, in particular those of indigenous


---
**Page 180**
---

peoples and members of local communities, respecting the national legislation and international agreements signed by Colombia

National legislation (Law 1713 of 2015) regulates citizen participation as a constitutional right and establishes mechanisms for public administration entities to guarantee citizens' participation in decisions that may affect their rights.

Colombia has in place a national system of citizen participation, led by the National Council of Citizens Participation (Consejo Nacional de Participación Ciudadana), which ensures the participatory means for agreement and deliberation at the departmental, municipal, district and local level. Colombia also promotes citizen participation through, for example, the implementation of tools that track participation through the National Environmental Forum, regional workshops and participation, and a participatory consultation process in the Amazon region, including 13 workshops, dialogue forums and meetings of the Amazon Regional Board.

Colombia recognizes the benefits of environmental services to communities provided by natural forests and their role in maintaining water resources, biodiversity. To guarantee this, legal and regulatory provisions are aligned with ecosystem services and the conservation of natural forests and biological diversity, ensuring that the actions referred to in paragraph 14 (a) do not lead to the conversion or degradation of natural forests into agricultural land or the conversion of natural forests, but are instead used to incentivize the protection and sustainable management of natural forests and their ecosystem services, and to enhance other social and environmental benefits.

In addition, several other policy instruments exist to promote the conservation and sustainable use of natural forests and associated ecosystems services, such as the National Policy for Integrated Management of Biodiversity (2015), the National Forest Strategy (2016) and the Climate Change Policy (2017). Forest Sector Planning, regulated by Law 2013 of 1974, Law 99 of 1993 and Decree 1391 of 1998, a manual of compensation for loss of forest cover.

## Actions to address the risks of reversals

### Colombia

Colombia provided information on how this safeguard is addressed and respected in its national legislation related to biodiversity conservation planning. REIO plus sector actions are implemented based on environmental and territorial planning instruments, as well as forest and biodiversity conservation.

Colombia identified the existing territorial planning instruments at the national level that are considered the most appropriate instrument to address the risks of reversals. This law establishes responsibilities for the identification of land categories (rural, urban, protected areas) and their use, including environmental licenses. Urban and construction licenses (sectoral actions) are aligned with these land categories in order to ensure long-term conservation and proper management of ecosystems, reducing the


---
**Page 182**
---

risks of reversal. In order to respect this safeguard, Colombia has accomplished the implementation of tracking tools and the operationalization of the National Forest Information System (SINIFOR) as part of the REDD+ programme. Colombia proposes to deactivate 60 per cent of emission reductions available to GFZ as an interim mechanism to manage risks of reversals.

The proposal includes considerations regarding actions to reduce displacement of emissions. The national early warning system monitors the risk of emissions displacement. A number of initiatives have been implemented in this regard, including the development and implementation of a national early warning system of deforestation articulated with community monitoring systems, as well as the strengthening of forest management procedures for loss and degradation and prevent expansion to other areas.

Colombia also has in place an environmental information system that has several sub-systems that address the environmental information needs of the country. The National Forest Information System (SINIFOR) and its components are regulated by Decree 1277 of 2013 and Decree 1655 of 2017, which addresses the management of forest resources, the national early warning system and its information systems, the national forest information system and the environmental information system.

| Colombia provided information in the funding proposal regarding the use of the proceeds to support the implementation of the REDD+ strategy in ways consistent with the objectives of GCF (as indicated in Document 2). GCF will provide technical assistance and capacity building for activities that contribute to the implementation of the REDD+ strategy within the framework of the national REDD+ strategy, as well as other strategies and policies. The strategies of the national development plan and the achievement of the NSG goals. In this context the national government has prioritized for this


---
**Page 183**
---

project the realization of activities that contribute to promoting a forest economy and strengthening indigenous territories governance in the region. The actions will be included in the national development plan (NDEP) and low-carbon development plans.

The areas of action indicated in the national development plan (NDEP 2015-2030) for the implementation of the GCF include strategies and economic instruments for making the productive sectors more sustainable, halting deforestation and forest degradation, and promoting the conservation activities in the areas of research and the public sector, all of which are in line with the objectives of the GCF.

The proceeds from the REDD-plus BIP pilot programmes will be used to support the national, regional and local implementation of the Colombia Amazon Forest Fund (Fondo Bosque Amazónico de Colombia) and the empowerment of local authorities, smallholders, farmer associations and indigenous peoples of the Amazon region. The activities that are expected as a result of the proceeds from the GCF project include: the reduction of deforestation and forest degradation; fire control; the sustainable management of the forests; territorial governance and representation of the indigenous peoples for forest management and conservation.

Consequently, the following percentages will be allocated to the REDD-plus BIP pilot programme: 80 per cent for local implementation (20 per cent for indigenous peoples; 60 per cent for local community associations; 5 per cent for civil society organizations; 10 per cent for national institutions); 10 per cent for national implementation (1 per cent for the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development; 9 per cent for other public institutions); and 10 per cent for the project technical unit and project management.

Information has been provided on how the proceeds will be used in a manner that contributes to promoting forest economy and strengthening indigenous territories governance. In this sense, Colombia noted that investments of the programme will support the design of a national strategy for REDD-plus and the implementation thereof; attracting other international cooperation or private sector funds and providing funding for future communities interested in establishing


---

community-based forestry units. In addition, GCF funds will support the establishment of long-term monitoring systems following an adaptive management approach to ensure that the activities are in line with the development on the natural resources of the managed forests and the surrounding natural areas. One of the main objectives is to identify and promote innovative approaches that contribute to the creation of a forest economy as an instrument to improve the livelihoods of local communities, while at the same time contributing to the long-term sustainability of the REDD+ activities.

In the description for this section, there is sufficient information regarding how projects will be designed and implemented in order to adopt financing strategies to ensure the financial, social and environmental sustainability of the forest economy. # Response from the accredited entity to the independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment (FP134)

## Proposal name:
Columbia REDD+ Results-based Payments for results period: 2015–2016

## Accredited entity:
Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO)

## Country/region:
Colombia

## Project/programme size:
Small

### Impact potential
FAO takes note of the assessment.
As stated in the FP, the benefits from the proposed RBP project will be distributed taking into account the lessons learned by the government of Colombia during the development of the REDD Strategy called “Integrated Strategy Deforestation Control and Forest Management” (EDCGF). The EDCGF is a trans-sectoral policy that recognizes the social, economic and environmental importance of forest ecosystems as well as their potential as a development option in the ongoing peace building process in Colombia.
As described in the FP, Colombia will use the funds generated by high impact in terms of climate change mitigation, adaptation and thus achieve their commitment under the Paris Agreement.

### Paradigm shift potential
FAO welcomes ITAP “High” assessment to this criterion.

### Sustainable development potential
FAO welcomes ITAP “High” assessment to this criterion.
FAO, in particular, values the recognition by EDCGF of the trans-sectoral importance of forest ecosystems as well as their potential as a development option in the ongoing peace building process in Colombia.
FAO appreciates the acknowledgement that the benefit-sharing system to be put in place for the reinvestment of the proceeds is built in accordance with the EDCGF and the AVP. This will facilitate the sustainable contribution to the sustainable development process of the most vulnerable population.

### Needs of the recipient

---

FAO welcomes ITAP "High" assessment to this criterion.

### Country ownership

FAO welcomes ITAP "High" assessment to this criterion.

During the formulation of this proposal, FAO could appreciate the continued high level of country ownership and commitment from Colombia. The NDA (Nationally Designated Authority) led the development of this Funding Proposal and provided essential feedback in defining the institutional arrangements for the project execution.

### Efficiency and effectiveness

FAO takes note of the assessment

Although the initial period of the post-conflict has been challenging in ensuring full protection of the natural heritage and necessary reduction of the pressures on ecosystems, the country is now fully committed to avoid deforestation. Colombia is promoting policies and projects and establishing a framework for sustainable land use that will help reduce forest exploitation and degradation. The use of proceeds from the RBP will significantly contribute to these government efforts to address the underlying drivers of the deforestation process.

### Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

FAO thanks the ITAP for its review of the proposal and confirms it can comply with the condition.

# Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND | PAGE 2 OF 60

### 1. Introduction

The following document introduces the Gender Assessment and Action Plan for the REDD+ Results-Based Payment (RBP) project, which is proposed by the government of Brazil to the Green Climate Fund (GCF). The Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO) will implement the project in close collaboration and coordination with the Ministry of Environment and Social Development of Mato Grosso.

The project will contribute to reduce deforestation and forest degradation in the Amazon biome by strengthening the capacity for sustainable management of natural forests. It is aligned with the five pillars of the Amazon Vision Program (AVP). The project seeks to promote sustainable rural development, ensuring the full participation of women and men in decision-making, indigenous governance of indigenous peoples and supporting enabling environmental conditions, focusing mainly on the implementation of the National Forest Monitoring System (SINIFOR) in Spanish), as the main decision-making tool for processes at the local level.

The main beneficiaries of this project are small producers (peasants) and indigenous people. To protect these communities from deforestation and forest degradation, the project will promote management based in community schemes that offer sustainable production alternatives to those communities while generating local capacities for the management, control, and monitoring of natural forests.

In line with the recommendations of the Green Climate Fund Gender Policy, the project will promote gender equality through the empowerment of women through incorporating the gender approach throughout the project cycle. The Green Climate Fund (GCF) recognizes the importance of incorporating a gender-sensitive approach in all its projects. This is reflected in the GCF’s Gender Policy and in the Gender Assessment with its respective action plan as part of the project proposals. The Fund's Gender policy aims to ensure that the needs, interests, and concerns of women are taken into account when the differentiated impacts of climate change on men and women, and the increased vulnerability of women to climate change are considered.

The aim of the Gender Assessment is to assess and identify gender-related issues relevant to the project and subsequently strengthen gender-sensitive actions within the project. In this sense, the present document provides a summary of the findings from the assessment as well as the international and national normative framework that promotes the protection and empowerment of women in Brazil. The document also includes an analysis of the existing gender strategies and in the process of construction of EICDGS regarding the period of results (2015-2030) and the gender strategy for the project, as well as a description of the gender impact of project activities. According to the gender assessment, the Gender Action Plan seeks to integrate gender equality into all aspects of the project cycle.

The project is aligned with the Gender Policy of the Green Climate Fund, the Gender policy of FAO and the Gender recommendations of the UN REDD Programme, and the environmental and social safeguards of the GCF. The document also ensures coherence of the project with the objectives of the Gender Policy of the Green Climate Fund.

# Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## Dynamics and gender inequalities in Colombia

### 2.1. Gender inequality index

By 2017, the Human Development Index (HDI) was 0.747, ranking the country in the high-human development category, placing it 89th out of 189 countries. The HDI value increased between 1990 and 2015 from 0.562 to 0.747, reflecting an average annual growth of approximately 3.5% in the HDI.

The Gender Inequality Index (GII) is a composite index that measures gender inequalities across three dimensions: health, education, and economic participation and opportunity. The GII for Colombia was 0.987. It is important to highlight that the human development index is lower in women than in men; however, the GII is higher in men than in women, even though women have on average more years of secondary education.

## Table 1: Gender Development Index (GDI)

| GENDER DEVELOPMENT INDEX | HUMAN DEVELOPMENT INDEX (HDI) | LIFE EXPECTANCY AT BIRTH | YEARS OF EDUCATION | YEARS OF SCHOOLING | MEAN YEARS OF SCHOOLING | ESTIMATED GROSS DOMESTIC PRODUCT PER CAPITA (PPI) |
|--------------------------|------------------------------|-------------------------|--------------------|--------------------|------------------------|------------------------------------------------|
| VALUE 2017               | MALE                          | FEMALE                  | MALE               | FEMALE             | MALE                   | FEMALE                 |
| 0.987                    | 0.747                        | 0.782                   | 71.0              | 64.9              | 14.3                   | 15.6                   |

Source: http://hdr.undp.org/en/composite/GDI

According to UNDP, the Gender Inequality Index is an indicator that measures gender inequalities across three dimensions: health, education, and economic participation and opportunity. The GII is calculated by the proportion of parliamentary seats held by women and the proportion of adult women in the labor market as measured by the female labor force participation rate. The greater the gap between men's and women's participation in these areas, the greater the gender inequality index; the greater the gender gap and the more lost it human development.

The Gender Inequality Index for 2017 was 0.383. Regarding the inequality index, it is important to point out that there are significant differences between men and women regarding secondary education: there is an important gap regarding to the rate of participation in the labor force, which is marginally higher in men than in women. The data is presented in the following table.

# Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## Demography and population

By 2018, the estimated total population is 48,258,494 according to DANE's National Population and Housing Census (DANE 2018). The Amazonian Biome accounts for three of the ten departments in Colombia. Of the total number of people registered, 51.2% are women (22,593,924) and 48.8% are men (23,664,570), with a sex ratio of 95.5%. The population density is highest in urban areas at 117.1 percent, followed by 15.8 percent in rural areas and 7.1 percent in populated centers.

Regarding age groups, 22.6% of the population is between 0-14 years; the majority is between 14-64 years with a percentage of 68.2%, and 9.1% corresponds to people aged 65 years and over.

The departments with the highest percentage of young population (between 15 and 29 years old) are Amazonas, Vaupés, Guainía, Caquetá, and Guaviare. The departments with the lowest percentage of young population are Meta, Vichada, Casanare, and Cundinamarca.

The departments that make up the Amazon Biome comprise the departments of Amazonas, Vaupés, Guainía, and Guaviare. For the other departments in this region, the population data by department can be found in the following tables. In the departments that make up the Amazon Biome, the population is distributed as follows:

## Ethnic group

According to DANE data for 2018, the populations that recognize themselves as indigenous is estimated at 150,637, which represents 4.4% of the total national population. Compared to the 2005 census, this figure shows an increase in population increased by 36.5 percent. Fifty-one percent are men and 49.9 percent are women.

According to the National Agricultural Census (2014), women account for 48.3 percent of the 5,126 agricultural workers, while men represent 51.7%. Women of African descent, Palenqueras and Raizales, and 3 percent of the 102 indigenous peoples.

## 2.A Head of Households

The percentage of female heads of households increased between 2016 and 2018, from 34.8% to 36.9%, especially in the municipal capitals, the proportion increased to 35.1%. In the populated and dispersed rural areas, it rose to 25.1%. The figure by gender shows that the Colombian population is more likely to be headed by a male (57%) than by a female head of household, rising from 39.7% in 2016 to 42.2% in 2018 (DANE, Encuesta Nacional de Calidad de Vida 2018).

According to DANE (2018), Males are the household-head for 62.9 percent of households with an indigenous -head. Female head 37.1 percent of indigenous households (see graph 1).

### 2.B. Poverty

#### Multidimensional poverty

According to DANE*, by 2018, the percentage of people in multidimensional poverty in Colombia was 15%. The incidence of poverty in the urban areas of the country was 13.8%, where Guajira and Vichada have the highest percentages of poverty (PM) with 46.9% and 35.7% respectively. For persons living in rural areas, the incidence of poverty is 20%. The departments with the lowest incidence of poverty are Norte de Santander, Cundinamarca, and Boyacá where Vaupés, and again Guajira and Vichada present the highest incidence of poverty with 78.6%, 59.4% respectively.

As noted above, Guaviare, Vichada and Vaupés are the departments with the highest incidence of multidimensional poverty. In these departments, which are located in the most vulnerable areas of Colombia's Amazon biome (Amazonas, Putumayo, Guainía, Caquetá, Meta and Cauca) the incidence of poverty is higher than in main.

### Monetary Poverty

By 2018, the percentage of people classified as poor concerning the national total was 27%, in the capitals this proportion was 24.4% and in the populated and dispersed rural centers it was 36.1%. In the departments with the highest incidence of poverty, the incidence rate for the national total, 29.5 per cent of persons belonging to a household headed by women were poor compared to 27.7 per cent of men. The difference between men and women in urban centers was 4 percentage points lower than in rural centers. In the headwaters, the incidence rate of poverty with female leadership was 30.8% while for men it was 29%. In populated and dispersed rural centers the difference between women and men was 40.5% and 34.7 respectively. Concerning extreme poverty, in urban areas the incidence rate among persons belonging to a household headed by women were extremely poor, compared with 6.5 per cent of persons belonging to households headed by men. In populated and dispersed rural centers this difference was even greater: 10.8% for women versus 3.7% for men.

Another important source for looking at the incidence of poverty by sex over time is the report “Gender, Poverty and Social Exclusion in Colombia” (2014) prepared under the framework of the Sustainable Development Goals by UNICEF, UN WOMEN, UNIFEM (2011). This report notes that for 2002, the incidence rate among women was 35.8% compared to 29.7% for men and in 2014 it increased two. In these percentages were 27.6% for men and 20.2% for a woman with a slightly greater gap of 7.4 percentage points, while the incidence rate among women was 38.5%, compared to 7.9 per cent in 2014 for men and 8.3 percent for women. In this regard, it is important to note that over time there has been a trend between men and women that continues to date.

## Labor Market

According to DANE’s Great Integrated Household Survey (GEH) for the July-September 2019 quarter, the national unemployment rate for women was 13.7% and for men 5.2%, with a gap of 8.4 percentage points in favor of men. The unemployment rate among young people aged between 15 and 24 years old was 16.1% for women compared to 9.0% for men, while the unemployment rate (of 3.1%) and women 1.4%, with a difference of 3.3 p.p.

To reveal the gender gap in employment, it is important to note that young people aged between 25 years old and 39 years of age correspond to 47.8% of unemployed people; while young men represent 42.2%.

* Boletín técnico Polémica Monetaria en Colombia 2018. Bogotá, 3 de mayo de 2019.

According to information from the National Agricultural Census (2014), women earn 25% less than men in rural areas and are more likely to work unpaid or underpaid, with greater unpaid workload since they use lech as much time for men for domestic tasks, as well as in child care. Women spend an average of 38 hours per week on agricultural activities compared to 30 hours spent by men (Botello and Guerrero, 2017; cited by Carrasco A et al., 2018).

### 2.8. Land ownership and tenure

According to information from the National Agricultural Census (2014), the majority of producers are women (76.2 percent) compared to 69.3 percent of men, who own plots of less than two hectares and occupy 9.5 percent of rural area, while men represent 69.3 percent and their Agricultural Production Units have a greater number of workers.

Land ownership is mostly concentrated in small plots, where female ownership is lower than male ownership (28% compared to 40%). This fact identifies that women producers are the ones who employ the most female labor, especially in territorialized communities, and that their care work is not considered an integral part of the rural economy.

The shared responsibility of men and women in the decision-making of the Agricultural Production Units (36%) is greater than for non-indigenous rural population (22.12%), while among the non-ethnic rural population (8.45%). Similarly, shared decisions are of great importance in production decisions in the collective property of 34%.

*Boletín técnico Gran Encuesta Integrada de Hogares (GEH) fuerza laboral y educación 2017

2.9 Health

By 2018, 93.4% of the people are affiliated to the General System of Social Security in Health for the total of the municipal head offices of the country. The lowest levels of affiliation were found in the Central District (75.6%) and the highest level was in the North District (98.1%).

According to DANE's Gender statistics for 2011, 90.6% of women were affiliated to the health social security system; while the proportion for men was 87.7%. Since 2007, there has been an increase in the percentage of affiliation.

## Legal and administrative frameworks protecting women and promoting gender equality

### 3.1 International protocols and frameworks ratified by Colombia in support of gender equality, women's empowerment and Human Rights

The promotion of equality, social inclusion, and human rights has been recognized through different international instruments such as the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) that have had the foundation for promoting gender equality and women’s empowerment such as the Convention on the Rights of the Child (CRC), the International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights, the right to rural women to participate in development plans, access to credit services, and agrarian reform policies. Colombia ratified these international instruments in 1985.

The Declaration and Platform for Action signed during the World Conference in Women (1995) and incorporated into the Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action (BPFA), which was adopted by the UN General Assembly in 2000, is a document that in order to achieve gender equality it is necessary to have specific activities aimed at the empowerment of women. The BPFA also states that governments should ensure that women continue to be represented in all decision-making processes and that they are not discriminated against by gender laws, practices, attitudes and stereotypes; low levels of education, lack of access to public services and because they are disproportionately affected by poverty (UN Women).

Similar to the situation in Latin America and the Caribbean in 2014 points to the need for States to make significant efforts to change the conditions of inequality (FAO, 2017, quoted by Gómez, A., et al. 2018)

The Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities (CRPD) also is an important instrument for the promotion of gender equality specifically; Objective 5 “seeks to achieve gender equity and

<https://www.unwomen.org/es/what-we-do/leadership-and-political-participation>

- Unlock women's access to productive resources, including land, financial services, and information and communication technology; to promote gender equality.
- Improve women's access to education, information and communications technology; to promote gender equality.

# Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## Instrument
### United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC)
- **Signatory**: 1992
- **Ratification status**: 1994
- **Relevant aspects**:
  - Colombia approved by Law 154 of 1994. In 2005, Colombia approved Resolution 3876 of 2005.
  - At the Conference of the Parties called for in Article 11(2) of the UNFCCC, explicit recognition is given to the differential impact of climate change to certain groups by virtue of their gender and other characteristics. The COP also recognized the importance of promoting women's participation in decision-making processes related to ecosystem management in relation to climate change.
  - Parties are urged to take into account aspects such as gender equality and the active participation of local communities.

### Vienna Declaration and Programme of Action (World Conference on Human Rights)
- **Signatory**: 1993
- **Ratification status**: 1995
- **Relevant aspects**:
  - The declaration recognizes children and indigenous peoples by name, as well as women.
  - It also recognizes the Special Rapporteur on the situation of human rights in Africa.

### Inter-American Convention on the Prevention (Punishment and Eradication) of Violence against Women (Convention of Belém do Pará)
- **Signatory**: 1994
- **Ratification status**: 1996
- **Relevant aspects**:
  - For the first time, it proposes mechanisms for the protection and promotion of women's rights.
  - It recognizes that women are essential to the fight against violence.

### World Summit for Social Development
- **Signatory**: 1995
- **Ratification status**: -
- **Relevant aspects**:
  - Points out that women participation in decision-making processes is crucial, and that women and men are essential for social development.
  - It emphasizes the need to promote gender equality between women and men in all areas of life.

### Optional Protocol to the Convention on the Rights of the Child on the Involvement of Children in armed conflict
- **Signatory**: 2005
- **Ratification status**: -
- **Relevant aspects**:
  - It points out that women's participation is crucial for promoting gender equality between women and men.
  - It emphasizes the need to promote gender equality between women and men.

### International Convention for the Protection of the Person from Enforced Disappearance
- **Signatory**: 2006
- **Ratification status**: 2010
- **Relevant aspects**:
  - Ratified by Law 1418 of 2010.
  
### Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities
- **Signatory**: 2006
- **Ratification status**: 2009
- **Relevant aspects**:
  - Approved by Law 1346 of 2009.

## 3.2 Key national frameworks, policies and laws for gender equality and women’s empowerment

At the national level, Colombia's constitution includes the principles of gender equality and non-discrimination through the following articles:

- **Article 5:** The State recognizes, without any discrimination whatsoever, the primacy of the family as a social institution and as the basic institution of society.
- **Article 13.2:** All individuals are born free and equal before the law; shall receive equal protection and treatment from the authorities, and shall enjoy the same rights, freedoms, and opportunities in all areas of public life, without distinction on account of sex, race, color, origin, language, religion, political opinion, or philosophy.
- **Article 43.1:** Women and men have equal rights and opportunities. Women cannot be subjected to any form of discrimination by reason of their sex; they shall enjoy the same rights as men in all areas of public life, including access to education, employment, delivery, women shall benefit from the special assistance and protection of the State and shall receive support for their economic independence, particularly those who are unemployed or abandoned. The State shall specially support head of household.

---

**Source Prepared by the authors with information from https://www.indicators.un.org/**

In general terms, these instruments are aimed at tackling the following problems: persistent and increasing poverty; lack of access to education for girls and women; lack of employment opportunities and training; unequal and inadequate access to health services; violence against women; effects of armed conflict on women's rights; gender-based discrimination in the exercise of political power; and access to resources; disparity between men and women in the exercise of power and decision-making processes. The instruments also address the need for states to collect information about women and inequality in their access to and participation in information systems and the media, as well as the need for measures on management and environmental protection that prevent discrimination and violation of girls' rights.


---
**Page 206**
---

There are also different laws at the national level that contribute to the promotion of gender equality and the empowerment of women. Women’s rights are recognized in the national normative framework through the following laws:

- Law 581 of 2000, which regulates the adequate and effective participation of women in the political sphere and establishes the responsibilities of public entities and agencies of public power.
- Law 731 of 2002, which aims to improve the quality of life of rural women by prioritizing their rights and promoting gender equality between men and women. These norms favor the recognition of rural women and guarantee their participation in decision-making processes.
- Law 1297 of 2008 establishes norms for the sensitization, prevention and sanctions related to violence against women.
- Constitutional Court Order No. 552 of 2008 which aims to protect the fundamental rights of women and girls from all forms of discrimination and violence, including those that may be gender impact given armed conflict and forced displacement. Prevention of the disappearance of women and girls in situations of armed conflict is also included.
- Law 1346 of 2009 establishes policies for the attention, assistance and integral reparation offered to victims of sexual violence committed during the armed conflict, as well as measures aimed at economic recovery of the conflict, and measures for the restitution of land to women.
- Law 1685 of 2013 which aims to promote gender equality in rural areas. This law establishes mechanisms to eradicate any form of discrimination against rural women.
- Decree No. 479 of 2010 on Rural Development This decree creates the Directorate of Rural Women within the Vice-Ministry of Rural Development.

The Vision for Rural Development of the Ministry of Agriculture and Rural Development (MADER) has the Directive Law No. 581 of 2000 and Law No. 731 of 2002. Within this Directive, the Rural Women Programme was designed and implemented to strengthen and improve the conditions of rural women in Colombia. This programme aims at improving rural women living in poverty and vulnerability, and promoting their equity. This program has led to progress in addressing the needs, situation, and demands of rural women in Colombia (Camaroto, A et al., 2018).

# Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND | PAGE 19 OF 60

Since 2012 the country has had a National Policy on Gender Equity, as an instrument to guide the mainstreaming of gender analysis in the country's public policy, which prioritizes the following strategies:

- Promotion of women’s participation in positions of power and decision-making.
- Promotion and strengthening of access to property and production resources. This strategy considers the access of rural and ethnic women to comprehensive technical assistance for the development of their productive activities and the achievement of food security.
- Reduction of risk factors and vulnerability of women about their home and environment. It proposes the implementation of actions to recognize and value the role of rural women and their contribution in the management of natural resources.
- Mobilization and communication for cultural transformation: raising awareness of women’s rights, promoting gender equality and the empowerment of women.
- Institutional strengthening.

Concerning gender considerations related to Climate Change according to Colombia's Second National Communication (SNC) to UNFCCC (2015), the country recognizes the importance of incorporating the gender perspective in climate change management to prevent existing inequalities from being exacerbated. The SNC also highlights the need for a differentiated vision of climate change with a territorial and sectoral approach, incorporating the different dimensions of vulnerability and resilience at national level, as well as at local level. This is recognized, as well as the recognition of the important role that women have in agricultural production and their contribution to the conservation of natural resources.

The SNC also highlights the need for institutional strengthening aspects to promote sustainable and inclusive development (UDICAM, et al., 2018).

This is reflected in the formulation of recommendations to incorporate the gender perspective into climate change management. The Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development has a gender mainstreaming approach, since 2017 the Climate Change Directorate of the Ministry of the Environment and Sustainable Development (MADS) has been implementing the National Guidelines to Promote and Strengthen the Gender Approach in climate change management with the aim of promoting the participation of women in decision-making processes. The MADS and the PNCC-SISLIMA has the Regional Climate Change Nodes for inter-institutional coordination, which promote the role of instances at the territorial level for the incorporation of the gender perspective.

This effect is reflected in the formulation of recommendations to incorporate the gender perspective into climate change management. The Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development has a gender mainstreaming approach, since 2017 the Climate Change Directorate of the Ministry of the Environment and Sustainable Development (MADS) has been implementing the National Guidelines to Promote and Strengthen the Gender Approach in climate change management with the aim of promoting the participation of women in decision-making processes and climate change management. (UDICAM, et al., 2018; p. 29).

The Ministry of Mines and Energy included a differential and gender focus in the adoption of the National Universal System for Climate Change Adaptation (SINACCA) and in the design of the actions it hopes to implement. DEANM, for its part, is moving forward with a methodological proposal that includes the incorporation of the gender perspective into the formulation of the National University; progress is being made in the construction of a system of indicators for the evaluation of the effectiveness of the actions and policies implemented.

It is worth noting that with the support of DEAN UNDP and the Great Alliance against Deforestation, the Convention "Gender and Climate Change Considerations in the Framework of the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change - UNFCCC" was held at the end of 2018 with the aim of facilitating a space for dialogue between the different actors involved in the country's process of addressing the gender perspective in national climate change reports. The event was attended by MADS, PNCC-SISLIMA, DEANM, and representatives from the World Bank, IDB, GEF Fund and WOFI Colombia. One of the main conclusions of this space is that, although the country

## 3.3 Gender equality policies, plans and programs

### Green Climate Fund Gender Policy

The Green Climate Fund stands out for being one of the first to incorporate gender considerations from the beginning of its operations. Gender equality is fundamental to the objectives and principles of the Fund. The Gender Equality Policy (2017) sets out how the Fund will ensure that the Fund's governance instrument and related policies have been adopted since its inception.

In 2015, the board adopted the Gender Policy and Action Plan (Green Climate Fund, 2017), which read as follows:

- Both women and men contribute significantly to combating climate change. Shifting the paradigm towards low-emission, climate-resilient development pathways, which is the Fund's objective, requires a gender-sensitive approach that recognizes the different roles of women and men. A gender-sensitive approach is therefore part of a paradigm shift.
- Climate change exacerbates existing inequalities between women and men, and existing gender inequalities are likely to be exacerbated by climate change.
- Gender equality is fundamental to achieving greater and sustainable results in the face of climate change. Women's increased vulnerability to climate change is due to gender norms and discriminatory practices that limit their access to resources and opportunities for better livelihood opportunities; less access to land and other productive assets; fewer decision-making powers, especially at the household level.
- By adopting a gender-sensitive approach, the Green Climate Fund contributes efficiently to gender equality by achieving greater and sustainable results in the face of climate change. The Gender Equality Policy sets out how the Fund will ensure that women's voices are heard in all the Fund's projects to promote the equal participation of men and women in the design, development, implementation, monitoring, evaluation, and reporting phases of projects; improve the effectiveness of climate change mitigation and adaptation interventions and ensure that benefits from these interventions reach both women and men equally. The Gender Equality Policy also ensures that gender considerations are integrated into all Fund operations to make climate change interventions to be more effective and efficient.

21

**Page 210**

# Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND | PAGE 22 OF 60

With the support of UN Women, the Fund developed a guide that includes a series of methodological tools aimed at incorporating a gender perspective in project design. Gender mainstreaming should shape the entire project cycle from project development to monitoring and evaluation. The guide is based on the following four pillars: 1) Gender Mainstreaming, 2) Gender Action Plan, and 4) Gender Sensitive Monitoring and Evaluation. These considerations were consolidated into this document.

## FAO Gender Equality Policy

Some United Nations Agencies have incorporated a gender perspective into their internal policies from time to time in order to promote gender equality, as well as the need to incorporate it into monitoring and evaluation processes and projects.

In this regard, FAO's Gender Equality Policy (2013) achieving food security goals in agriculture and rural development is based on the recognition of women’s roles in agricultural production and the empowerment of women as central elements in sustainable agricultural production and rural development, for the reduction of hunger and poverty. 

The view that women who close to the land, the soil, the agro-ecosystems and other aspects of rural life contributes to generating significant advances for sustainable development. In this regard, FAO considers that gender equality is a key element in achieving food security goals in agriculture and rural development. While recognizing that increasing food security for all by meeting levels of nutrition, optimizing agricultural productivity and the management of natural resources, and reducing poverty are important objectives in their own right; in this sense, it is recognized that agricultural production in the family environment depends on the challenges faced by women.

The main objectives of the policy are the following (FAO, 2013):

- Women participate as much as men in decision-making in rural institutions and the formulation of laws, policies, and programs.
- Women have equal access to and control over decent jobs and income, land and other production aspects
- Women have equal access to the goods and services needed for agricultural development and markets
- Through poverty is reduced by 20 percent through improved technologies, services, and infrastructure.
- Agricultural productivity for women's projects and gender equality increases to 30% of total agricultural support.

In this way, FAO incorporates gender aspects into all facets of its work, seeking to make gender mainstreaming a cross-cutting theme in its normative work and its programs and project at the level of regions, subregions, and countries. 

In this regard, FAO will ensure the incorporation of gender analysis in the formulation, implementation, monitoring, evaluation, and reporting processes of all projects. It is important that needs and priorities of rural women are taken into account in all the processes that FAO conducts. We do not believe that the lack of attention to the specific needs and priorities of women and discrimination against them, will contribute to creating internal structures and systems that promote gender inequality and discrimination.

In countries where the gender gap is very large, FAO will promote programs and projects specifically targeting women.

As fundamentally to FAO's mandate, gender equality is integrated into the Strategic Framework as a cross-cutting theme to support the achievement of strategic program (SPs) at two levels: within and across SPs. FAO’s work will systematically review and consider the needs, priorities, and experiences of women in all its programs and projects. This will be done through gender analysis that is used to ensure that women and men benefit equally from interventions and that gender inequality is

not perpetuated or exacerbated. To integrate gender equality and women's empowerment into planning and implementation processes at various levels to ensure gender-equitable results and to encourage gender-sensitive monitoring and reporting systems, a task team consists of gender experts from the Gender Unit, Gender Focal Points in all Headquarters and in Decentralized Offices (DOs) have been established. The GFF Network at headquarters and regional and country offices plays a central role in ensuring that FAO's gender equality policy is implemented.

At the project level, to incorporate a gender perspective (such as the process of assessing the application of gender equality and men of any planned action), two actions will be developed before project implementation:

1. Baseline survey of contact at the initial stage of project implementation, including gender-related issues and integration into the monitoring framework/outputs as indicators of outputs, outcomes and impact to be tracked through the evaluation and monitoring system.
2. Establish a participatory and gender-sensitive monitoring framework to assess progress and monitor the achievement of gender equality objectives, and make suggestions for changing or adding new activities, with self-evaluation by participants.

At the same time, FAO has the FAO Technical Guide to support the governance of responsible and gender-equitable land tenure. This guide incorporates a gender perspective to achieve more equitable access to land resources in all countries, regions, and institutions. It focuses on the concept of equity and how land administration considers the different needs and interests of women and men. The guide provides examples of policies, strategies, and actions that contribute to improving gender equality in processes and activities related to land tenure.

Gender equity must be incorporated into the governance of land tenure to ensure that the interests and needs of all people—women and men—are considered; and that transparent, consultative and participatory processes are used. Involvement of stakeholders and institutions (FAO, 2013: p. 5).

## UN-REDD Programme Gender Approach

For its part, the UN-REDD Programme made up of UNDP, FAO and UN Environment has been promoting gender equality since 2008 and has therefore taken steps to systematically and actively promote a gender perspective in all its activities. The UN-REDD Programme Secretariat has developed different instruments and tools for the inclusion of the gender perspective in its actions.

The UN-REDD Programme Gender approach has three main objectives (UN-REDD, 2017):

1. Ensure that the principles of gender equality and women's empowerment guide the formulation, prioritization, and implementation of UN-REDD policies and measures and are fully integrated into them.
2. Comply with gender equality provisions set out in international REDD+ agreements including their respective national strategies.
3. Promote gender mainstreaming in planning, implementation, monitoring, and knowledge management within the UN-REDD Programme with a gender perspective and contribute to QDS 5 on gender equality.

To promote a gender approach, the UN-REDD Programme integrates gender equality and women's empowerment as cross-cutting elements in all UN-REDD thematic areas and its


---
**Page 212**
---

# Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND | PAGE 24 OF 60

technical assistance at national, regional and global levels. In this way, it supports countries to systematically include gender-sensitive activities in the design, implementation, monitoring, and assessment of their REDD+ measures so that all stakeholders can participate and benefit equally and meaningfully.

It is important to mention that, through the Programme, a guidance guide was designed to integrate gender perspectives into the National REDD+ Strategy. This guide provides advice for including gender considerations throughout the different phases of REDD+ processes in countries. This guide makes the following recommendations for mainstreaming gender (UN-REDD, 2013):

* Both women and men are key agents of change, whose unique but often differentiated knowledge and skills can contribute to more effective forest management.
* Given the diverse social, economic, and cultural inequalities and legal models, particular attention should be paid to the needs of vulnerable groups (e.g., women in rural areas, the poor, youth and the disabled) are constrained in their ability to fully participate in and benefit from REDD+ activities.
* Women, men, and youth often respond differently to REDD+ incentives and policies; they have different access to resources and decision-making power within households, communities (including local governments and traditional authorities) and unequal access to and control over resources.

Activities that do not take gender differentiation into account not only limit the scope and effectiveness of REDD+ activities but also fail to address the needs of women and men. Strengthening livelihood activities and indigenous knowledge of the forest can contribute to activities such as monitoring, soil management, and forest restoration functions, which can contribute positively to the achievement of REDD+ goals.

The UN-REDD Programme has also designed different instruments to guarantee an effective incorporation of the gender perspective in its program. It is important to mention the following:

* Checklist for gender-sensitive workshops
* Sample questions for a National REDD+ Strategy
* Step-by-step efforts to make progress towards gender equality
* UN-REDD Methodological Report on Gender (2017)
* Indicators for monitoring and reporting on gender equality

The UN-REDD Programme at the global level has several advances in the field of gender to guide the preparation of National Strategies for Reducing Emissions from Deforestation and Forest Degradation (REDD+) and associated activities, which are being implemented by United Nations Agencies that lead it.

The work has focused on the need to establish broader and more informed channels of participation, addressing structural inequalities in the plans of REDD+ related to gender inequality such as the unequal distribution of access to and control over resources, and substantive participation (Cancino, A. et al., 2014).

In Colombia, the UN-REDD National Programme developed two consultancies specifically in gender issues: first, to identify the most relevant aspects of gender and REDD+, secondly, to have a baseline in accordance with the recommendations of Cancino et al. (2014). The consultancies were carried out by experts and representatives of organizations of indigenous, Afro-Colombian and peasant communities for 2015-2017.

This work was carried out taking into account the guidelines of the United Nations and the UN-REDD Programme, and was developed within the framework of the process led by the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development since 2009, in conjunction with the program

### 4. Gender Issues around Forests and National REDD+ Strategy

To provide guidance for the development of the REDD+ strategy, it is recommended to consider the document prepared by the UN-REDD Programme (2018) "Forests and Gender in preparation for REDD+" as input for a gender-sensitive approach in the Integrated Strategy for Control of Deforestation and Forest Management.

Some of the main considerations regarding this issue are presented in this section of the document.

First, it is important to note that the effects of climate change are different for men and women who live in both rural and urban environments according to their roles and the relationships they weave with natural resources.

According to OFEPAF quoted by (Camacho, A. et al., 2018), women experience greater vulnerability to the negative effects of climate change, related to less control over and access to resources, increased workload in domestic tasks, and that it generates other specific effects that are experienced, for example, after natural disasters.

*Women are important mobilizers of change that enhance the capacity to adapt to and mitigate climate change. They have a greater influence on decisions about land use, consumption patterns and the provision for their homes.* (Camacho, A. et al., 2018, p. 13.)

Thus, it is important to recognize the roles that men and women play in forest management, since gender differences are reflected in the different ways they interact with forests and natural resources, in aspects related to water supply, food preparation, fuel and care work, which are traditionally considered feminine activities and which are made more demanding by climate change.

In this sense, it is important to highlight women's practices and actions within climate change mitigation. Women are conceived as transitioning subjects towards more sustainable and resilient life modes.

The need to incorporate the gender perspective in REDD+ actions and processes is highlighted, as well as the importance of considering the different roles that men and women play and the potential risks for women that could have a negative impact on them, such as the increase in workload due to climate change. It is also important to consider the role played by women and their families, the increase in inequalities in participation and access to information and training opportunities.

In this sense, gender mainstreaming is fundamental for the construction and implementation of national REDD+ strategies. According to Sietzewski, 2012, quoted by Camacho, A. et al., 2018, the international community has recognized that:

Specific knowledge, skills, and experiences of women as main forest users, and the different roles, rights, responsibilities of men and women, as well as their access to the resources they manage.

Accurate information on the causes and agents of deforestation and forest degradation. Mitigation strategies for reducing the effects of climate change through conservation and restoration of forests.

- Improved the equitable distribution of benefits.
- Consistency with the human rights-based approach to development.

In general, women are one of the poorest actors in rural areas. Despite existing efforts towards gender equality, rural women have the lowest levels of schooling and the highest illiteracy rates (FAO, 2017). They also face greater difficulties in accessing land ownership and credit, as well as being employed in rural areas, owning land and being a substantive part of decision-making processes. Women are often excluded from access to resources.

In environmental management and rural development in Colombia there are large gender inequalities related to land ownership, access to resources by women belonging to ethnic and peasant communities, and the division of labor between men and women. This is due to the fact that reproductive work as part of rural activities (LÓPEZ, 2017, quoted by Canachao, A., et al., 2018). It is important to note that the impact of deforestation and degradation on land use and permanent displacement has differential effects on men and women. The losses and lopes tend to be borne more heavily by rural women because they are responsible for the maintenance of the function of generating income in their homes, having to assume not only reproductive but also productive activities, increasing their workload.

It is important that organizations and groups at the community level are fundamental to the structuring of forest management based on environmental governance. It is therefore essential to promote women's participation in REDD+ actions and decision-making spaces.

To understand the differentiated impacts by gender on deforestation and degradation of LÓPEZ forests, it is necessary to consider the division of labor between men and women into account the following areas of analysis for the integration of the gender perspective in the Strategy:

1. Roles in forest management and the sexual division of labor
2. Gender inequalities related to access to resources
3. Substantive participation in decision-making on land use

### 1. Roles in forest management and the sexual division of labor

According to Canachao, et al., 2018, regarding the sexual division of labor, rural communities have been built around a patriarchal ideology where women usually do reproductive work while men do productive work. Women's work is considered less important because it does not generate monetary income, but rather serves for the sustenance and care of the household.

Rural women have fundamental ancestral knowledge for the sustainable management of forests related to the use of forest resources (e.g., collection of medicinal plants, fruits, nuts, honey, for security; collection and processing of fruits and seeds, horticulture, management of the orchard), health (use of medicinal plants in traditional medicine) and crafts (production and use of forest resources for the elaboration of handicrafts and other objects of daily use); selection of wood for construction.

Men, on the other hand, generally work in commercial activities such as cattle ranching, timber extraction, agro-industrial crops and transportation that generate monetary resources, but have a greater impact on the environment and are considered as causes and agents of deforestation and forest degradation.

The use of forest resources is widely marked by the sexual division of labor. Generally, women are responsible for collecting and processing forest products to meet their own needs or for their commercialization and market access, which generates inequalities and imbalances in the distribution of benefits.

# Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND | PAGE 27 OF 60

Due to their nature, women have a greater vocation towards the care of nature, their families and their communities; this potential can be used towards better conservation and sustainable use practices. However, there is little recognition of the work women do in natural resource management within these frameworks.

However, in climate change scenarios as mentioned above, women experience different difficulties in carrying out their traditional tasks and must devote more time to carrying them out, generating a greater workload.

It is important to note that rural women experience different types of discrimination linked to their age group, marital status, educational level, and family burdens. In this context, the role of women in forest management, for example, implies an overload of women's unpaid work, the depoliticization of the female agency in environmental issues, and the underrepresentation of women in decision-making processes.

Many roles are related in different and diverse ways to the forest across access, knowledge, control, forms of management and management. Although the role of women in forest management is recognized as a key element for sustainable development, it is not always reflected in forest use and management, agroecology, food security, defense of territories, transmission of knowledge, among others (Camacho, A., et al., 2015).

Some fundamental considerations to take into account for gender mainstreaming from the perspective of the sexual division of labor in the Strategy are as follows:

- Equitable distribution of the benefits
- Capacity building and Community Forestry Work
- Recognition and remuneration of differentiated work with the management of natural and forest resources
- Visualization of the role of women in food security and livelihoods

## 2. Gender Equality in access to control over natural resources

Inequality in decision-making processes, the subordination of the feminine role within the patriarchal structure, and the lack of recognition of women's contributions are obstacles in access and control of natural resources between men and women, which also extends to mechanisms for acquiring and mitigating climate change impacts (Camacho, A., et al., 2015).

Although women have the capacity to manage land and water resources, as well as material, monetary and immaterial resources, and have ancestral knowledge about the management of natural resources, they are not always recognized in decision-making processes. Women generally do not have the power to manage the revenues from their work or the capacity to control the benefits derived from these resources.

As mentioned above, women represent 48.3 percent of the census rural population, of which 36.4 percent are agricultural producers. However, concerning the labor market, rural women earn less than men and have fewer opportunities for education and training in order to increase their control over resources. It is important to note that access to land ownership, inheritance practices, the lack of credit, and access and credit become important barriers in achieving gender equality in the rural sector.

Gender inequality in land ownership and tenure is accentuated in contexts of armed conflict where armed groups appropriate women's property for military purposes (e.g., use of forests for timber extraction or minerals; illicit use of crops) to exercise territorial control. In this sense, it is important to recognize how the gender dimension affects participation in local environmental schemes

### Initiatives in political and community participation, where women are in conditions of greater vulnerability to gender violence.

In this context, it is important to bear in mind Law 160 of 1994, which establishes ways of promoting access for men who are not landowners and who have a traditional occupation as farmers or agricultural workers, and women who derive their income from agricultural activity, under the current Rural Development Statute of 2007, which provides for joint titles in the case of spouses.

FAO highlights the need to know the obstacles or problems faced by rural women about land tenure and access to resources. It is necessary that the rights of rural women over land and their rights are promoted on an equal footing. The current content of land-use planning is centered on the fight against social inequality in rural areas.

In this sense, the key elements to be considered from the perspective of access and control of resources for mainstreaming the gender approach in the strategy are the following (Camacho, A., et al., 2018):

- To strengthen capacities for decision making about the use and management of territories.
- Promote women's participation in the control over the production of products and access to markets.
- Improve conditions for access to land-based on the context of rural areas.
- Consider financial and human resources destined to include gender issues in terms of local sovereignty, strengthening forms of tenure resources and strengthening of traditional forms of knowledge transmission.

## Substantive Participation in Decision Making on Land Use

In general, in projects, the construction of gender indicators is limited to quantifying data disaggregated by sex. This approach does not take into account the different roles that women and men play in rural areas and scenarios do not mean that gender considerations are being taken into account in the decision-making processes. The forms of participation adopted by women depend on the spaces they occupy in decision-making spaces, the forms they adopt, their means of negotiation and the evolution of their participation throughout the process.

The key issue is to make it possible for women's participatory indicators that make it possible to account for how women's participation is articulated with their strategic interests and practical needs.

Substantive participation is, therefore, a category of analysis that accounts for this possibility. performance and sustainability of social transformations promoted by a key agent (in this case women) in the context of rural areas and scenarios.

The substantive participation of women is a central element in all moments of diagnosis, consultation, joint work and implementation in environmental governance. In this sense, the active participation of women in decision-making processes is essential for the sustainability of discussion on how actions, measures, and policies are determined within the framework of the Strategy. The active participation of women in these processes implies that they participate effectively in their actions; this implies making effective existing policies and public instances and providing them with a voice.

In this sense, the key elements to be considered from the perspective of substantive participation for mainstreaming the gender perspective in the strategy are the following (Camacho, A., et al., 2018):

- Improve information and strengthen capacities in the decision-making processes on the Strategy.

### 5. Incorporating the gender perspective into forest conservation efforts and REDD+ action in Colombia

In 2013, the formulation process of the UN-REDD-Colombia Programme began at the request of the National Government. The Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development was the country for the implementation of REDD+. This process is based on the expectations and needs raised by various women's groups, especially women belonging to indigenous communities, Afro-descendant organizations, and peasant organizations.

It is worth mentioning the accompaniment of the Programme to the National Government in the process of preparing the National REDD+ Strategy. The Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development organized a series of dialogues with government institutions, sectors and community-based organizations on the global issues associated with climate change and the implementation of the REDD+ approach at the national level.

During these dialogues, some recommendations were made aimed at incorporating the gender perspective into all actions in the construction of the National REDD+ Strategy. These considerations, as well as the general guidelines of the UN-REDD Programme at the global level on the subject and national policies on this matter were considered for the implementation of the Programme.

The Programme aims to support the integration of the gender perspective in the preparatory actions of the National REDD+ Strategy, based on the following considerations and general guidelines. "In its implementation, the program sought to mainstream the gender approach at four levels: i) at the national level, through the identification of specific actions that would make the gender perspective visible in a comprehensive manner; ii) At the regional level, through the establishment of agreements and gender policies in intermediate articulation spaces and instances, promoting the establishment of agreements and gender policies in intermediate articulation spaces and instances, promoting the establishment of agreements and gender policies at the territorial and forest management levels; iii) At the local level, to ensure that women's participation is not only substantive but also effective at this level in the territories; iv) Strengthening the institutional framework, led by MARN, for the implementation of REDD+ in Colombia in the process of preparation and implementation of REDD+" (García A., et al. 2018, p. 77).

The UN-REDD Programme has some positive results for the incorporation of the gender approach into forest conservation efforts and REDD+ action.

In 2016, a process of dialogue was initiated with various women's groups, especially women belonging to indigenous communities, Afro-descendant organizations, peasant organizations regarding the issue of climate change and the REDD+ preparation process. These dialogues were aimed at knowing the perspective and considerations of women's organizations to include projects aimed at getting to know the perspective and considerations of women in forest conservation efforts and REDD+.

A baseline was also prepared based on the REDD+ guidance notes, which included a gender perspective (UN-REDD 2013) and a proposal for the incorporation of the gender perspective in the Strategy, including methodological considerations for its mainstreaming.
