The Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development (MADS), published in August 2017, the EICDBG to respond to national and international commitments regarding deforestation reduction.

The EICDBG is a cross-sectoral policy instrument that involves the co-responsibility of the different sectors of the Colombian State. With the purpose of halting deforestation and forest degradation, it seeks to promote sustainable development in the territories where they are located, emphasizing the strategic significance of these ecosystems for the country, for their socio-cultural, economic and environmental importance, or their potential as a development option in the framework of

# Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND | PAGE 31 OF 60

The peace building process, and for their contribution to mitigation and adaptation to climate change (MADS, IDEAL, 2017, p. 2)

The Strategy seeks to reduce deforestation and forest degradation through its management and sustainable use by local forest communities, as well as a sustainability development approach based on natural forests that contributes to the local livelihoods of rural populations contributing to local climate change resilience by promoting adaptation and mitigation strategies (MADS, IDEAL, 2017).

It proposes five strategic lines each with one measures and actions that seek to address the causes and agents of deforestation and degradation in Colombia.

These are:

* **Socio-cultural management of forests and public awareness.**
* **Development of a gender perspective in forest governance.**
* **Sectoral-segmental management of land-use planning and environmental determinants.**
* **Promotion of women's participation in decision-making processes.**
* **Generation and strengthening of legal, institutional and financial capacities**

The strategy recognizes the diversity of actors who are related to and interact with the forest and who have different interests and needs regarding its use and management.

In this way, gender equality is part of the guiding principles of the strategy that guides the measures and actions. Gender equality is not only a fundamental human right but also the necessary foundation for a peaceful and prosperous society in Colombia. Promoting gender equality is essential to access education, health care, decent work, and representation in political and economic decision-making processes. It is also important to ensure that all Colombian women benefit from policies and procedures as humanity as a whole will benefit at the same time (MADS, IDEAL 2017, p. 15).

The ECOSGF is framed within the Millennium Sustainable Development Goals. In the area of gender equality, it contributes to the achievement of Goal No. 3: Ensure equal access for women and girls to education, health care, decent work, and representation in political and economic decision-making processes (MADS, IDEAL 2017). It also contributes to the procedures and humanity as a whole will benefit at the same time.

For the conception of the ECOSGF, MADS has focused on "identifying the role of women as producers and consumers of forest products; identifying the invisibility of the participation of women, and observing the different effects of programs from a population-based approach" (Carmen, 2017).

There is also other gender-sensitive issues related to food security, sustainability of production processes through women, and better product benefits that can be enhanced through environmental management practices.

Within the lines and measures established in it, the gender focus is transversal as a guiding principle that constitutes an opportunity to establish different initiatives and activities in its implementation.

### 5.1. Development of the participation process within the framework of the process of construction and preparation of the Strategy

It is important to emphasize that the formulation of the Strategy was carried out from a regional approach allowing to integrate gender equality, and involving the key actors in the process: dialogue and participation around forest management and deforestation control (MADS, 2015).

With a view to promoting gender equality and the empowerment of women, two central approaches were followed. The first one is the promotion of the substantive participation of women in the processes and the approach by type of actor that aims to concentrate efforts so that gender considerations are recognized and applied by the different actors related to the strategy.

Concerning the substantiative participation of women, it is important to point out that during the process of construction and preparation of the Strategy, the organizations were asked to include women in their work teams. Since men usually hold leadership positions within these organizations, this was a novelty for them. In addition, efforts were made specifically for the participation of women to identify the different needs from the perspective of both women and men. This was done through the implementation of the National Forest Roundtable’s Safeguard Workshop, within the framework of the National Territory Commission; each organization had the opportunity to present its safeguard plan in a participatory manner.

Also in national scenarios such as the National REDD+ Roundtable (now the National Forest Roundtable), where women and men are equally represented with their organizations or partnership with the forest come together, the participation of men was 56.3% and of women 43.7%.

Concerning participation in the spaces developed within the framework of the UN-REDD Programme in Colombia, it is important to highlight (see table 15).

### Participation of indigenous women in the Strategy:

Within the framework of the participation process with indigenous peoples, workshops and meetings were held at national level between 2015-2017. These spaces were developed by indigenous organizations participating in the Permanent Roundtable on Indigenous Consultation in order to address the issues related to gender equality and the empowerment of women among indigenous peoples require for the capacity-building process in the REDD+ preparation process. As a result, agreements were reached with the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development that these spaces, women's organizational processes were strengthened, promoting their participation in the relevant decision-making bodies.

Thus, during 2015-2017, a total of 39 dialogue, information and capacity building workshops were held with leaders of indigenous organizations at the national level and with the support of UN-REDD. In these spaces, the importance of gender equality as an essential element in the development of the Strategy, social and environmental safeguards were addressed, and the importance of the issue of women's empowerment was highlighted. The participation of women in the organizational processes promoted in these spaces, these participation figures indicate that we are still far from having equal representation.

It is important to note that participation in the Amazon Region was the highest in terms of local attendance and national representation. This is not only due to the fact that it is the largest region in the country but also to the national government's provision of resources for this purpose, which has contributed to reducing deforestation rates. However, it is important to continue making efforts to promote the participation of indigenous women in these spaces and to achieve gender equality.

It is worth pointing out that, within the framework of the Strategy, the participation of indigenous women was active from their platforms such as secretaries, tables or departmental authorities. This shows the importance of these spaces for the representation of indigenous peoples. However, it is important to note that, about the proportion of male participation in these spaces, there are still many spaces and forums for consultation and decision-making with indigenous organizations.

In particular, the construction of the Strategy involved two main communities with indigenous peoples in which women participated: the National Indigenous Women's Platform (Pronind) and the Amazon Women's Platform. Pronind is a space that provides information and capacity-building on issues related to climate change and REDD+. In the same way, the Amazon Women's Platform is an organization of indigenous women from the Amazon Region (Higher Government) who participated in the national instances, where women participated from the regional platforms. The participation of these women was very important because they provided important inputs about the indigenous women's vision of the forest, which was taken into account in the construction of the Strategy.

The improvement of promoting women's participation within the women's family and generation councils was identified in these capacity-building forums with indigenous peoples' authorities and leaders. In this sense, the National Indigenous Women's Platform (Pronind) and the Amazon Women's Platform were spaces where the Indigenous Peoples' Gender Strategy document was produced for the Strategy. This document discusses the importance of women's participation within their communities and the consequences of climate change for indigenous women and proposes a role for women's participation in decision-making processes.

With regard to the management of natural resources, the importance of women's work in forest management was recognized, as experts and bearers of specific knowledge in various thematic areas such as food security, health, education, and social reproduction. Women play an important role in resource management, among others, and the importance of their role for the economy and social reproduction is evident because they are the main producers and people, since their food security and family care depend on them (Camacho, A., et al., 2018).

### Promote Sustainable Land Use Activities with the Aim of Preserving Biodiversity and Reducing Deforestation

Within the governance, management, and monitoring of forest commons since 2015 have been implemented actions in support of actions of the reservation's land-use related to food sovereignty and gender equality. These actions include strengthening women's organizations, promoting the use of traditional seeds in orchards, and the validation of ancestral knowledge in the formulation of cultural norms for environmental management.

It is important to highlight that within the actions of the project, the participation and empowerment of the women are essential elements. This has been achieved through the incorporation of the gender perspective into the initiatives. The program focuses on strengthening the traditional systems of orchards to recover and rescue a variety of crops as an alternative to improving their living conditions.

The participation of women in these actions contributed to the recovery of ancestral knowledge around the use of seeds, plants, and trees. In particular, with the support of the Association of Indigenous Captains of the Miriti Amazonas (ACIMA) and Tuyuca ethnic groups, the characterization of the traditional system of the orchard, the classification of the species present in the orchards, and the identification of seeds and plants for the transmission of knowledge of diets and food. This becomes an important input for their land-use instruments.

Besides, there are 11 primers elaborated by 33 women from 11 communities that are part of the Association of Indigenous Captains of the Miriti Amazonas (ACIMA) where, through participatory methodologies, they have characterized and classified the species present in the orchards. These primers that will be used by women and their communities become an important element for the conservation of biodiversity.

This allowed the communities to recognize the role of women in the use, management, and conservation of the territory, as well as to generate actions to strengthen food sovereignty that contribute to the preservation of cultural norms and ancestral knowledge.

A conversation was also held with the support of FAO and the Heart of the Amazon project, where women representatives from various organizations presented strategies and actions that women have implemented in their communities. The discussion focused on how these strategies and actions help mitigate the consequences of climate change through the recovery and rescue of traditional seeds and plants for food sovereignty and environmental processes. During the discussion, the importance of strengthening governance processes and a gender perspective was highlighted to ensure the sustainability of actions with connection to biodiversity conservation.

In compliance with FAO's Guideline 8 related to gender equity, the Heart of the Amazon Project has adopted the following measures:

- The participation of indigenous women in specific workshops to address their needs and aspirations regarding food security and productive alternatives in the orchards in Miriti Parish.
- 504 women and 568 men in project areas benefited from improved access to conservation services.
- The project supported traditional food security systems (orchards) run by women.
- Women's organizations were strengthened through training, which includes their traditional planting systems that promote environmental conservation and sustainable use of natural resources. This not only strengthens the resilience of women but also their traditions, transmitting knowledge from generation to generation and conserving the environment.

# Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND | PAGE 39 OF 60

Considering that almost half of the country’s natural forests (47%) are present in the collective territories of indigenous communities, it is necessary for environmental management and land use planning to be carried out from an intercultural perspective, but also from a gender perspective. This is because women have a significant role in the sustainable management of resources as well as the importance of the role of women in caring for nature, territory and the maintenance of traditional knowledge.

According to the Gaia Amazonas Foundation, how gender is constructed in Amazonian peoples has to do with the division of labor between men and women. In these communities, relations between men and women are expressed in horizontal terms where the tasks are carried out in a complementary way to achieve a good life in community and harmony with nature.

The role of women is related to the care of the house and family, as well as food preparation and raising children. Hence, the women are in charge of sowing and harvesting the various crops, since the orchard is considered their domain. They also take care of the animals that live on the farm; men carry out predatory tasks: they chase the land, cut down trees, burn the place where the orchard will be planted, etc., while man and woman participate jointly in the collection of wild fruits (Gaia Amazonas, 2019).

As Gaia Amazonas notes, within the traditional roles of Amazonian indigenous women, the role of the house is also important. In this sense, they are responsible for preparing meals, organizing some old practices related to the preparation of ceremonial spaces and rituals that take place in the orchard, as well as taking care of the children who accompany them during these activities.

The work of caring for, preserving and maintaining the orchard and the seeds, as well as providing food for their families is a task that women carry out throughout life. In addition to this, they also have knowledge to perform work assigned during the sacred ritual of Yurupari in the maloca.

However, it is important to highlight that women’s roles have undergone changes over time and therefore, it is necessary to take into account the contributions made by indigenous women researchers, which contribute to recover traditional knowledge and transmit this knowledge from generation to generation. This is because women are the bearers of knowledge about nature in general and the role of women in the care and management of the territory. Bearing in mind the need for a gender perspective in environmental management and land use planning, it arises that women become spokespeople for their initiatives and participate in spaces for strengthening their leadership roles.

The influence of indigenous women is permanently reflected in the daily life of the communities, since, through various activities such as the elaboration of baskets, baskets, and ceramics, they produce knowledge about nature. In this way, women transmit their histories, stories, and experiences from generation to generation. This way, the language of women is a reflection of the relationship that these women have with nature.

They are bearers of millenary knowledge about the care of their territories, work that they do through the management and transformation of the forest into spaces of abundance of food and life for all people. In this sense, women’s role in environmental management is not only for prevention and health, knowledge that brings balance, reconciliation, solidarity, strength, harmony with nature, but also for the maintenance of their languages, which are the way they have with nature since the creation of the world, their customs, and spiritual practices have been

woven and originated for the recognition of MOTHERLAND (Zabalaia, 2012: 4 cited by Garcia, E., et al., 2018, p. 51).

In this way, it is essential to generate initiatives aimed specifically at indigenous women and youth, such as capacity-building activities, intergenerational dialogues, leadership training, participation in decision-making processes, increasing access to and appropriation of appropriate technologies for the conservation and sustainable use of forests.

In fact, REDD+ policies and actions should be designed with a gender perspective. In this way, they will contribute to the protection and recovery of biodiversity in the territory and forests, as well as all REDD+ actions, consider the protection and recovery of traditional knowledge and ancestral practices; protection of species for forest use and management.

### Key Considerations in National REDD+ Social and Environmental Safeguards

Decision 1/COP.16 (COP.16, Cancun) established a set of methodological guidelines for developing national social and environmental safeguards as part of the design phase prior to REDD+ implementation, based on meeting four requirements for access to performance-based payments:

1. National Strategy or Action Plan: describes policies, actions, and measures to address the causes and agents of deforestation and reduce forest degradation. It considers ensuring that these strategies are consistent with national development plans towards the effective participation of stakeholders, ethnic communities and peasants.
2. Indicator System: establishes indicators for monitoring progress in safeguarding REDD+ implementation.
3. Safeguard Information System (SIS): to provide information on how REDD+ social and environmental safeguards will be respected and addressed in accordance with the national context.
4. Reference level on Forest Emissions to know the status of deforestation and greenhouse gas emissions.

The application of the UNFCCC REDD+ safeguards needs to be revised and interpreted to the reality of each country, considering the national and regional context, be aligned with existing national policies and actions, stakeholder groups and how activities will be implemented in the territory. As part of the commitments made by Colombia during the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC), the country has developed a National Strategy for the application of the Cancun Safeguards and in the construction and consolidation of a Safeguard Information System (SIS) to monitor progress in safeguarding REDD+ implementation. These safeguards are being respected and addressed in accordance with the national context.

The process of consideration and interpretation of safeguards by the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development (MADS), has been accompanied by the development of guidelines for capacity building with multiple actors such as institutions, indigenous communities, Afro-descendants, women, youth, among others. The MADS has also developed a guide to interpret the various actions on how to address, respect and interpret safeguards in the implementation of REDD+ projects. This guide is based on the recommendations made by the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC), the Carbon and Forest Cooperative Fund (FCP) and the UN-REDD program supported these second-level guidelines for safeguarding REDD+.

As a result, the country has a National Interpretation Chart of Environmental and Social Safeguards for REDD+ that would allow local communities to understand REDD+ safeguards simply and clearly. In this way, the interpretation chart becomes an important tool in the process; the primer becomes a useful tool for communities, institutions, and organizations to start developing their own safeguarding plans as part of the Integrated Strategy for Control of Deforestation and Forest Management (EICODF).

The seven REDD+ safeguards defined under the Climate Change Convention have been translated into 19 elements that must be applied to all REDD+: Policies, Actions and Measures

The integration of the gender perspective in safeguards calls for a genuine process of integration from which opportunities to improve the safeguard system can be considered in the framework of national instruments (Camacho A. et al., 2017). In this context, it is not only necessary to formulate gender-specific safeguards, but on the contrary, how gender considerations are implemented should be part of the safeguards intended for Colombia.

According to (Camacho, A. Et al. 2018), a series of observations are included that account for the opportunity to propose an integral proposal to integrate the gender perspective into the National Safeguard System (see table 11).

#### Summary of Information for Safeguards
- Involve women and men and safeguards in the process of preparing the summary of environmental and social impacts.
- Generate a manual of good practices in gender and intercultural issues.

FAO’s Environmental and Social Standards (ESS) were adopted in 2015. At programmatic and field level, nine HE standards were designed to help manage and improve FAO's environmental and social performance through a risk-and-basis-of approach.

In this context, the project aims at gender equality and will promote women active participation and will propose for addressing gender and women differentiated needs. Activities under the three components of the project are designed to address gender inequalities, reduce poverty, and reduce risks; strategies to promote women participation, programs to increase women empowerment, and actions to improve women’s access to resources and services through training activities, monitoring activities, territorial governance and making-decisions processes. Thus, in order to achieve objectives and goals of this Project, activities of each component include:

- **Appropriate gender-sensitive institutional arrangements:** Activities will be developed by women and men, strengthening needs and prioritization of areas of work and roles.
- **Women’s empowerment and participation:** Women will participate in decision-making processes related to deforestation reduction, forest management and indigenous governance.
- **Decision-making processes:** The project will ensure that the training needs of women to participate actively in local monitoring and making-decisions processes are addressed. This includes capacity building for women on participatory approaches and capacities for local governance.
- **Identify and implement strategies to promote active participation of women in forest management, production activities associated with forests, and land tenure.
- **Identify and support rural women face for project formulation and management.
- **Design technical assistance programs considering a women empowerment needs.
- **Monitoring and evaluation:** The project will monitor the progress made by women on implementation of conservation agreements.
- **Dissemination of information to ensure that women access to training programs, technical assistance activities and other lessons learned derived from the implementation of the project.

## 6. Conclusions and Recommendations:

In summary, Rural women in Colombia constitute 48.31% of the rural population and 30.97% of the total agricultural workers and have autonomy in the decision of production in 21.88% of the Agricultural Productive Units. This numbers reflect that despite population of women is high, their participation in decision-making processes related to forest management activities and making decisions process is low. In the context of the Amazon, patterns are the same. The lack of access to information and training opportunities for rural women may have an impact in reducing the leadership roles and spaces for women. In addition to the above, the lack of representation of women in decision-making processes also limits women’s participation in development process. In the case of ethnic communities, women are often excluded from decision making processes because they define specific roles for women and may create conditions for discrimination and psychological barriers.

The gender analysis and the gender action plan have built on the process of construction of the ECOSUR, which generated a set of gender considerations to be considered in the implementation of the project. The gender action plan has been elaborated from primary and secondary information sources. Primary information to identify women’s needs was collected through FAO’s Gender Assessment Tool (GAT) executed in different territories of the country and includes areas related to the Project such as rural development, natural resources management and land tenure. This


---
**Page 231**
---

previous work involving rural women, including ethnic communities has generated substantive information about the circumstances in which women live and the level of action that is required in the development of a gender strategy for this project. Secondary sources such as the results of the participatory research carried out by the EICGDB and the Amazon Vision Program (2017) and the Colación de la Amazonía Programa (2017) have also provided good practices for climate change adaptation, food security and gender equality in the region. The heart of the Action Plan project has documented good practices for financial climate change adaptation, food security and gender equality in the region. The document as well as seed rescue for food security. FAO has also documented the role of women in the conservation of biodiversity and the implementation of the Amazon Project of Protected Areas in the Amazon. Within the framework of the AVP, gender-mainstreaming activities are being successfully developed in the indigenous pillar.

In order to incorporate the gender perspective in the construction of the Strategy, a two-approach was adopted: (1) The EICGDB has defined specific areas of work that will be effective the participation of women in the different areas of forest governance, in accordance with the principles and objectives of the strategy. In this regard, the heart of the EICGDB include specific areas of work to be considered in the political agenda and in the implementation of the Strategy: (1) Gender equality in the definition and division of labor; (2) Gender equity in the access and control of natural resources Full and effective participation of women in decision-making processes related to land use. As a result of the implementation of these approaches, the EICGDB developed a set of recommendations for the formulation of the Strategy, which include the Vision and Goals of the strategy, which currently guide the implementation of projects. Also the heart of the EICGDB has defined specific areas of work that will be effective the specific activities to strengthen women capacities and participation in the regional and local decision-making processes.

There is a need to continue ensuring that Colombia’s REDD+ does not pose risks to the most vulnerable groups such as women, youth and the elderly and indeed actively promote their inclusion. In this regard, it is necessary to ensure that the Strategy continues to take proactive measures to ensure the integration of the gender perspective in the Strategic Plan.

Although significant progress has been made in gender mainstreaming, it continues to be addressed in isolation as something that can be achieved through specific action. In this sense, it is necessary to integrate and mainstream gender into all programs to address this approach in an integral and transversal manner to all actions.


The proposal to mainstream the gender approach focuses on making effective and inclusive participation of women a priority for the Strategy. In many instances and in the process of construction and implementation of the Strategy, the participation of women is not considered as a priority or even as a goal. It is necessary to pin efforts to guarantee the effective participation of women to reduce gender inequalities and promote their empowerment through increased decision-making recognition, capacity building and leadership beyond the idea that gender mainstreaming simply refers to the inclusion or targeting of women in projects.

* The participation of indigenous and Afro-descendant women continues to be weak in the spaces and instances of consultation, coordination, and decision-making with ethnic group organizations; so it is necessary to develop methodologies to implement appropriate mechanisms for their full participation in the management of forests in these spaces, valuing their role and knowledge in forest management, use, and conservation.

* To support the sustainable management of forests, it is necessary to promote our systems of environmental governance with a gender perspective, articulating local government policies with national policies. It is also essential to develop forest conservation initiatives based on the traditional knowledge of women in the economy of care and their role as guardians of nature. The development of territorial management plans and other instruments of territorial ordering is also essential to support the implementation of REDD+ actions. In this sense, it is necessary to promote participation and leadership capacities in decision-making instances related to REDD+ actions.

* According to the lessons learned from the gender mainstreaming process in the country's EDCGB project, it is necessary for all actors involved (government officials and decision-makers at the national, regional and local levels) to understand the dynamics of gender relations and their impact on forest governance. It is also essential to establish a gender mandatible or focal point for the implementation process of the Territorial Management Plan, with the objective aimed at strengthening women's knowledge and contribution to forest governance.

* It is necessary to consider the practical and strategic needs of women in all project activities related to REDD+ actions. This includes the need to establish a gender evaluation of gender-differentiated development impacts of the value chain. Although there are some initiatives that have been implemented, such as those aimed at long-term conservation, it is necessary to generate quantitative information and have a battery of indicators that allow for more consistent reporting of gender information.

* Although significant efforts and progress have been made in promoting gender equality and women's empowerment and to integrate a gender perspective into REDD+ actions, gender equality has not yet been achieved. Statistics show that the participation of women in decision-making processes related to rural development, where women suffer discrimination in health, education, political representation, and access to resources among others, which limits their capabilities and possibilities in their quality of life.

The project will implement specific activities such as: ensuring the participation of young women and heads of households in the implementation of environmental projects; promoting the use of participatory methodologies with gender content and recommendations for increasing women's knowledge and practices, invisibility of women's contributions to family agriculture and the household economy; lack of information on gender-based violence assistance, gender-based violence and lack of knowledge about the Information and Communication Technologies (ICTs) and innovation processes.

# Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND (PAGE 46 OF 60)

* There is a clear need to work on gender-based violence in all projects involving rural women, since the different forms of violence against women are present both in the rural and in the ethnic context and this has serious consequences for the sustainability of the processes. The project will contribute to the prevention of violence against women by raising awareness among community members about gender-based violence (GBV), according to FAO's mandate, in the project areas. This will be done through a series of activities that will be carried out during the implementation of the project. The objective will be to increase awareness regarding the GBV and promote the adoption of preventive measures by the communities, as well as to strengthen the active sensitization in the health, protection and justice sectors.

* There are different forms of violence against women, including but not limited to the existence of prostitution, violation of their own and customary practices regarding women's bodies, sexual harassment, domestic violence, forced marriage, among others. The project will address these issues through a series of activities that will be carried out in all project routes. Nevertheless, the offer of the State is obligatory throughout the national territory and it is a fundamental right that all women know about and are in position to put for it.

* The Project will support gender equality and will define specific actions to achieve this goal. Gender equality will be defined as the equal participation of men and women in all aspects of life, including but not limited to: (i) identification of forest and non-forest land uses that can be used by both men and women; (ii) identification of activities that could be carried out as part of the forest units; (iii) analysis of opportunities for women's participation in the management of forest resources through a monitoring system, market analysis and identification of market opportunities. (iv) measures to support women's participation in the management of forest resources by supporting women to identify their roles in sustainable forest management units and providing them with training on forest resource management; (v) strengthening programs for indigenous women according to the activities of the Output 3.

* For gender mainstreaming, the project will adopt a participatory approach. The Project will be adopted in the most relevant processes and activities, depending on the context and needs of each community. This will be done by identifying the specific need for development-specific actions: to strengthen women's capacities, increased self-esteem, empowerment, promotes a gender-sensitive leadership and assertive communication, among others.

* At the beginning of the project, the initial assessment on gender issues will be improved. In order to do this, the Project will carry out an analysis of the gender situation in order to make the strategy and the mainstreaming process specific to each result, specifically results 2 and 3.

* The Project will support actions (processes and technical tools) for gender mainstreaming for both communities and government entities, thus making it possible to carry out activities that are more effective. This will be done by identifying the areas that have an impact on government plans and policies or other ongoing processes.

* The Project will have a gender specialist who will address this issue and will be supported by knowledge management tools such as the Gender Action Plan (GAP) for results 2 and 3, and differentiation will be made given that the context includes farmer and indigenous women. This will allow for the development of indicators that reflect gender mainstreaming to be incorporated into outcomes 2 and 3.

* Road maps, tools and instruments that FAO Colombia has already designed and applied in other projects will be used as a reference. The Project's technical support activities to address gender issues will be visible in the project's Logical Framework and ADDT, to guarantee the monitoring of the implementation of actions over time. Finally, the


---

# Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND | PAGE (47 OF 60)

The Project will undertake an assessment in each area of intervention to analyze the context and identify the activities needed to address causes of inequity and reduce potential barriers to women's participation. The assessment will also help the Project to identify the relevant institutions that need to be involved, including women’s organizations, local government bodies, and other stakeholders. It will also define tools and methodologies to assure involvement of these institutions continuously, not only in gender mainstreaming but also in the implementation of the project activities. These mechanisms will also consider the traditional and local institutional arrangements.

It is planned to creating a working group on gender issues with representatives from the relevant institutions and organizations. The group will have as its main goal point of the team and the objective of the project, and will be responsible for defining strategies and making decisions to improve the implementation of the project according to the particular needs of women in each area of intervention. The working group will meet at least once a year, guaranteeing one of the indispensable requirements: a participatory approach and effective dialogue with the women receiving the impact of the project.

Lessons learned by FAO in the implementation of other projects in the country have also been considered:

* **The approach of gender with indigenous communities goes hand in hand with the emphasis on traditional leaderships.** The importance of these leaderships is often expressed by women understanding the mediation that there is with the major authorities.
* **Work with male farmers and/or producers is relevant to gender equity.** In the area of agricultural production, it has been observed that when male and female leaderships are considered, which facilitates the adoption of different quotas and the use of different techniques in the same plot of land, there is a greater participation for women. There is no harmonious transformation of gender roles without men involvement.
* **Project investments must reach the women of the communities directly, even when working with male farmers or producers.** This can be done through community councils. It is essential that there be a tangible and measurable benefit from the investment in each area of intervention.
* **Local technical teams must be sensitized and have a basic conceptualization of gender equity.** They should also receive training on this subject, both theoretically and technically measured. Training on this subject is part of the gender action plan.

The expected results for gender equity can be categorized as follows:

* **Instruments:** The Project will monitor sex-disaggregated indicators to identify women’s participation in each area of intervention. These include the number of country Annual reports, implementation of Deforestation Control Protocols, local Zero Tolerance protocols, and other instruments related to the monitoring and evaluation of projects and leadership of local initiatives and others related to planning and budgeting.
* **Women farmers:** The Project will implement specific activities to strengthen women’s participation in agricultural production. These include training on the use and implementation of the forest-ecosystem model promoted by the Project such as implementing agroforestry systems, promoting the adoption of sustainable land management practices, design and implementation of commercial strategies for their products, and the development of financial instruments and incentives that promote environmental sustainability, among others.

- **Indigenous women:** In order to recognize and enhance women's traditional knowledge in forest management, productive projects and sustainability, the Project will undertake activities to strengthen capacities related to the formulation and implementation of gender policies and strategies (with particular attention to the collective ethnic context) and access and participation to IPs decisions.
- **Gaps:** In order to significantly improve the effectiveness of the project, we need to identify specific gaps in the areas of intervention in order to identify the specific needs of women. Based on this assessment the project will adjust the gender plan for each area of intervention and execute activities accordingly.

# Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND | PAGE 49 OF 60

### 7. Gender Action Plan

The project design considers the incorporation of a gender perspective, emphasizing the need to adopt a perspective in the project that, through its measures and actions, take into account the diversities, differences and inequalities related to differentiated population groups, to provide attention to the specific needs of their rights.

Although the women play a fundamental role in the management of natural resources and in the development of productive activities, the results obtained from the projects implemented and activities continue to reflect important gender gaps that are accentuated under conditions of ancestral poverty. The gender action plan provides for actions aimed at promoting equality by strengthening the effective participation of women in decision-making, promoting their access to land tenure or natural resource management, and improving their representation making visible the role that women play in the use, management and conservation of the territory.

The gender action plan provides for catalytic actions for equality in land tenure or natural resource management. These actions include strengthening the capacity of women to participate effectively and strengthen the impact of women on resource governance within ethnic communities and the strengthening of their representation in decision-making processes and protocols, as well as in the improvement of women's productive units, the promotion of financial mechanisms and incentives for the sustainable management of resources account for this.
